## R: Ra–red snapper

### Ra
 
 <i>n.</i> the Sun God

>  <b>FORSTER</b> A Comparative Table of the Various Languages in the Isles of the South-Sea ... [English] The Sun ... [New-Zeeland] he ra [a-macron grave] [????] <i>(1778,Observations Made During a Voyage,284</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The traditional inventions relative to the sun (Ra[acute]) and moon (Morama[a1-acute]) are innumerable; Rona is the `man [245] in the moon,' whose lodgings on earth have been vacated previous to the decease of Mawi[a-acute]. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/244</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> Ra (Rah): The Sun <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,46</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,2/205, 4/441</i>)

### ra ra room
 
 <i>n.</i> recreation room

>  Shearers and shedhands move into the room next door - the "ra ra" room, Hugh calls it - each with their ration of beer for the night. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/92</i>)

### ra-ras
 
 <i>n.</i> [?the upper classes; ?the rugby fraternity]

>  ..as [they] pointed out, state house rugby has always been looked at with disdain by the ra-ras. <i>(Jan 15, 2006,Sunday Star Times,B9</i>)

### rabbit
 
 <i>n.</i> ?Rastafarian argot

>  The court was told that Kennedy was a `rabbit' or junior associate, of the Rastafarians and acted on the instructions of two senior members. <i>(Mar 25, 1988,Dominion Post,1</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to run and dodge like a rabbit [(rugby union)]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> He [a six foot seven inch player] was chasing the smallest member of the [football] club, who was running with the ball; when close up to his quarry, the little fellow `rabbited' him. The giant went sprawling and the little fellow jumped up and continued his run amid roars of laughter from the spectators. <i>(1935,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,59</i>) [NOTES: c1870's]



 
 <i>v.</i> (esp. as rabbiting verbal noun) (in rugby) carry a grounded ball illegally over the try line [does this come from an early cit ?1905 ?1910] Johnny Johnson saw what I did ... that Williams had rabbited the ball across the goalline.]

>  <b>GASKELL</b> Offside a mile. Rabbiting. You handled it on the ground.' `Free-kick be damned ... It was a good forward's try. Right out the book.' <i>(1947,Big Game,8</i>)

>  `Offside a mile. Rabbiting. You handled it on the ground.' `Free-kick be damned ... It was a good forward's try ... <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,6</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Gaskell in 1977 N.Z. Listener Short Stories 6]

>  <b>GASKELL</b> `Offside a mile. Rabbiting. You handled it on the ground. ...' <i>(1978,All Part of the Game,33</i>) [NOTES: 1944 Gaskell `Big Game' in 1978 All Part of the Game 33]

>  ... the big Jeremy Janion decided to go on his own, only to be picked up in a tackle of the covering Dougan. Janion then wriggled his way over the line but rightly was penalised for rabbiting. <i>(Sep 6, 1973,Dominion,16/7</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> rabbiting illegal carrying of grounded rugby ball over try line. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,91</i>)

>  <b>SWAN</b> Members of the City Clubs held a meeting to modify the Laws of the Game... This meeting, on June 19, decided that :- "Slinging, Rabbiting and Butting be added to Rule 57." <i>(1948,History of NZ Rugby Football,70</i>) [NOTES: From 1877]

>  <b>SWAN</b> After "rabbiting" for a while, Abbott all but scored as a result of such a movement. <i>(Jul 4, 1930,Northern Advocate,7</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to steal surreptitiously (esp. potatoes by digging out potato tubers, leaving the tops still growing; BANDICOOT; MILK) (cf RABBIT n. for poss. derivation; phs f. general idea of rabbits' burrowing for tubes or perhaps from a stock answer to enquiry `Where's my potatoes?', `The rabbits have got them.' But rabbits would usually eat tops and leave tubers.) [ca1930 Nelson potatoes -->ca1952 Wellington potatoes]

>  <b>GLOVER</b> On the way out at the gate Joe [an ex-convict] hesitated then said to himself, No, it wouldn't be fair to rabbit the old codger's letterbox. <i>(1978,Men of God,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an article made for private use with official materials in official time [-->@n1 (Naval Dockyards, Auckland) -->a FOREIGNER;. -->ca1940 at least in Naval Dockyards, Auckland. (J.P.M. Cornwall) `perhaps Australian' -->cf P: 5 Property stolen from the Royal Dockyards, most frequently used in Devonp ort: naval: 20c: Bowen. 10 (ex5.) Any `scrounged' article: Naval: since ca1920; AND v borrow, stal B1943; ?Rastafarian argot]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to collar an opponent from behind by grabbing by the back of the neck and by the trousers, lifting him and pushing him forward on his face. [(? obs -->[?d]? 1941 St Patrick's (Silverstream) -->cf P: (B 1942) 2. `collar opponent by ankles' C.20]



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> [to kill rabbits / free land of rabbits]

>  The next run hadn't been properly rabbited, and his run was so rough that an Inspector couldn't do any more himself; even a dog could hardly run amongst the scrub. <i>(Oct 24, 1883,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  rabbit v.t. to render an area free from rabbits. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> Rabbit.- To kill rs., to be employed as a rabbiter. (C.) He may be a permanent or a temporary hand, or may take a block for the skins. He may use dog, gun, and spade, poison, or traps. ([1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> Leighton said: ` ... To attempt to rabbit Molesworth [a station] with unemployed would involve the presence of an ambulance.' <i>(1969,Molesworth,128</i>)

### rabbit block
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McLEOD</b> At the end of the [mustering] season they took a rabbit block together, and had quite a good winter. <i>Ibid.,</i> 124 ~: A contract to catch rabbits on a certain piece of country, either for wages or for the skins. <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,27</i>)

### Rabbit Board
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  At a meeting of the Awatere Rabbit Board, the Chairman read a letter from Mr. Beaumont, a runholder, who has made various experiments in getting rid of the rabbit pest on his property. <i>(Oct 4, 1887,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Mr Huddlestone, the clerk and inspector to the Rabbit Board, states that he saw a kea attacking a rabbit. <i>(Jun 4, 1881,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  Speaking at a meeting of the Rabbit Board, the Chairman (Mr T. Crosse) drew attention to the proposal to erect a rabbit canning factory at Waikato, and declared that any attempt to use the rabbit commercially would seriously handicap the efforts to cope with the pest. <i>(May 5, 1908,Hawera & Normanby Star,7</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> The chaps working for the Rabbit Board were coming to take away our old draught horses. <i>(1971,Our Open Country,242</i>)

>  Our local Rabbit Board has not employed a rabbiter for the last six months. <i>(Mar 25, 1943,NZ Farmers' Weekly,34</i>)

>  TUTURAU RABBIT BOARD. Notice to Landowners in the Tuturau Rabbit District. The Trustees of the above Rabbit District have this day levied a RATE of 1/4d per acre, in terms of Clause 9 of the Rabbit Nuisance Ordinance. <i>(Sep 11, 1880,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  At present rabbit districts can only be constituted by the Governor on petition, and rabbits must be found in great numbers before the settlers will take on themselves the additional burden and taxation which the existence of a rabbit board implies. <i>(Aug 6, 1881,Otago Witness,21</i>)

>  The Wairarapa North Rabbit Board have offered a subsidy of 10s per pair of ferrets turned out by ratepayers in the district. <i>(Aug 14, 1890,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Holding, then, these views it was with satisfaction that we observed that the Wallace County Council, at its last meeting, showed that it, at least, has other ideas besides the making of roads and bridges, by taking action as to the amalgamation of rabbit boards with country councils, a step, as we think, in the right direction, and one which we commend to the attention of such local bodies and Parliamentary constituencies as may be interested therein. <i>(Sep 3, 1879,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  Why the rabbit-poison supplied by the Government is - in the opinion of the Rabbit Boards - so poor in quality, and whether some of the dope with which Ministers try to chloroform the people would not be much more effective if tried on the rabbits? <i>(Mar 28, 1925,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  <b>ARMFELT</b> Kane Potts ... helped Terence on the Rabbit Board. <i>(1971,Catching Up,67</i>)

>  <b>KERSE</b> However, about the 1950s, a new concept of rabbit control was emerging. This was `Rabbit Board' control. Within a defined area, the farmers, as rate-payers, elected the members of the Board whose task was to destroy the rabbits within that area. But by no means did the Otama Rabbit Board have a gentle and smooth passage to life. <i>(1984,Knapdale Run, The,195</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> When he was at Blenheim school he used to hitch his way, where his brother was on the Rabbit Board. <i>(1985,G'day Country,121</i>)

>  To his mind it was a dangerous scheme to start as a big proportion of the farmers did not need this assistance and if the "Rabbit Board" was formed, extra expense would be put on all farmers. <i>(Sep 20, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,4</i>)

>  In their hey-day (1960) there were 210 Rabbit Boards. <i>(2008,NZ Geographic,94/93</i>)

### rabbit camp
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a makeshift shelter or hut for rabbiters and rabbit skins. [also rabbiters' camp] (See [rabbiters' camp](ra-red.md#rabbiters'-camp))

>  The working men are seen no more in hut or rabbit camp, / The stockwhip never will be heard about the river swamp, / No more the mighty fleeces crown the bins like drifted snow, / No more princely rams go down, the wonder of the show ... <i>(Oct 8, 1896,Otago Witness,50</i>)

>  He added that he would take her to a rabbit-camp with him, though the girl did not know what a rabbit camp was. <i>(May 6, 1926,NZ Truth,7</i>)

>  The hills and downs showed one large rabbit camp, and quantities of dead rabbits. <i>(Aug 8, 1884,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>McKEE WRIGHT</b> <i>(1897,Station Ballads,44</i>)

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> Rabbit camp. <i>(1897,Station Ballads,44</i>)

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> He then set out over the Robinson Saddle for the rabbit camp on the shore of Lake McRae. <i>(1969,Molesworth,116</i>)

### rabbit cart
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a collection vehicle for rabbit carcasses. [Also attrib]

>  One notices the rabbit cart going the rounds of the district now, and by all appearances the rabbiters are having a good opening. <i>(Apr 19, 1900,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  FOR SALE, CHEAP. SECOND-HAND Thorough-brace Coach, suitable for Heavy Rabbit Cart, up to two tons. <i>(Mar 3, 1900,Mataura Ensign,3</i>)

>  FOR SALE. Also Light Spring Cart, suitable for Milk Cart or Rabbit cart. Thoroughly overhauled and painted. <i>(Jun 19, 1917,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  The rabbit cart is not going to run until March 1. <i>(Feb 23, 1899,Otago Witness,34</i>)

>  Lugeon, the old rabbit cart horse, made a star showing at Wingatui. <i>(Jun 8, 1912,NZ Truth,2</i>)

>  The rabbit cart still plies on the roads, conveying poor bunnies to the packing depot. <i>(Jul 15, 1898,Clutha Leader,6</i>)

### Rabbit Club
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  A club has been formed at Masterton, Wellington province, having for its object the destruction of rabbits. It is to be called the "Rabbit Club," and orders have been sent to Melbourne for a number of greyhounds. <i>(Apr 27, 1876,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  "I have before me a copy of the Canterbury Rabbit Club's rules, printed at the Press office in 1875. The 30 gentlemen who formed the club, under a president, other officers, and committee, and a gamekeeper (to protect rabbits from poachers), had undertook the preserving of rabbits on Templar's Island ..." <i>(Aug 30, 1905,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  Coster, ranger, under the Protection of Animals Act, said he saw the accused on the Rabbit Clubs reserve on Messrs Ford and Newton's run, with dogs and guns, twice. <i>(Jun 11, 1874,Star, The,2</i>)

### Rabbit Commissioner
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The Rabbit Commissioner's report was characterised as calculated to injure Southland, and to retard settlement. <i>(Jul 8, 1876,Otago Witness,19</i>)

### Rabbit Committee
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  THE RABBIT COMMITTEE .. The following resolution shows that the Rabbit Committee were far too much in earnest to be very choice in their language. <i>(Jun 19, 1875,Otago Witness,7</i>)

### rabbit country
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) land where rabbits flourish, to the detriment of farm stock.

>  <b>ACTON-ADAMS</b> I enclose a copy of my income-tax returns to prove to you how unprofitable this rabbit country is. <i>(1892,Letter,153</i>) [NOTES: in McCaskill (1969) Molesworth]

### rabbit district
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  At present rabbit districts can only be constituted by the Governor on petition, and rabbits must be found in great numbers before the settlers will take on themselves the additional burden and taxation which the existence of a rabbit board implies. <i>(Aug 6, 1881,Otago Witness,21</i>)

### rabbit dog
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rabbiter's dog trained to catch and kill rabbits. [aka rabbit-dog]

>  - After discussion, on the motion of Cr DUNCAN, seconded by Cr ORBELL, it was resolved - "That the clerk of this Council reply to the ratepayers in Waihemo with regard to the petition in reference to rabbit-dogs, informing them that the rabbit inspector is the person to make the application to." <i>(Apr 1, 1882,Otago Witness,22</i>)

>  WANTED 12 Rabbit Dogs, from 6 months to 3 years of age. Terriers and collies preferred. <i>(Feb 3, 1876,Southland Times,3</i>)

>  Rabbit dogs.- Those spoken of such include greyhounds, lurchers, spaniels, and nondescript terriers; sometimes retrievers. They were called the r. pack and in the `nineties some station r.ps. had as many as 30 dogs in them. If one of them retrieved he was called a carrier and sometimes rabbiters point out a dog as a great nester (smeller out of r. nests). (C.) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  It was resolved that the dog tax for the ensuing year should be as follow:- Sheep and rabbit dogs 2s 6d each, all other dogs 5s each, and the police were appointed collectors of the tax. <i>(Feb 8, 1894,Star, The,3</i>)

>  WANTED to Sell - Good Rabbit Dog. <i>(Aug 2, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,1</i>)

>  This later gang should be provided with a few quiet-working rabbit dogs to 'hole' any rabbits that may be in the grass or scrub. <i>(Dec 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,605</i>)

>  Rabbit dogs.- Those spoken of such include greyhounds, lurchers, spaniels, and non-descript terriers; sometimes retrievers. <i>(Nov 18, 1993,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> My brother-in-law was riding down the Ure River, by Boar Gully, with his rabbit dogs.. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,117</i>)

>  A fox-terrier, a member of a team of rabbit dogs, ran into a thicket of manuka scrub, and did not come out again. <i>(Mar 13, 1906,Wanganui Herald,7</i>)

### rabbit factory
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There is some talk of a rabbit factory being started at Heriot. <i>(Sep 18, 1889,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  In the Marlborough district the landowners were being ruined and their property devastated until the establishment of the rabbit factory in the centre of the infested district. <i>(Jan 8, 1890,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  The Mount Ida Chronicle is informed that the old rabbit factory at Waipiata is being fitted up as a sanitorium, under the supervision of Dr Martin, of Dunedin. <i>(Jan 17, 1906,Otago Witness,61</i>)

>  A man named Cossgrove , who had been employed at Spain and Company's rabbit factory at Alexandra, was found dead at the foot of a cliff yesterday. <i>(Aug 24, 1915,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  Claim for GBP5, balance of GBP10 drawn from the Rabbit Factory on account of Scott and Laurie and Walshe and Taafe, who had been rabbiting together at Meadow Bank. <i>(Nov 24, 1885,Marlborough Express,3</i>)

>  There were pictures of the Coal and Copper Mines, the Rabbit factory, the Catholic Church with grass like velvet and a latticed fence smothered with pink, red and white roses overlooking the Heather Hill. <i>(Apr 21, 1931,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,1</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> [caption] Taking a load of rabbit carcases to the Kurow Rabbit Factory established by Thompson and Finlayson in 1891. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,40</i>)

### rabbit fence
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rabbit proof fence, with weirs and gates, that ran for extensive lengths between stations and provinces. (See [rabbit-line](ra-red.md#rabbit-line))

>  The furry pest is not yet in the province, but has spread to the rabbit fence on the southern boundary ... <i>(Jun 15, 1883,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  The body of Angus Hart, a rabbit fence keeper, of Balmoral has been found in the Dove River. <i>(Jul 23, 1906,Wanganui Herald,5</i>)

>  En route, the traveller croses [sic] the Hawkes Bay boundary, and also passes through the rabbit fence, which runs from the Pahiatua side right through to the sea at Akitio. <i>(Apr 9, 1913,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  <b>ACTON-ADAMS</b> By an agreement ... dated 25 July 1899 they agreed that `the said WActon-Adams might depasture cattle ... northward ... of the rabbit fence which runs along the Clarence and over Maling's Pass.' <i>(Feb 7, 1901,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1901 Feb 7 Acton-Adams Letter in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 85]

>  [Rabbit Nuisance Act] Rabbit-proof Fencing. [heading] Rabbit Fences. - In respect of rabbit fencing, the different legal rabbit-proof fences are:- ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,216</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> Though rabbit fences are still used by individuals, no attempt has since been made to protect a whole province, as in 1887. <i>(1938,High Country,163</i>)

>  <b>CHRISTIE</b> The local county council employed a full-time rabbit inspector to visit farmers and ensure that active steps were taken to eradicate the pest and to enforce the erection of rabbit fences on the perimeter of those farms bordering the wasteland. <i>(1987,Candles and Canvas,121</i>)

>  Tenders are now invited by the trustees for splitting and hauling of timber for the erection of six miles of rabbit fence to be erected on Akitio run. <i>(Sep 5, 1881,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  The rabbit fence, it had been shown, has been carried within seven miles of the junction of the Tiraumea and the Manawatu. <i>(Jun 11, 1889,Waipawa Mail,2</i>)

>  All rabbit fences may be declared sufficient under the Fencing Act <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,217</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Beyond the rabbit fence, at the mouth of Steyning Creek, we sidled down some narrow faces. These rabbit fences are a feature of this part of the country. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,179</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> He put them through the rabbit fence into Fisher's Hope, then rode back to the hut. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,75</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> For the benefit of those who've not seen a rabbit fence, they have wire netting from about the second top wire, down to eighteen inches deep in the ground. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,76</i>)

>  <b>POULTON</b> I first became aware of the Rabbit Fence as a child in the 1930s. Rabbit fence separating Hawke's Bay and Wellington Provinces was 52 miles stretching from coast to Woodville in 1884. <i>(2000,Southern Hawke's Bay Rabbit , The,xi.</i>)

>  A rabbit fence separates Birch Hill from Glentanner, the next station down the Tasman River. <i>(Feb 2002,Historic Places,84 / 33</i>)

>  The Royal Commissioners appointed by Parliament to examine and report upon the efficiency of the two rabbit fences in Canterbury, and to state whether in their opinion the money these have cost has been judiciously expended, have just been engaged in an exhaustive inspection of the South Canterbury rabbit fence. <i>(Mar 24, 1891,Star, The,3</i>)

### rabbit gate
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a gate formed of rabbit netting across a bridge to prevent rabbits from entering a province or station.

>  <b>BENNETT</b> On the Taranaki end of the bridge a mesh gate was installed. It was called the rabbit gate and was designed to stop the rabbits coming from Auckland province into Taranaki. <i>(1990,Bryans, The: At Home on the Farm,33</i>)

>  <b>POULTON</b> The rabbit gate was in a saddle at 1780 ft on top of the Waewaepa Range. <i>(2000,Southern Hawke's Bay Rabbit , The,xi</i>)

>  Mokau resident Ian Whittaker said a 10-minute ceremony at the Southern end of the bridge at 8pm on Saturday would re-enact history with the symbolic closing of a replica of the old 'rabbit gate.' <i>(Jan 10, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,2</i>)

>  By law, motorists still had to contend with a 'rabbit gate' stationed at each end of the bridge. <i>(2001,NZ Memories,June/July 63</i>)

### rabbit inspector
 
 <i>n.</i> [[Check Act or Regulations for appointment.]]

>  Mr. Bamford (Rabbit Inspector) furnished the Council with an account amounting to GBP3 15s 3d being wages of men employed destroying rabbits on the Benger commonage. <i>(May 15, 1880,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  Piripi Waaka was fined GBP5 and costs 9s for failing to obey an order of the Court to destroy rabbits on his property. The charge was preferred against the native by L.C. Webb, rabbit inspector, who stated that accused stopped work before the pest was satisfactorily dealt with. <i>(Apr 8, 1911,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  Nearly every Rabbit Inspector in the Australasian Colonies swears by it, and yet when, in 1884 - 1886, we suppressed the rabbit in South Wairarapa - over 1,500,000 acres of land - rabbit-fencing was avioded as a remedy. <i>(Jun 15, 1895,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  A letter was received from Mr. D. Mackellar, Tapanui, pointing out that a great deal of destruction was occasioned by the wholesale cutting of bush in that district, and suggesting the appointment of Mr. Bamford, rabbit inspector, to have an oversight of the bush in that locality. <i>(Mar 22, 1879,Otago Witness,11</i>)

>  The Clerk was instructed to direct the lessee of the Domain to clear rabbits to the satisfaction of the Rabbit Inspector. <i>(Jun 14, 1921,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  It is to be hoped, with our acts of Parliament and rabbit inspectors, that we will make an end of bunny this time; but rabbiting is not the gold mine of years gone by. <i>(Apr 8, 1897,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  ... a reference to Mr. Bayley's (the Chief Rabbit Inspector of the colony) annual report for 1888 will show that the Government and every Rabbit Inspector are willingly allowing the use of traps <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/429</i>)

>  <b>ACTON-ADAMS</b> I hereby surrender to the Crown my licence of the Tarndale back country. I find it impossible to comply with the requirements of the present Rabbit Inspector. <i>Ibid.,</i> 93 [See quot. RABBITING vbl. n.] <i>(Jan 1, 1893,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1893 Jan 1 Acton-Adams letter to Commissioner of Crown Lands in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 78]

>  ->[include the famous story of rabbit inspector] -->nf OED, W3 <i>(Dec 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,517/2</i>)

>  <b>CHRISTIE</b> The local county council employed a full-time rabbit inspector to visit farmers and ensure that active steps were taken to eradicate the pest and to enforce the erection of rabbit fences on the perimeter of those farms bordering the wasteland. <i>(1987,Candles and Canvas,121</i>)

>  The rabbit Inspector notified the Board to fumigate rabbits in embankments and close up rabbit holes. <i>(Apr 8, 1930,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,4</i>)

>  The rabbit inspector at Ashburton was sufficiently alarmed by the discovery of a colony of rabbits in the Upper Rangitata to recommend the building of a rabbit-proof fence from the upper reaches of the river to the sea. <i>(Dec 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,517</i>)

### rabbit net
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  I, Charles G. Thurston, do agree to transfer all my right in perpetual lease sections 5, 6, 7, and 8, block 2, Wendonside district, and to build a six-roomed house, plant 1000 forest trees and 500 fruit and rabbit net them in; also, put up a windmill and fix same to pump to lift water for domestic use ... <i>(Aug 28, 1890,Southland Times,3</i>)

### rabbit pack
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a team of dogs used by rabbiters to locate and kill rabbits.

>  Now uncle keeps a rabbit pack, to earn an honest penny, / And in the pack of nondescripts the breeds are mixed and many. <i>(Jun 9, 1910,Grey River Argus,8</i>)

>  A rabbit pack belonging to a rabbiter working on the Brothers Range, near Fairlie, captured a splendid specimen of either a kangaroo or a wallaby the other day. <i>(Feb 5, 1902,Otago Witness,54</i>)

>  They are supposed to be dogs that originally belonged to rabbit packs. <i>(Oct 9, 1890,Otago Witness,27</i>)

>  <b>EVANS</b> The dogs were a mixture of collies, lurchers, spaniles, greyhounds and terriers. It was an excellent rabbit pack. <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,122</i>)

>  <b>MCGREGOR</b> During the early days some of the stations had their own rabbit packs and kept a special horse which the dogs were in the habit of following. The story is told of a new hand on a Wairarapa station, whose first job was to ride out with the rabbit pack. <i>(1980,Dog's Life, A,36</i>)

>  <b>SCURR</b> rabbit pack: the dogs in a rabbiter's team. <i>(2000,Rabbiters' Glossary,3</i>)

### rabbit proof gate
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  G. A.F. Wilding, Ohai, applied for permission to erect two rabbit proof gates across the Wairaki settlement road between Sections 75 and 80, and Sections 79 and 91. <i>(Oct 21, 1924,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,1</i>)

>  A letter was read from Mr C. R. Campbell, stating that a rabbit proof gate will be erected on the North side of the Lower Hurunui traffic bridge, as requested by the Board. <i>(Oct 16, 1890,Star, The,4</i>)

>  The Board resolved to further enquire concerning the rabbit proof gate on the Delta flat before taking any action. <i>(Jul 6, 1895,Marlborough Express,4</i>)

### rabbit sick
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of land and vegetation] ruined by rabbits.

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> The Commissioner of Lands made a personal inspection and wrote to the Under-Secretary - 'The greater part of the best land is rapidly becoming rabbit sick.' <i>(1969,Molesworth,157</i>)

### rabbit-cart
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We were struggling home with a big night's catch [of rabbits] - struggling down to the road-line, along which the rabbit-cart would travel next day. <i>(Feb 10, 1921,Quick March,45</i>)

### rabbit-fencing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It was suggested that settlers should be empowered to combine and form rabbit-fencing districts. <i>(May 7, 1886,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  Nearly every Rabbit Inspector in the Australasian colonies swears by it, and yet when, in 1884-1886, we suppressed the rabbit in South Wairarapa - over 1,500,000 acres of land - rabbit-fencing was avoided as a remedy. <i>(Jun 15, 1895,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The tender of Mills and Co., of Wellington, has been accepted for forty-six miles of rabbit-fencing material to complete the fence in South Canterbury, from Lake Pukaki to the Waitaki River. <i>(Sep 29, 1888,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  I cannot see on what grounds the sheep-farmers of Hawke's Bay County should be asked to contribute towards fencing-in the Ponrangahau runs unless they are willfully blind to the fact that rabbits exist in many of their own sections, and so rapidly do they increase that it will not be long ere they will want rabbit fencing for themselves. <i>(Aug 11, 1883,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  It is fondly hoped that the Danyz experiments will prove effective and therefore save the expenditure of much capital in erecting rabbit fencing. <i>(Apr 18, 1907,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  [nf AND] <i>Ibid.</i> 431 I placed as little reliance on the ferret as I did upon poisoning or rabbit-fencing. <i>(1889,TrNZI,1/309</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> There are fifty miles of rabbit-fencing on this property which will, doubtless, in time prove beneficial; ... <i>(1894,In the Land of the Tui,202</i>)

>  [Rabbit Nuisance Act] Rabbit-proof Fencing. [heading] Rabbit Fences. - In respect of rabbit fencing, the different legal rabbit-proof fences are:- ... <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,216</i>)

>  In many cases there are wire entanglements and lengths of rabbit fencing, both in the woods and in the open, carefully aligned so that they can be swept by rifle fire and machine guns, which are invisible from our side of the valley. <i>(Sep 24, 1914,Evening Post,7</i>)

### rabbit-hole
 
 <i>n.</i> [(WW1, Gallipoli)]

>  <b>HYDE</b> The men crept away into their dug-outs and bled to death. Their mates ... found them stiffened up in the rabbit-holes, ... 122 If you start thinking ..., your being stowed away in a rabbit-hole where the next whirling, twisting fire-cracker coming down ... may be your own packet. <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,119</i>)

>  He crawled out of his rabbit hole and got up a bit nearer us, and hid behind a 'ummock only a few yards away, waitin' his chance. <i>(Mar 23, 1915,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,7</i>)

### rabbit-killer
 
 <i>n.</i> a rabbit-punch [ca1935 Havelock P: only Rabbit's punch, [check OEDS]]

### rabbit-line
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rabbit-proof fence.

>  <b>TAIT</b> The rabbit line is a 52-mile fence which took 6 years to complete. <i>(1958,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol III,129</i>)

### rabbit-net
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  In the first place, I would rabbit-net all bush reserves where they adjoin cultivated or surface-sown lands ... <i>(Aug 24, 1893,Otago Witness,12</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> The old mustering and boundary huts were of stone and the fences were rabbit-netted. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,68</i>)

>  <b>KERSE</b> Farmers within the proposed area who had no rabbit problem naturally objected to paying another rate as did farmers who had cleared their farms of the pest, some even having their boundaries `rabbit-netted'. <i>(1984,Knapdale Run, The,195</i>)

### rabbit-netted
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  At the present time there are large plots of land in the Ida Valley, rabbit netted and irrigated, which in the greenness of their grass stand out as an oasis. <i>(Apr 23, 1917,Poverty Bay Herald,6</i>)

>  Improvements comprise about 3 miles fencing; of which one boundary is rabbit-netted; hut and two yards. <i>(Apr 24, 1907,Otago Witness,37</i>)

### rabbit-netting
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There is a rabbit-proof fence round the inner part of the property, and each section as taken up is also fenced with rabbit-netting 2ft 6in high. <i>(Oct 13, 1890,Wanganui Herald,3</i>)

>  Rabbit netting advanced 10 per cent. <i>(Nov 28, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,5</i>)

>  Permission was granted Mr. A. Falls to enclose with rabbit-netting a piece of council land leased by him in Clark-street. <i>(Dec 9, 1909,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  -"Inquirer," Waitepeka, can get rabbit-netting at Le Keong's, George street, for a shilling a yard. <i>(Oct 7, 1882,Otago Witness,18</i>)

>  Thrashing Machines on various principles .. fiend, carriage, and entrance gates, rabbit netting &c. <i>(Jun 2, 1860,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  Clearing Sale .. Sheep Netting, Cyclone Netting, Rabbit Netting, Sundries, etc. <i>(Jul 3, 1922,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  10,000 YARDS Galvanized Rabbit Netting <i>(Aug 26, 1879,Southland Times,4</i>)

### rabbit-poisoner
 
 <i>n.</i>

### rabbit-proof
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  WIRE FENCING. THE UNDERSIGNED HAS ON SALE AND TO ARRIVE:- .. Rabbit-proof 15 7/8 [imperial measurement] do. <i>(Nov 22, 1867,North Otago Times,3</i>)

>  The Rabbit-proof Wire-netting Fences Bill and the Land Drainage Amendment Bill were read a third time and passed. <i>(Nov 3, 1898,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Then, as time wore on, the rabbiters might acquire the freehold of moderate areas for their homesteads, and, availing themselves of the latest and cheapest forms of wirework, fence in portions of their holdings as their means allowed - 'rabbit-proof.' <i>(Jul 24, 1880,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  There are 21 miles of post and wire fencing, and on the side towards the hills there are eight miles of rabbit-proof wire netting. <i>(May 17, 1879,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  Seventy-five acres [in Otago] ... have been fenced with rabbit-proof netting. <i>(1879,AJHR,C-1</i>) [NOTES: Lands and Survey Report]

>  With regard to rabbit-proof fencing, I always thought it a [430] weak thing, and I would have nothing to do with it. <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/429</i>)

>  Swing Gates. - Swing gates or rabbit-proof gates can be put up across roads and bridges with the consent of the local authority; they can remain only during that authority's pleasure. They must be properly hung, and provided with an efficient fastening capable of being opened by a person on horseback. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,163</i>)

>  Second Quality Rabbit-Proof Wire Netting <i>(1912,Sheepowner's Handbook, The,57</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> No hard and fast rules can be laid down as to the class of fence to erect, as so much depends on the purpose ... e.g., whether boundary, rabbit proof, etc., ... <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,15</i>)

>  [caption] Rabbit-proof enclosure, same species within and without ... <i>(1938,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,20/229A</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> Rabbit-proof nettting was mentioned in Middleton's [c1880s] reports only when an enclosure of one acre was planned to lure rabbits on to a crop. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,29</i>)

### rabbit-proof fence
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  This portion comprises what is known as Peninsula Hill, which could be easily protected by a rabbit-proof fence ... <i>(Jun 12, 1880,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  Mr Harvey appeared on behalf of Captain Raymond to ask for a variation in the boundary line of his run, No. 154, Taringatura district, as he wanted to put up a rabbit proof fence in a straight line at a particular part of his run. <i>(Dec 8, 1876,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  Mr McClure's fine farm of a thousand acres presents an object lesson in the efficacy of rabbit proof fences as a remedy for the rabbit plague. <i>(Sep 1, 1914,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,5</i>)

>  (2.) Sufficient Rabbit-proof Fence. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,161</i>) [NOTES: 1895 Fencing Act in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 161]

>  Valuation for improvements at the end of a lease is not to exceed three times the annual rent, or five times when the rent is under [pounds]50. Rabbit-proof fences are extra. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,71</i>)

>  ... but when the [pounds]100 spent in repairing and renewing rabbit-proof fences is considered as improvements to the property the total account should only be debited with [pounds]241 ... <i>(Jan 15, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,475</i>)

>  rabbit v.t. to render an area free from rabbits. -rabb'iter n. one employed to kill rabbits, an important industry in rabbit-infested areas.-rabb'it-proof fence' n. a fence that rabbits cannot pass, even by trying to burrow under it. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  - fence. <i>(Dec 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,517/2</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> `We'll put the mob through the rabbit-proof fence,' Rusty instructed ... <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,39</i>)

>  <b>NEAVE</b> As some protection, a great rabbit proof fence was erected which ran for 80 miles from the Waitaki River ... to the Tekapo River. <i>(1980,Land of Munros, The,122</i>)

>  The Works Committee reported having called tenders for (1) Rabbit proof Fence (32 chains) round Cemetery Reserve; (2) Materials Shed; (3) Footbridge, tenders to be considered this meeting. <i>(Feb 12, 1929,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  We have no rabbit-proof fences, not to any extent. <i>(1884,AJHR,I-5/127</i>)

### rabbit-proof fencing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Rabbits are committing such depredations on the Hon. Neil Black's estate in Victoria that he is going to erect thirteen miles of rabbit-proof fencing. <i>(Jul 6, 1878,Otago Witness,20</i>)

>  The Committee could bring forward useful suggestions, such as rabbit proof fencing, entrance gates, further beautifying work from planting, etc. <i>(Nov 3, 1931,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  An interesting decision to farmers was given in Wellington the other day regarding a case in which rabbit-proof fencing was concerned. <i>(Feb 27, 1909,Tuapeka Times,4</i>)

>  If some such simple and inexpensive plan, universally adopted, should prove unsuccessful in practically subduing the rabbit, then there is nothing for it but to go in for wholesale rabbit-proof fencing and subdividing runs and properties, and killing them off each block or subdivision in detail by the above and other means. <i>(May 3, 1877,Southland Times,2</i>)

>  [Rabbit Nuisance Act] Rabbit-proof Fencing. [heading] Rabbit Fences. - In respect of rabbit fencing, the different legal rabbit-proof fences are:- ... <i>Ibid.</i> 217 (attrib) It enables rabbit-proof fencing districts to be set up by the Governor (as small as 1,000 acres in area, with as few ratepayers as three) on petition of a majority of the ratepayers. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,216</i>)

>  RABBIT-PROOF FENCING NECESSARY. [heading] ... the first and most important step is the erection of rabbit-proof netting fences on the boundaries ... <i>(Dec 16, 1912,NZ Journal of Agriculture,602</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> In order to cope ... with the rabbit pest there must be ... a great extension of rabbit-proof fencing ... <i>(1948,Down the Years in the Maniototo,150</i>)

### rabbit-sick
 
 <i>adj.</i> of land, driven to infertility by overstocking by rabbits [(cf CATTLE-, SHEEP-, STOCK-SICK)]

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> The best country ... was `covered by rabbits in countless numbers'. Even the small paddocks adjoining the homestead were in a disgraceful condition and the greater part of the land was rapidly becoming hopelessly rabbit-sick. <i>(1969,Molesworth,121</i>)

### rabbit-skinner
 
 <i>n.</i> a nickname for a slow shearer. [(shearers')]

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> He spoke wise, god-fatherly words to Rabbit-skinner for five long minutes. <i>(1904,Sons o' Men,85</i>)

### rabbit-sucker
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [sucker](../S#sucker))

### rabbit-trapping
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LEE</b> The native pastures nourished as many rabbits as sheep, and a rabbit-trapping industry gave regular and considerable winter employment. <i>(1934,Children of the Poor,28,1949 ed.</i>)

### rabbiter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person employed either by a runholder, farmer, a local authority or Rabbit Board, to kill rabbits.

>  <b>HENRDERSON</b> That truck quaffed petrol like a beerdrinking rabbiter hitting town after six months in the back of the Dunstan. <i>(1971,Our Open Country,242</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> The chimney, it is true, left something to be desired; the last party of rabbiters had repaired the holes with old sacking and grass, with the result that when we lighted a fire we nearly set the cottage in a blaze. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,158</i>)

>  He is accounted a crack rabbiter, and the rabbits are fast advancing him into the foremost ranks of local society. <i>(Dec 8, 1873,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Then, as time wore on, the rabbiters might acquire the freehold of moderate areas for homesteads, and, availing themselves of the latest and cheapest forms of wirework, fence in portions of their holdings as their means allowed - 'rabbit-proof.' <i>(Jul 24, 1880,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  The Arbitration Court delivered judgement in the claim for compensation against the East Coast Rabbit Board lodged by the widow of a rabbiter killed by a fall over a cliff on New Year's Day whilst engaged in hunting goats to provide food for his dogs. <i>(Aug 3, 1915,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Over our breaks during three days he recounted the saga of the Mint Princess a rabbiter's wife he claimed to have loved in Culverden. <i>(Dec 10, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49</i>)

>  He could be the rabbiter, Walter Devon who lives out in the wilds of Springvale Station. He has no telephone or radio. <i>(Dec 2001,NZ Memories,No. 27/43</i>) [NOTES: 1936 quote]

>  A run of 160,000 acres, lately thrown up by Hon. R. Campbell on account of rabbits, has been rented by some rabbiters for GBP30 a year for the purpose of rabbiting. <i>(Jun 15, 1880,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  They were greeted by 45cm of snow, a rather ramshackle building, and two rabbiters who served them their first meal - rabbit stew, prunes and rice, all cooked in billies over an open fire. <i>(Jan 13, 2000,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  <b>CARR</b> In the North Island a pack of dogs and a gun are the rabbiter's stock-in-trade <i>(1913,Country Work,27</i>)

>  That Hutt was a rabbiter, who uses strychnine in the course of his occupation. <i>(Dec 22, 1927,NZ Truth,3</i>)

>  In the middle of a debate about greening commercial and residential building sites, one old dear makes a rousing speech about bringing back the rabbiters. <i>(Jul 31, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  ...the dried skins proved such a good income that some rabbiters were eventually able to buy the block of land they were working on. <i>(2008,NZ Geographic,94/93</i>)

>  <b>HAWKE & SCOTT</b> These furrows have been made by the rabbiters, they are the bait lines. <i>(1985,Early Farm Machinery in NZ,27</i>)

>  On Te Awaiti there are four outstations - three usually occupied by shepherds and one by a rabbiter. <i>(Nov 1891,NZ Farmer,418</i>)

>  <b>WILSON & FRASER</b> The rabbiters occasionally visited a hotel. <i>(1936,Book of the Pioneers, The,31</i>)

>  The [rabbit] board also decided to take over the control of all rabbiters. <i>(Nov 23, 1939,NZ Farmer,9</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND</b> About the month of May each year this rabbiter ceases operations as the snow becomes too thick around this area. <i>(1947,Holiday on Horseback, A,56</i>)

>  A party of six rabbiters, isolated by snow in backblocks of St Helen's Station ... <i>(Jul 4, 1947,Hawera Star,6</i>)

>  <b>MACINTOSH</b> Many farmers employed a rabbiter to trap and poison on their properties ... <i>(1974,Never a Dull Moment,125</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> The settlers employed a rabbiter by the name of Jack Johnson who went from farm to farm. <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,20</i>)

### rabbiters' breakfast
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> Next morning we were up too early for a meal at the cookhouse, so Mae said, `It'll have to be a rabbiters' breakfast for us today - a trip to the toilet and a cigarette.' <i>(1975,Milford Road,68</i>)

### rabbiters' camp
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka rabbit camp] (See [rabbit camp](ra-red.md#rabbit-camp))

>  He partly recovered, and dragged himself to a rabbiters' camp, where he obtained assistance to resuscitate Uren. <i>(Jan 17, 1910,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  They employ eighteen poisoners on the station, while six men with pack-horses (known as "Packers") are engaged in conducting traffic between the Home Station and the rabbiters' camp in the ranges - carrying out poisoned wheat and necessary supplies and returning with the rabbit skins. <i>(May 26, 1880,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  He saw three dogs belonging to a rabbiters' camp which were frozen stiff. <i>(Jul 23, 1895,North Otago Times,1</i>)

>  Since I last wrote to you I have got a job as cook in a rabbiters' camp, and it is nice work, I can tell you, especially when you have to get up at 5 o'clock every frosty morning. <i>(Jul 20, 1904,Otago Witness,73</i>)

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Each week ... Bessie packed up stores for the cooks at the two out-stations, for the rabbiters' camp and for the station cook-house. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,15</i>)

### rabbiters' hut
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  RUN No 1 - Containing about 3600 acres, upset rent, 4d per acre, subject to payment of valuation of L47 7s 6d, for fencing and rabbiters' hut. <i>(Oct 30, 1894,Mataura Ensign,5</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> It has always interested me in the back country to study the printed and pictorial matter in shepherds' and rabbiters' huts. <i>(Mar 19, 1951,Shepherd's Calendar,24,1961 ed.</i>)

>  Some idea of the trouble this involves may be gathered from the fact that one of the sub-enumerators will have to cover more than 30 miles of the roughest country lying between Outram and Middlemarch for the purpose of leaving census forms at about 70 households, many of them being diggers and rabbiters' huts in all kinds of out-of-the-way places amongst the everlasting hills. <i>(Apr 18, 1906,Otago Witness,16</i>)

### Rabbiters, the
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an exclusive gang of rural blue-blooded Hawke's Bay workers who were remittance men with some form of British title.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> The Rabbiters: a gang of rural Hawke's Bay workers made up of remittance men that included only those with some sort of British title. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,64</i>)

### rabbiting
 
 <i>n.</i> the occupation or activity of destroying rabbits [some citations below are of the verb to rabbit (v. 1)]

>  On the last occasion the inspector told him to go rabbiting on a certain flat where they were getting numerous. <i>(Nov 8, 1884,Otago Witness,13</i>)

>  Roberts played badly, having been on a rabbiting excursion in the forenoon and was knocked up. <i>(Sep 11, 1876,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> Last winter, a chap passed here who had been rabbiting at Barerocks.river. <i>(1900,Tales of the Colonial Turf,96</i>)

>  A run of 160,000 acres, lately thrown up by Hon. R. Campbell on account of rabbits, has been rented by some rabbiters for GBP30 a year for the purpose of rabbiting. <i>(Jun 15, 1880,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  A great many persons entertain the idea that rabbiting is an indolent life and affords an easy manner of obtaining bread and butter. Let such erroneous ideas cease to exist; rabbiting is essentially hard work. <i>(Dec 8, 1873,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  <b>RIVES</b> I went out rabbitting with the Trolove boys but never got a shot. <i>(Feb 11, 1875,Jottings on the Spot,1</i>)

>  A man .... was engaged `rabbiting' on the run <i>(1881,TrNZI,14/239</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

>  <b>JUNE CHRISTIAN SHIRRES</b> He casually mentioned having been rabbiting for seven months ... <i>(Jun 1888,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1888 June Christian Shirres Diary in 1980 Neave The Land of Munros 53]

>  <b>WALLACE</b> On the property ... ten men have to be kept `rabbiting' all the year round. <i>(1891,Rural Economy of Aust. & NZ,320</i>)

>  <b>ACTON ADAMS</b> Rainbow Rabbiting. You weakly gave in to the Inspector against my instructions .... The rabbit Inspector fairly bluffed you. <i>(1906,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1906 Acton Adams Letter in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 93]

>  Rabi.bit-ing (-&ibreve.ng), n. The occupation of a rabbiter. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

>  We were out rabbiting today and caught nine. <i>(Jan 23, 1890,Otago Witness,43</i>)

>  Robert Donaldson, nine years' [sic] old, was killed at the Peninsula while rabbiting. <i>(Jun 25, 1897,Feilding Star,2</i>)

>  It is to be hoped,with our acts of Parliament and rabbit inspectors, that we will make an end of bunny this time; but rabbiting is not the gold mine of years gone by. <i>(Apr 8, 1897,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  It thus appears that a saving has been effected in cost of rabbiting private lands only and it is hoped some more economical means may be found in dealing with lands for which this Department alone bears the financial responsibility. <i>(Oct 2, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,6</i>)

>  There he spotted the prodigal, "keeping body and soul together".. by "rabbiting", that is by destroying rabbits as vermin. <i>(Jun 4, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,8</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> After the final clean-up on Marchburn there was no rabbiting done there for thirty years. <i>(1956,Early NZ Families,167</i>)

>  We used to go out rabbiting with the dogs. <i>(Feb 19, 1924,Otago Witness,71</i>)

### rabbiting block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a contracted area of land to be cleared of rabbits.

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> The men had long heavy days on the rabbiting blocks.... <i>(1965,Skippers Road,47</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> At the end of the season they took a rabbit block together, and had quite a good winter. <i>(1958,Tall Tussock,27</i>)

>  <b>HOSKEN</b> Some time after this incident, Mr and Mrs T decided to leave and take a rabbiting block out on the run. <i>(1964,Life on a Five Pound Note,52</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> The men had long heavy days on the rabbiting blocks. <i>(1965,Beyond the Skippers Road,47</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> I wrote and found the advertiser was going overseas with the Expeditionary Force and wanted a youth to take his place with his uncle on their rabbiting block. [rabbit block] <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,26</i>)

### Rabbiting Commission
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It seems that that terrible report of the Rabbiting Commission in Southland has stirred up the small holders there to rebellion, or something like it. <i>(Jul 8, 1876,Otago Witness,19</i>)

### rabbiting dog
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BARRY</b> He had a great many rabbiting dogs of all breeds. <i>(1966,In the Lee of the Hokonuis,65</i>)

### rabbiting gang
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a team of rabbiters and their dogs, contracted to kill rabbits on a station or block.

>  <b>MCCASKILL</b> Brosnan undertook to look after the buildings in return for permission to occupy them with his rabbiting gang and thus become a kind of honorary caretaker. <i>(1969,Molesworth,133</i>)

### rabbito
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> The postie biked past twice a day ... The milko would rattle past with his cart of a morning ... cause we was getting milk through the Charitable Aid Board [c1930]. Then there was the rabbit-o, who sometimes talked Mum into buying a couple of his little skinned carcases. <i>(1987,Oracles and Miracles,40</i>)

>  <b>KEITH</b> One suburban sound of the 1940s, now completely vanished from New Zealand streets, was that of food vendors: the rabbito and the whitebait seller and the clip-clopping hooves of the milko's horse-drawn float - as the milk waggon was called. <i>(1991,Lovely Day Tomorrow, A,41</i>)

### rabbity
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>DICK</b> No one in his wildest dreams would ever have called our run `rabbity', and even before the advent of Rabbit Boards there were always only just enough rabbits for the odd pie and the succulent braised pot-roast: ... <i>(1964,High Country Family,109</i>)

### rabid
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> rabid amazed or angry, or pretending so, in 1960s teen slang; eg `Jane was rabid about losing her new Elvis record. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,91</i>)

### race
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SCOTT</b> He went off to hustle and push the sheep along the race. <i>(1953,Breakfast at Six,34</i>)

>  If there's no trace of a race, a place will hide its face in disgrace. <i>(Mar 28, 1902,Dominion,10</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Hadn't we seen our officers, when we came into camp, weeding 'em out - through the race - the culls into the infantry. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,238</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [stockyards] a passage through which stock pass single file in drafting, or into dips or showers, or the passage through which dairy cows pass entering or leaving a cowshed.

>  I have fixed up a bail at the end of the race, or crush pen. One side of the race at the bail is formed by a strong gate, which is opened when the cow is bailed for milking. So soon as the cow has entered the race a bar is slipped in behind her across the race, and another bar is slipped across behind her when close up to the bail. <i>(Mar 13, 1883,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> Made an attempt to brand but the race was too wide ... <i>(Oct 4, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,61</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> [the sheep] being turned out after they have passed through a narrow passage, called a 'race', where each sheep is branded. <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,34</i>)

>  <b>HARTLEY</b> For the practice in backing live sheep, the race used for fat lamb picking should be used. <i>(1949,Shepherd's Dogs, The,41</i>)

>  Backing dog - a useful type of huntaway, trained to scamper along sheep's backs in jammed races and loading ramps ... <i>(Aug 15, 1983,NZ Journal of Agriculture,10</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> A strong bail was made at the end of the race to secure his head. <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,50</i>)

>  Another feature of the plan before us is that instead of one gate at the end of the race, there are two, which when open are parallel with the sides of the race, giving a passage between them into a yard for lambs, while the opening of either gate closes this passage and opens a space sufficient to allow a single sheep to pass either into a yard for ewes or wethers. <i>(Jul 24, 1869,Otago Witness,14</i>)

### race call
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>COSTELLO & FINNEGAN</b> Kelly.. was to do the race calls at Trentham and most other lower North Island courses until his retirement in 1983. <i>(1988,Tapestry of Turf,208</i>)

### race holder
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  .. I shall apeak of resolution No.3 - viz, that any race holder or holders shall be at the mercy of one or more miners that shall have the liberty to run tailings into any water race, thereby rendering such water valueless. <i>(Aug 14, 1890,Otago Witness,14</i>)

>  A spontaneous offer of a reduction in the price of water by the race holders in this district was expected some 18 months ago. <i>(Feb 12, 1870,Otago Witness,9</i>)

>  ".. The Big Race Company even, who have hitherto enjoyed the advantage of a liberal supply of water, are now running short, and unless rain soon falls the Company will be placed in a similar position to that of other race holders ..." <i>(Jan 30, 1864,Otago Witness,5</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [Is it 'commissioner' or 'conciliator'???]

### Race Relations Conciliator
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Sir, - Surely our new race relations concilliator has misread his instructions? Or has he? Is he merely a Coloured Race Protector? <i>(May 14, 1986,Dominion,14</i>)

>  The Race Relations Conciliator ... has aroused much controversy since he coined the phrase `white flight' to describe parents of European - as opposed to the offensively racist and discriminatory term Pakeha - ancestry moving their children to schools where due emphasis is placed on values the majority of New Zealanders still adhere to. <i>(Oct 4, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  (John C. McNab) Brown and white there were no differences between the colours then [in WW2]. The happy members of the 28th Battalion referred to themselves as `horis' (I've never known what the word meant, but now to use it is as big an insult as for a white to be called a Pakeha), and spoke of `Maori time' (some time) and `Maori PT' (lying on one's bedroll) and we followed their lead, not knowing that they, and we, were using such appallingly insulting terms to the Maori race that a special Race Relations Conciliator would have to be installed to quell them. <i>(Jul 1, 1988,Dominion,10</i>)

>  On the face of it Rotorua is a long way from the prejudice of Auckland's rental accommodation business condemned by the former Race Relations Conciliator... <i>(May 17, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  The Attorney-General... has decided not to refer the recent spate of 'pom baiting' to the Race Relations Conciliator. <i>(Mar 14, 1974,Dominion,3</i>)

### raddle
 
 <i>v.</i> to rob (esp. be raddled)

>  raddled: This word means `robbed' in the sense of being deprived of one's proper payment. It is an extension of `raddling', carried out by unscrupulous farmers before the Shearers' Union looked after shearers' interests. Any sheep considered unsatisfactorily shorn was marked with red or blue raddle and such a sheep would not be counted in the shearer's tally. The raddle stick was also called a `Toby'. This must not be confused with [27] marking a sheep for other purposes, such as identification of certain sheep for easy selection later. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/26-27</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I did not brand the bales, but marked them with raddle, and if there were two sorts I would mark them so. I also sent tickets describing the wool. I have sometimes known mistakes between the raddling and branding; with a large lot and different men mistakes might easily arise. <i>(Jun 23, 1892,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  Bath bricks, raddle, linseed oil ... <i>(Dec 15, 1852,Taranaki Herald,1</i>)

>  ON SALE, by the undersigned:- Woolpacks, sheepshears, sacks, pitch, linseed oil, lamp black, raddle, fencing wire and iron standards. <i>(Dec 27, 1876,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  Direct from Calcutta. Wool Packs .. full stocks raddle, marking oil, boiled oil, stencils, wool hooks ... <i>(Nov 1, 1898,Feilding Star,2</i>)

>  Same day - S.S. Storm Bird, 67 tons, Malcolm, from Melbourne, 995 bags Chilian flour .. 10 cases axes, 1 case levels, 1 cask raddle, 5 cases drapery ... <i>(Dec 29, 1860,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  North of Ireland Chemical Dip, Red Bolme Yellow Green Raddle, Sheep Marking Oil (4 colours), Johnson's English Galvanised Wire (6, 7, 8, and 4 and 6-inch barb) Galvanised staples (all sizes), Hay Rake and Lever Harrows. <i>(Dec 19, 1902,Wanganui Herald,6</i>)

>  PRICE LIST .. Raddle 0[s] 8[d] ... <i>(Nov 2, 1921,Ashburton Guardian,4</i>)

>  Raddle in those days [1880's] was in great demand, and when the boss of the board was in a bad humor [sic] or perchance did not like the color of the shearer's hair he used the raddle on the sheep shorn by the shearer and the raddle sheep were counted out and the shearer not paid for them. <i>(Mar 29, 1912,Maoriland Worker,55/12/2</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Les and Alex got hold of several lumps of raddle, some red and some blue, caught Dinny, put broad red and blue stripes on the natural white background of his coat, ... <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,77</i>)

>  ON SALE .. Wool Packs, Sheep Shears, Lamp Black, and Raddle ... <i>(Nov 17, 1880,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to mark sheep with a raddle stick for identification purposes.

>  Briefly stated, the complaint of these shearers is that Mr John McCaw, who is described as manager for the Bank of New Zealand Estates Company, went to this particular shed, at which there were twelve shearers, accompanied by his under-manager, and made the under-manager umpire to decide which sheep should be 'raddled,' or 'marked,' and which should not. <i>(Dec 14, 1895,Observer (Auckland),2</i>)

>  `Well, boss' says he, `I shear for myself in the morning, and for my girl in the afternoon, and he wouldn't be much of a man who could raddle a sheep on a girl, would he, boss?' <i>(Oct 15, 1910,Maoriland Worker,2/4/3</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> Raddle. To declare a sheep badly shorn and therefore not included in the shearer's tally. This practice was outlawed in the 1890s. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,97</i>)

>  And the new rousie instructed to raddle (mark with colour for the farmer's attention) the cut pizzle on the underside of a ram. <i>(Mar 1988,More Magazine,31</i>)

>  He asked accused to raddle the sheep's face so that he would know it again. <i>(Oct 24, 1914,Stratford Evening Post,3</i>)

>  If rams remain with the ewes for more than 4 weeks, they should be raddled on the brisket so that the late-lambing ewes can be drafted off. <i>(Mar 1955,NZ Journal of Agriculture,264</i>)

>  <b>MALLOCH</b> For 65 years he raddled lambs for that firm and even in his 80s he could be seen clutching the drafting gate at Waikaia Plains. <i>(1992,From the Tops,112</i>)

>  WoolPro strongly recommends to growers and shearers that they raddle flystruck sheep (on the head) as they go over the board, then treat them afterwards in the pens outside the shed. <i>(2000,Wool Grower,Spring 5</i>)

>  The scattered fleeces about the floor of the busy station shed, / Or the new-chum shears on the struggling ewe, black-tarred and raddled red ... <i>(Oct 23, 1909,Poverty Bay Herald,11</i>)

### raddle money
 
 <i>n.</i> [(shearers')]

>  <b>MILLS</b> This [South Island] shed is often said ... to have been paid for with `raddle money', a term that many of our younger shearers have never even heard. In those days ... a shearer had to shear cleanly, or have many of his sheep heavily marked in the count-out pens and excluded from his tally. Inevitably the boss stood to benefit from such a one-sided bargain, hence the term, raddle money. <i>(1960,Sheep-O,122</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a deduction made to a shearer's pay for sheep that are cut during shearing [such sheep being marked with raddle.]

>  <b>MILLS</b> In those days .. a shearer had to shear cleanly, or have many of his sheep heavily marked in the count-out pens and excluded from his tally. Inevitably the boss stood to benefit from such a one-sided bargain, hence the term, raddle money. <i>(1960,Sheep-O,122</i>)

### raddling
 
 <i>n.</i> [(obs.)]

>  I did not brand the bales, but marked them with raddle, and if there were two sorts I would mark them so. I have sometimes known mistakes between the raddling and branding; with a large lot and different men mistakes might easily arise. <i>(Jun 23, 1892,Otago Witness,17</i>)

>  Raddling.-An old-time shed practice, whereby the shed boss put a raddle-mark on any sheep in the counting-out pen not shorn to his satisfaction, and the shearer was not paid for it. Old hands often spoke of this practice. I think the great shearers' strike at the beginning of the 'nineties ended it. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> In 1886 there is mention of `raddling' (and not paying for) badly shorn sheep. I think by the 'nineties the shearers had managed to get this custom abolished. <i>(1946,Early Canterbury Runs,167</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  This is like going back to the good old raddling days. <i>(Dec 8, 1911,Maoriland Worker,14</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the marking of sheep with raddle stick for identification purposes.

>  <b>RIDDOLLS</b> Check pens may also be used for such jobs as raddling and dagging. <i>(1958,Farm Engineering,333</i>)

### radiata
 
 <i>n.</i> also called Monterey pine, pinus (radiata) the member of the pine family most commonly cultivated to provide timber and wood products in New Zealand. -->see PINE @n2, RADIATA PINE - [aka radiata pine; aka aka pinus]

>  ORNAMENTAL TREES .. Pinus Insignis, Maritima Canarienses, Radiata, Persica, &c ... <i>(Sep 3, 1885,Poverty Bay Herald,1</i>)

### radiata pine
 
 <i>n.</i> the Monterey pine, <i>Pinus radiata</i> [aka pinus; aka radiata]

>  Rows and rows of radiata pine seem to have put us off using pines in the garden setting, but don't judge all pines by the sombre dark green of the Monterey Pine (<i>Pinus radiata</i>). <i>(Feb 2001,Growing Today,58</i>)

>  Boring old radiata pine trees?... Radiata pine grows faster here faster here than anywhere else in the world, with an average age-to-harvest of under 30 years, which helps to make forestry New Zealand's third-biggest export industry. <i>(Mar 24, 2003,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  That radiata pine had become widely, though not universally frowned on is probably because of unfair comparison with its more feral cousin, P. contorta. <i>(Feb 10, 2004,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

### radio-sode
 
 <i>n.</i> short radio episode

>  A funky new 'radio-sode' targeting youth will air every day in a bid to encourage a new generation to speak Maori. <i>(2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.tetaurawhiri.govt.nz/maori/press_m/brownst.shtml]

>  The public campaign will focus on seven projects in its first six months: a game show for Maori Television; a radio drama serial for native speakers; a bilingual "radio-sode" (or short episodes) aimed at young people; an interactive website; a resource kit for new parents; phrase booklets; and a language event to promote Matariki, or Maori New Year. ... "That we now have funding to undertake specific projects - such as the quiz show for Maori Television and the funky new 'radio-sode' - is a sign of the positive commitment that the Government has made in its role to support the Maori language. ... Radio "Brown Street", a series of 30-second bilingual "radio-sodes" - or short episodes - produced by youth music station Mai FM in association with Te Taura Whiri. <i>(May 2005,Kokiri Paetae,6</i>)

### Rafferty('s) rules
 
 <i>n.</i> espec.(BE) RUN ON RAFFERTY RULES, of a game, a household, a raffle, etc, (be run) without a sense of order, method, correctness, etc; with no rules at all

>  A second special attraction will be an old-timers' football match, "under Rafferty's rules." ### rahui
 
 <i>n.</i> a prohibition or ban [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Before the stripping of coastal rocks and the placing of a rahui [harvesting ban] by local Maori to save the remaining shellfish on Aucklands west coast ... <i>(Dec 26, 2004,Sunday Magazine (Auckland),p18</i>)

>  The natives always treat a rahui with much respect, considering it an act of great dishonesty to catch eels, or any other fish, to hunt pigs, snare ducks or parrots, or in fact destroy in any way or [sic] game of whatever kind which it is erected to preserve. So strictly is this code carried out, that a short time ago - (and even now in some parts of the island) the infringement of a rahui would be considered quite a sufficient <i>casus belli</i> to give rise to a bloody war between two tribes. <i>(Nov 2, 1850,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

>  Tangaroa decreed that.. when necessary elders will place a rahui (ban) on taking seafood until Tangaroa is appeased. <i>(Dec 14, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  "Heremaia's things had been put into a house that was marked (<i>rahui</i>), and it was burnt; and if the General got them into a house, even though it was marked as sacred, they would be served in the same way." <i>(Mar 29, 1866,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,4</i>)

>  The fruit of the tutu is sometimes preserved for the owners by means of a rahui, which warned persons <i>(1910,TrNZI,42/477</i>)

>  ". . . . We can start by placing a rahui [ban] on smoking at our marae." <i>(Oct 20, 2005,NZ Herald,-</i>)

>  Claimants will dredge up evidence about historical rights to traverse various regions, to hunt and gather, and - most interestingly - to impose bans on use and passage to mark deaths or drownings or conserve resources (rahui), or preserve sacred areas (wahitapu). <i>(Aug 9, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),22</i>)

>  Oh I know rahui, I can look up and talk to people and when I go and film rahui I've got my script, the funders know what they want and they will tell me what the public wants. <i>(Nov 14, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C15</i>)

 <i>n.</i> one who follows a rail trail

### rail-bus
 
 <i>n.</i>

### rail-fence
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori fence made out of vertical rails; ?=a paling fence

>  <b>POLACK</b> We remember seeing a pig ... both of whose ears had been bored ... through which a stick was thrust, ... to prevent the animal from entering between the rail-fence. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers</i>) [NOTES: 1/194fn]

### rail-sitter
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an observer or non-participant in farming activities. (See [gate-leaner](g-go.md#gate-leaner))

### railer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HINTZ</b> The railers flocked to it. These are so-called fishermen whose greed for fish far outruns their angling skill. They are a pest likely to be encountered on many Taupo waters. They stand in a swift current, peel off the whole length of a dressed line and let it float down the stream, generally with 40 or 50 yds. of backing as well. Then they fish the [218] fly on the reel. It is illegal under the Taupo fishing regulations, but it is a practice which the rangers cannot easily detect or suppress. <i>(1964,Trout at Taupo,217</i>)

### railing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HINTZ</b> The owners of the lines, a husband and wife who had made themselves thoroughly objectionable during a week of unashamed railing, were roused to action. <i>(1964,Trout at Taupo,219</i>)

### railway cup
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  No more thick white cups and saucers with the distinctive Railway stripe though. <i>(Feb 1989,North & South,19</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> The tea came in a Railway cup. The rim was so thick that the drinker felt he had grown a thick lip. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,87</i>)

### rain river

Rain Rivers - a second class of rivers are those which have their origin in the outrunning spurs of the Southern Alps bounding the plains' appropriately named by the settlers rain rivers in contra-distinction to the larger ones, which are formed by the glaciers, and to which the term snow rivers has been applied. <i>(1864,Report on the Formation of the Canterbury Plains,30</i>)

### rainbird
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Like the Cuckoos in other parts of the world, they appear before rainy weather or coincident with it, and have thus come to be known in many widely different localities as `Rain-birds' and `Storm-birds'. In many parts of Otago and Canterbury they were called by the early settlers `Potato-birds', as they invariably came on the scene as the potato-planting was going on; similarly, in the North Island they got the name of `Kumara-birds'. <i>(1904,TrNZI,36/134</i>)

>  When the rain-bird sings in the teatree.. / I'll be riding down the track. <i>(1969,AUTC Song Book (3rd ed.),Song No. 36</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a name given various to birds in different parts of the world [AND any of several birds (esp. two cuckoos) 1827]: in NZ, the following (esp. and chiefly 1) are `rainbirds') (See [mohua](mob-mys.md#mohua))

>  <i>Observations on</i> Puffinus gavius</i> (Forst.), Rain-bird,</i> (Hakoakoa), <i>their Habits and Habitats. </i> <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/9</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> grey warbler, riroriro

>  <b>MOORE</b> Riroriro or Rainbird, the only true song bird in New Zealand. <i>(1937,Reminiscences</i>) [NOTES: (1860's). (Letter 3.)]

>  <b>HULME</b> I hear them singing in the hills, <i>kui kui kui, whiti whiti ora.</i> Rainbirds. I used to think, O hell, rain coming. <i>(1986,Te Kaihau,119</i>)

>  <i>rainbird n. NZ</i> = RIRORIRO. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,624</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> wet-bird, shearwater,? laughing jackass, hakoakoa

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 125. PUFFINUS GAVIUS. Forst. Rain-bird or Wet-bird. Shearwater. Laughing Jackass(?). Hakoakoa. <i>P. opisthomelas</i>. COUES. <i>P. assimilis</i>. HUTTON <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,45</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> PUFFINUS \{GAVIA. (FORSTER'S SHEARWATER.) ... <i>Native name</i>.- Pakahaa: `Rainbird' of the colonists. ... \{237\} They fly low but swiftly, and utter a note resembling the native name by which the bird is called, but somewhat prolonged, as <i>paka-ha-a - paka-ha-a</i>.\} <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,318,{1888 2nd edn 2/236-237} mainly 1888 edn ed.</i>)

>  <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/93</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek) [get quot.]]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Pakaha</i>, n. <i>Puffinis gavia, rain-bird. = hakoakoa. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,292</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> mottled petrel [Also called: Scaled petrel, Peale's petrel, rainbird, korure, titi.] (See [mottled petrel](mob-mys.md#mottled-petrel))

>  He states that the rain-bird is the only petrel seen in that locality. <i>(Feb 5, 1910,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  <b>MONCRIEFF</b> .... Like other Petrels calls at might before rain, hence name. Horrible scream. [check quot] <i>(1948,NZ Birds,56</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> MOTTLED PETREL <i>Pterodroma inexpectata</i> Other names: Korure, Rainbird. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,50</i>)

>  <i>Mottled Petrel Pterodroma inexpectata</i> (FORSTER, 1844) ... OTHER NAMES:<i> Scaled petrel, Peale's petrel, rainbird, korure, titi. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,76</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i> [ca1956 Said to be because its call foretells rain. According to R. McGueen the name and story come from the South-West of the South Island (R.Mason) -->[? 1968 or 1960] Mar 15 NZ List 6/3 [?cuckoo] <i>Screech!</i> went a rainbird, not really meaning it [check quot.]]

### rainbow cake
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  THE WAIMATE SHOW .. COOKERY SECTION. .. rainbow cake, Mrs Hunt 1, Mrs Philips 2 ... <i>(Feb 17, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>)

>  Rainbow Cake (by request) - One cupful of butter, two cupfuls of sugar, three cups of flour, five eggs, one cup of milk, half teaspoonful of soda, one teaspoonful of cream of tartar. <i>(Sep 27, 1894,Otago Witness,47</i>)

>  Rainbow Cake... Divide the mixture into three parts... As ordinary tins are not big enough, as the cake rises very high, cut the bottoms of three petrol tins, leaving them with sides about 3 ins. high. <i>(Mar 6, 1926,NZ Observer,22</i>)

>  The following is a tested recipe for rainbow cake:- Beat lb of butter to a cream; add lb of sugar and 3 eggs separately (not beaten), a cup of milk, lb of flour, 1 teaspoonful cream of tartar and a teaspoonful carbonate soda; essence to taste ... <i>(Dec 30, 1893,Bush Advocate,5</i>)

>  Rainbow Cake. Three-quarters lb. butter; ?lb. sugar; 1lb flour; 1 teaspoon cream tartar; teaspoon carbonate soda; 4 eggs; ? cup milk, cochineal, enough to color; 2 tablespoons Cadbury's chocolate; vanilla to taste. <i>(Sep 25, 1930,NZ Truth,20</i>)

>  ... rainbow cake <i>(1901,NZ Illustrated Magazine,IV/855 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FINLAY</b> RAINBOW CAKE... Divide mixture into 3 parts. Flavour 1st with teaspoon grated lemon rind., Flavour 2nd with teaspoon orange rind, and colour pink with cochineal... Add to the 3rd 1 dessertspoon cocoa... Bake about hour in 3 sponge sandwich tins... Fasten together with lemon honey, and ice all over with chocolate icing. <i>(1948,Dunedin City Gas Dept. Cookery Book,120</i>)

>  <b>BASHAM</b> Rainbow Cake... Divide into three lots. Make one pink; leave one plain; and add 1 dessertspoon cocoa or allspice to the next. Cook in three tins and stick together with jam. <i>(1968,Aunt Daisy Cookbook, The,99</i>)

>  "Rainbow cake" was a birthday treat; the chocolate, raspberry and vanilla sections all had to be separated and eaten slowly to be apreciated. <i>(Feb 12, 2005,Dominion Post,E3</i>)

### Rainbow Rage
 
 <i>n.</i> (Sport) an annual 106 km adventure bike race from St Arnaud to Hanmer, first held in 1996.

>  The annual Rainbow Rage event is a 106km mountain bike adventure ride that starts outside of St Arnaud, goes through some of the South island's awesome sub alpine high country and finishes in the Hanmer domain. <i>(2007,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.rainbowrage.co.nz]

>  Rainbow Rage. The 11th year of this very popular point-to-point event that travels the 106 km from St Arnaud to Hanmer via the gravel Rainbow Road. <i>(Nov 2005,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.vorb.org.nz/article-31310.html]

>  Sporting events included the annual mountain bike races the Rainbow Rage, with over 1000 competitors, and Molesworth Muster, with 560 participants. <i>(Jun 2007,Conservation Action,26</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a bout of vomiting, and its result - cf. technicolor yawn [ca1960 Auckland. (V. O' Sulliwan)]

### Rainbow Warrior
 
 <i>n.</i> the Greenpeace vessel scuttled in Auckland by French secret service operatives [e.g. in the phrase the <i>Rainbow Warrior</i> affair]

>  [Hdg] End of the 'Rainbow': the French perspective. After two bomb blasts sent the <i>Rainbow Warrior</i> to the bottom of Auckland Harbour on July 10 last year, the French press reported the condolences offered by the French ambassador in Wellington to the bereaved Greenpeace team. <i>(Feb 15, 1886,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  Reports from France suggest that one of the few things French President Mitterand and his new right-wing Prime Minister, Jacques Chirac, have in common is a strong desire to retrieve the <i>Rainbow Warrior</i> bombers. <i>(Apr 19, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  Later that year French secret-service agents bombed the <i>Rainbow Warrior</i> as it was preparing to sail from Auckland to Moruroa, killing a photgrapher on board. <i>(Jun 21, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),27</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  Around Peter Wilson's stable the kids have nicknamed him `Rainbow Warrior'. Punters know him as Harbour Road, the jumper who bombs out all the time. <i>(Jul 20, 1986,Sunday Star (Auckland),B16</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>RAINBOW FISH Suezichthys</i> sp. 2 (<i>Halichoeres</i> sp.) ... The rainbow fish is closely related to the crimson cleaner but is even more colourful ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,260,1984 ed.</i>)

Rainbows, such reinforcements as had not reached the front when Armistice was signed, from their arrival after the storm; <i>(1935,Slang Today,286</i>) [NOTES: 1935 Mitchell and Strong in Partridge Slang Today 286]

### raindrops payment
 
 <i>n.</i> [(Unionists')]

### rainforest
 
 <i>n.</i> woodland in a mild climate with heavy rainfall, typically having many tree varieties but dominated by one, e.g. in New Zealand often by podocarps. Also called temperate rain forest.

 <i>n.</i> flagstaff

>  <b>MANING</b> <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,225</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (Rural) mustering stick [also known as a nibby, hillpole or hillstick]. [ORIGIN: Maori]

 <i>n.</i> rod as learning tool [ORIGIN: Maori, (from the net) Te Ataarangi is an immersion style of learning with rakau (rods). It focuses on developing oral fluency in te reo Maori, including tikanga Maori, ...]

### rakau momori
 
 <i>n.</i> a Moriori tree carving

>  Just 50 years ago 1145 dendroglyphs, known to the Moriori as rakau momori, had been identified in the island's forests - at the last official count in 1998 there were just 185. <i>(Oct 21, 2000,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  The rakau momori was shipped back to the Chathams in time for the opening of the first-ever Moriori marae .. DOC is working with Moriori to identify future management needs for around 180 other internationally significant rakau momori. <i>(Jul 2005,Conservation Action,53</i>)

>  [caption] Jack Fry, a wood conservator, with rakau momori that he restored. <i>(Jun 23, 2005,Footnotes,15</i>)

>  Every known rakau momori was re-surveyed by a team of DOC staff last year and this revealed a continual gradual decline in overall tree health .. so management options are being considered in consultation with Hokotehi Moriori Trust. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 6</i>)

 <i>v.</i>

 <i>n.</i> [(?mining)] (See [cf. race noun hom.2 in DNZE](../R#race-noun-hom.2-in-dnze))

>  <b>EVANS</b> [The coal] was then taken to a focal point in the mine and a rake of trucks was formed. A `rake' consisted of, on the average, from ten to twelve trucks. <i>(1962,Waikaka Saga,78</i>)

>  <b>McARA</b> Rake: `The rake' referred to the forty or so trucks of ore hauled constantly through Waihi by steam locomotives to the battery at Waikino. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),331</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to snow-rake sheep.
