### rakkoo
 
 <i>v.</i> (See [rakau](ra-red.md#rakau))

### rakkyo
 
 <i>n.</i> a small Japanese onion

>  A new vegetable said to be ideal for stir-frys, curries and salads is ready for planting and could be in the shops in time for Christmas dinner. It is a small onion, described by Crop and Food Research scientists who developed it as a cross between shallots and chives. Its taste is mild, somewhere between garlic and onion, and it is crisp in texture. Known in Japan as rakkyo and used in a popular pickle called tsukemono,it was brought to New Zealand in 1997 to be tested as a commercial vegetable. <i>(May 25, 2002,Dominion,7</i>)

### rallykaboo
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Then he discovered that the he had a fearful thirst on him (This invariably follows a champagne rallykaboo) and he looked in vain for an open wet goods emporium. <i>(Oct 13, 1906,NZ Truth,69/1/5</i>)

### ram
 
 <i>n.</i> a pump

>  <b>HARPER</b> A ram pumped the water to a small reservoir near the house, ... <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,36</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> With the faint distant call of the creek and the Ram ticking, I knew writing had collected me, for keeps. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,105</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [ca1949]

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> `That dirty old man of Erina's!' `That fellow, the classer, he the Number One Ram.' cf WF ram bam 1943; P v. only `coit'. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,22</i>)

>  Among young people greetings like `Hophead', `Ram', `Burglar', `Sheik', `Stopout', are accepted as flattery: `burglar' I heard only in the army. <i>(Sep 1952,Landfall,208</i>) [NOTES: (Pearson `Fretful Sleepers')]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> male homosexuality; or perhaps its more bestial station objects -->cf. the Marlborough verse: `When I think of all the rams and the wether(s) I've been through/ There'll never be another you.' cf SHEPHERD'S GRUMMET

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> On the wool stations women were hard to find so that consolations were homosexuality (known in Canterbury as 'ram') and grog. <i>(1980,Southern Gentry,52</i>)

### ram block
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a large fenced area on a station where rams are kept, with little attention.

>  <b>WHEELER</b> Lake Hawea's history as a separate station is relatively short. It was formerly a ram block on the gigantic Morven Hills run of Big Jock McLean. <i>(1971,Historic Sheep Stations SI II,32</i>)

### ram fair
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PERRY</b> The rams are usually shorn early, especially those intended for the ram fair, so that they can have a fair growth of wool on them. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,83</i>)

>  <b>AYSON</b> About this time I went to the Gore Ram Fair where I saw a pen of rams which ... were Corriedales. <i>(1937,Pioneering in Otago,102</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> My next job was to attend a `ram fair' and buy some rams ...These were put in the best paddock to `flush them up' before putting them out with the ewes, ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,148</i>)

### ram muster
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the gathering in of rams for inspection.

>  <b>KERR</b> Bill, Arthur's stuck for the ram muster. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,15</i>)

### ram paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a field or paddock on a farm where rams are grazed, separately from the ewe flocks or a viewing paddock for ram buyers.

>  The improvements, which are very complete and in thorough good order, consist of stone dwelling-house, stone woolshed, men's hut, shearers' hut, barn, stable, sheepyard, stockyard, outhouses, grass-paddock, oat-paddock, horse-paddock, and ram-paddock, forming the home station. <i>(Jan 17, 1885,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  In the ram paddock, on taking final delivery, the defendant said that he was between 300 and 400 short. <i>(Mar 21, 1868,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  26 Australian Merino Rams, bred by Messrs. Buten and M'Masters, imported to Napier last year, and have since been running in Ram paddocks. <i>(Jan 21, 1870,Hawke's Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  The Hangaroa dogs trials, which were held in the ram paddock of "Strathlane" on Saturday last, proved quite successful. <i>(May 20, 1919,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  This is the ram paddock. ... I don't go in for fat lambs right through. Like to breed my own ewe-hoggets. <i>(1953,Breakfast at Six,17</i>)

>  T.C.K. and Hughie fencing in ram paddock at Kaikanui. <i>(Jan 4, 1879,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The traditional story has always been that they used Hallelujah Flat as a ram paddock. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,59</i>)

### ram power
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the ability of a ram to inseminate ewes.

>  Mulvaney believes 'ram power' - the ability of a ram to inseminate a ewe and with high potency sperm - is a very important factor for successful mating performance, but says rams are often the most underdone animals on the farm. <i>(2002,Wool Grower,24</i>)

### ram-tipping
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the turning of rams on to their backs, for inspection or shearing.

>  They did all that fencepost digging, trailer-backing and ram-tipping stuff, plus heaps more. <i>(Nov 9, 2002,Dominion,A12</i>)

### rama
 
 <i>n.</i> rum [ORIGIN: Maori, transliteration]

>  "Rum" (Rama) and "Brandy" (Parani) are merely "tots" of humanity, the names of children, "reincarnations" of bottles perhaps held in high esteem by the parents. <i>(Jun 9, 1906,Evening Post,6</i>)

### ramarama
 
 <i>n.</i> a shrub or small tree, Lophomyrtus bullata, of coastal and lowland forest, having shiny blistered leaves (more common in the North Island). [ORIGIN: Maori, [1919 July 29] Ver.(WB). = Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W.Best, Otaki (And) <i>Myrtus bullata</i> rum-a-rum]

>  R|ama r|ama; A shrub so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,LEE & KENDALL,99 ed.</i>)

>  The ramiram for ramming in the posts in fencing [in Taranaki] <i>(Jan 15, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/97</i>)

>  Other edible berries include..ramarama (Lophomyrtus bullata), "very much like guavas" when fully ripe... <i>(Apr 30, 2006,Dominion Post,E12</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i>Myrtus bullata</i>, Banks et Sol. ... Nat. name, `Rama-Rama', <i>Cunn.</i> ... An erect shrub or small tree, 10-15 feet. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 71</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Ramarama, <i>Col[enso]. Myrtus bullatus. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  Of our shrubs and smaller timber trees, several are of strikingly beautiful growth ... (e.g.) the Houhere, <i>Hoheria populnea </i> ...; the Horopito <i> Drimys axillaris </i> ; the Manuka-rau-riki, <i> Leptospermum ericoides </i> ; the Kohuhu, <i> Pittosporum tenuifolium </i> ; the Kowhai, especially the small-leaved mountain variety <i> Sophora tetraptera </i> var.: <i> grandiflora </i> ; the Koromiko-taranga, <i> Veronica,</i> several species; the Mairehau, <i> Phebalium nudum </i> ; the Toro, <i> Persoonia Toro </i> ; the Pukapuka, <i> Brachyglottis repanda </i> ; the Northern Maire, <i> Santalum Cunninghamii </i> ; the Tawari,<i> Ixerba brexioides </i> ; the Tipau, <i> Myrsine Urvillei </i> and <i> M. salicina </i> ; the Tangeao,<i> Tetranthera calicaris </i> ; the Ramarama, <i> Myrtus bullata </i> ; the Ti, <i> Cordyline australis </i> ; the Kahikomako, <i> Pennantia corymbosa </i> ; the Pate, <i> Schefflera digitata </i> ; the Horoeka, <i> Panax crassifolia </i> : and, on the sea-coast, ... the <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/5</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  Karo, <i> Pittosporum crassifolium </i> ; and the truly evergreen Ngaio, <i> Myoporum laetum </i> ... <i>Ibid.</i> -1/III/31 the curious red fruit of the Titoki, or Titongi, (<i> Alectryon excelsum </i> ); the fruit of the Tutu-papa (<i> Coriaria thymifolia </i> ); of the New Zealand Bramble, Tataramoa, (<i> Rubus australis </i> ); of two of the New Zealand Myrtles, the Ramarama (<i> Myrtus bullata </i> ), and the Rohutu (<i> M. pedunculata </i> ); of several species of <i> Coprosma </i> , - particlularly the Karamu (<i> C. lucida </i> ), and <i> C. robusta </i> ), of the Papaauma (<i> C. grandifolia </i> ), and of the two littoral speciedepressa </i> ); ... and of the Kareao, or Pirita, (<i> Rhipogonum parviflorum </i> ). s, Taupata (<i> C. retusa </i> ), and Tataraheke (<i> C. acerosa </i> ); of the Koropuku (<i> Gaultheria <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/5 (cont.)</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Rama-rama (<i>Myrtus bullata</i>) has a good hard wood, but is small. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/196</i>)

>  [Native name] Ramarama .. [Settlers' name] Ramarama .. [Family] Myrtaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  MYRTUS BULLATA. (Banks & Sol.) ... The `RAMA-RAMA'. This singular leaved species is almost confined to the Northern Island; where it ... varies from a small upright shrub, to a tree twelve to fifteen feet in height, ..<i>Ibid.</i> 178 Ramarama [Maori name] .. Ramarama [Settlers' name] .. Myrtus bullata .. Shrub <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,166</i>)

>  Ramarama .. Myrtus bullata .. Timber reddish brown, hard, tough, and often prettily figured; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,13</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Rama-rama</i>..Maori name for a New Zealand shrub, <i>Myrtus bullata</i>, Banks and Sol. The name is used in the North Island. It is often corrupted into <i>Grama. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,379</i>)

>  Rai.ma-rai.ma (ra.i.m&adot.-ra.i.m&adot.; <i>colloq</i>.r&ubreve.mi.$a-r&ubreve.mi.$a), n. [Maori.] (<i>Bot.</i>) A New Zealand myrtaceous tree <i>Myrtus bullatus). [Local, N. Z., & obsoles.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

>  <b>CHBEESEMAN</b> <i>M[yrtus] bullata, Sol. ... Ramarama. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,168</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Myrtus bullata [(The Ramarama)]</i> ... A shrub or small tree.. Maori name Ramarama. ... rare in South [Island]. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,288,1940, p. 305. ed.</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The limestone range east of the lake [Tutira] at one time grew ... dense fern, high tutu, koromiko, and a considerable area of `whitey-wood' bush, kowhai, fuchsia, rama rama, ngaio, kaiwhiria, etc., etc., ... . <i>(1927,Birds of New Zealand,2</i>)

>  <i>ramarama</i>, a tree (Myrtus bullata), often corrupted to rummyrum. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i> rummy-rum</i>: native running plant with small leaf; a bundle is springy when cut and dried: some call it micky-mick. [qv] <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,279</i>) [NOTES: (Waikato)]

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  ~ n. New Zealand shrub [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  <i>rama-rama</i>... n. N.Z. shrub [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSON</b> During the warm months there was a long season for koninis from the kotukutuku trees (native Fuchsia) then ... the ramarama berries (Myrtaceae) were ripe. <i>(1970,Life's Vagaries,16</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Lophomyrtus bullata</i> (A. Cunn.) Burr. Common name: <i>Native myrtle</i> Maori name: <i>Ramarama </i>... It was known as <i>Myrtus bullata</i> Sol. ex A. Cunn. <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,72</i>)

>  <i>ramarama n</i>. NZ variety of myrtle. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,625</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  612 Ramarama slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,28</i>)

>  <b>Crowe</b> Ramarama is also known to contain bullatenone, a derivative of furfural known to have antiseptic properties. <i>(2009,Which Native Forest Plant?,50</i>)

>  There are even a few ramarama, small shrubs with trademark "bubbles" on their olive and red upper leaf surface, and a carpet of parataniwha. <i>(Dec 26, 2005,Dominion Post,B2</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Myrtus Ralphii</i>

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>)

### Rameka diorite
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. definition

>  Again the colour and the symbol for the talc-serpentinite and chlorite schist (mos) of the Onekaka Schist are about the same as those for the Rameka Diorite (rr). <i>(Jun 1972,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,32/27</i>)

### rammer
 
 <i>n.</i> a tool used to consolidate earth around a post [(fencing)]

>  The ramiram for ramming in the posts in fencing [in Taranaki]] <i>(Jan 15, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/97</i>)

>  (Annual Contracts, Col.Secretary's Office) -->a blasting tool [heading] TOOLS FOR ROAD PARTIES ... Blasting Tools/ Crowbars ......per lb./ Jumpers ......per lb./ Rammers .....per lb./ Spoons and Needles ......per lb....Saws/ Hand .....per foot./ Cross Cut .....per foot./ Pit ......per foot. <i>(Dec 21, 1852,NZ Government Gazette,V/31/193</i>)

>  Across the paddocks a man in a black singlet was planting a post, steadying it with one hand while he tamped the earth around it with short even strokes from a rammer. <i>(1967,NZ Short Stories III,68</i>) [NOTES: Middleton `Killers']

>  Rammer: Tool used to consolidate earth around a post. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary</i>)

### ramp
 
 <i>v.</i> to leave, clear out [(criminals')]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> I was down there with Old Ashmore's lot, doing the trampoline, and so on; but things was very quizby. Everything went crooked somehow, and the gorger had a hawful cramp in his kick. Well, ramp was the word, and vamose we did, the lot on us; some here, some there. I was reg'lar beached, and I tell you when a cove 'as to spout his smish it's about time to look out. However, I got a job at a livery stable, but although that kep' me in grub, and I slep' in a spare stall, the billet was very multy, and I kep' on the kivvy for something better. [fn] 1. [trampoline] Vaulter. 2. [quizby] Bad. 3. [crooked] Wrong. 4. [cramp in his kick] Emptiness in his pockets. 5. [ramp] Clear out. 6. [vamose] Go. 7. [beached] Hard up. 8. [smish] Shirt. 9. [multy] No good. 10. [kivvy] Look out (qui vive.) <i>(1879,Spangles and Sawdust,5</i>)

>  A pickpocket ... `ramped' the captain, getting away with 7/10 <i>(Aug 17, 1905,NZ Truth,9/7/8</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a style of silage stack.

>  Some interesting information resulted from the survey of 49 wedges, buns and ramps made with the buckrake. <i>(Jan 15, 1953,NZ Journal of Agriculture,33</i>)

>  If above-ground silage is made, buns appear to have some advantage over ramps on flat ground ... <i>(Jan 15, 1953,NZ Journal of Agriculture,38</i>)

### ramping
 
 <i>n.</i> [as ODE verb ramp: rush about uncontrollably?]

>  Cath McWhirter's accent and ramping as Sharlene might be reined in more once established. <i>(Nov 7, 2000,Dominion,27</i>)

### ramshorn shell
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Spirula spirula</i>

>  <b>POWELL</b> RAMS HORN SHELL (<i>Spirula spirula</i>). This is the white openly coiled shell about an inch long, which washes ashore in great numbers on our West Coast beaches. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,32</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Ramshorn shells, the spirula, the internal shells of a small squid and coiled like a watchspring but papery-looking, lie ... among the `violets' [sc sea-snails]. <i>(1970,Stewart Islanders,186</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> RAMSHORN: see Squid. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,197</i>)

### ranch
 
 <i>v.</i> to feed (possums) in close confinement [applied (?as an up-market alternative to farm) to the business of possum farming, salmon farming]

>  At the possum farm, animals are caught live and `ranched': fed in close confinement for three months prior to killing...Ranch the wild animal for three months and the fur becomes a much better commodity, says Keber. <i>(Feb 6, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),17</i>)

### ranchslider
 
 <i>n.</i> sliding glass door

>  It hasn't got glass ranch sliders yet, so when the weather is bad, the roller doors are down and it looks closed. <i>(Jul 17, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  ranchslider NZ glazed sliding door opening onto outside terrace etc. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,626</i>)

>  <b>MANTELL</b> Smart looked once at Danny, remote on his humpty by the ranchsliders then he pulled a rocker closer to his wife's wing chair .. <i>(1980,Murder or Three, A,169</i>)

>  There are ... quite a few cases where there is a distinct New Zealand word - cutty grass, freezing works, graunch, ranch-slider, pie-cart, sheepmeat and jandals ((P) for proprietary name); no etymology is given for the last-named; I am told it is a portmanteau word from Japanese sandals; <i>(Apr 26, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>) [NOTES: (Forrest Scott)]

>  Tidy 3-bedroom brick home, private section. Ranchsliders from lounge, double garage plus games room..he;FLY-SCREEN <i>(Apr 22, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>) [NOTES: 1989 Mar 23 Property Press in 1989 Apr 22 Listener 5]

>  ... jewellery worth about $120 was taken when someone entered the kitchen of a house through a fly-screen. <i>(Apr 22, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>) [NOTES: 1989 Feb 25 Gisborne herald in 1989 Apr 22 Listener 5]

>  Our boxy green bach with uncool aluminium ranch sliders on a cliff outside Opononi is the place my husband Geoff and I are truly happy. <i>(Mar 2003,North & South,122</i>)

>  <b>Owen Marshall</b> They had ranchslider doors that opened onto a broad concrete patio... <i>(2005,Watch of Gryphons,172</i>)

>  Melanoma could have been an island off the coast of Sumatra for all the difference it made to the sun-crazed New Zealanders of legend, with their golden beaches and sandflies, baches and ranchsliders. <i>(Jan 5, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),95</i>)

### random
 
 <i>adj.</i> unacceptable, crazy? [international E?]

>  "It's really common for young guys to drive past and yell really random stuff." <i>(Sep 15, 2005,Dominion Post,A8</i>)

>  Another was proud of how having children meant he was always on top of kidspeak, like "munted" (for broken), "random" (for crazy), and "gay" (for unfashionable). <i>(Nov 26, 2005,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### Ranfurly Shield
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FRASER</b> All Blacks Cooke, Mill, Johnson and Grenside played for the "Magpies" who retained the Shield this year [sc. 1926]. <i>(1952,Ungrateful People,1952</i>) [NOTES: Pagination from 1985 edition]

>  <b>McMENAMIN</b> Many people have tried to explain what Makes Ranfurly Shield rugby so special. <i>(1986,Glory Days,12</i>)

>  Hawke's Bay had lost the shield and that after getting Cookie and half the All Blacks to go and live in the capital of the province of the wool kings... Hawke's Bay had lost the Ranfurly Shield the only way they could lose it - by pinching. <i>(Jun 9, 1926,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  The little country was growing up. Its footballers since 1902 had been playing for something called the Ranfurly Shield, donated by Governor-General Ranfurly. <i>(Dec 5, 1999,Sunday Star Times,C7</i>)

>  AUCKLAND VERSUS WELLINGTON SHIELD GAME DRAWN. <i>(Aug 22, 1910,Hawera & Normanby Star,5</i>) [NOTES: Headline]

>  THE RANFURLY SHIELD. The Ranfurly Challenge Shield presented by his Excellency the Governor to the New Zealand Rugby Union, was formally handed by his Excellency to Mr. A. C. Norris, Hon. Secretary of the Union, at Government House yesterday afternoon. <i>(Jun 19, 1902,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  <b>GLOVER</b> ... no one was to know that the Ranfurly Shield would become the most prized possession of the province. The village now feels the cynosure of all eyes ..., and though we have no players in the Shield team as yet, I appear to have belittled Rugby ... <i>(1963,Bedside Book,110</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> . they were talking about who was going to win the Ranfurly Shield ... <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,44</i>)

>  Ranfurly Shield premier trophy in NZ Rugby football. [fifth Earl of Ranfurly, Governor of NZ 1897-1904] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,626</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 403]]

>  Still, I could see they wanted someone outside themselves to believe in rainbows and trout and pots of gold and that Southland would win the Ranfurly Shield ... and Dad would win on his ticket to Tattersalls and take us all home to Galway where he'd come from. <i>(Aug 29, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>) [NOTES: (Davin)]

>  But this Saturday will be the first time he has ever called a Ranfurly Shield challenge. <i>(Sep 26, 1987,Dominion,2</i>)

>  The decision of the Taranaki Rugby Union not to play Wellington for the Ranfurly Shield this season has not been favourably received... If Taranaki can lift the Shield, it will mean matches next season that we will otherwise not get. <i>(Oct 17, 1904,NZ Observer,10</i>)

>  Wellington will meet our reps. in Auckland in a special Ranfurly Shield match. <i>(May 17, 1913,NZ Observer,10</i>)

>  Three Ranfurly Shield matches between Hawke's Bay and Canterbury were played in the 1923-28 period. <i>(Jun 11, 1940,Evening Post,5</i>)

>  To be candid, the Ranfurly Shield has no great bearing on the selection of New Zealand teams. <i>(Jun 5, 1935,NZ Free Lance,51</i>)

>  The same season, Auckland regained the shield. <i>(Jun 5, 1935,NZ Free Lance,51</i>)

>  Ideal weather prevailed for the Bay of Plenty Challenge match against Hawke's Bay for the Ranfurly Shield. <i>(Sep 7, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,54</i>)

>  Southland made Rugby history when it won back the Ranfurly Shield from Otago in the same season in which it lost the trophy. <i>(Sep 21, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,65</i>)

### rangatahi
 
 <i>n.</i> [[Ma. <b>rangatahi</b> modern youth] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 211.]

>  But personally I like mixing with rangatahi like those at the secondary school, national waka ama competitions in Rotorua or the Whakawhanaunga hui hosted by EIT in Hawke's Bay last month. <i>(May 2005,Kokiri Paetae,32</i>)

>  Shew concludes, wrongly, that Te Kooti was a later name of the prophet. It was mentioned in 1852, when he was merely a troublesome rangatahi ("wild boy"), and was, as he himself said, his baptismal name. <i>(Apr 2, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),109</i>)

>  It has inspired so many rangatahi out there in Aotearoa. <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,8,Autumn ed.</i>)

>  We get a bad rap when we go into town, and our rangatahi are going through that still, being picked up for nothing. <i>(May 20, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

>  This book .. tells "the story of the rangatahi, modern Maori youth living in the city". <i>(Oct 30, 1993,Listener (New Zealand Listener),47</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [rangatira](ra-red.md#rangatira))

### rangatera
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [rangatira](ra-red.md#rangatira))

### rangatira
 
 <i>adj.</i> [mostly adj]

>  He asserts that the rangatira company have a promise of a loan, whereas the diggers could not get the same. <i>(Jun 11, 1859,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  The grace and gentle bashfulness of this rangatira damsel were in strong contrast with the coarse and rude appearance of the half-clad slaves .. <i>(1847,Savage Life,2/85</i>)

>  Te Puea says that the family are convinced that Rugby is the rangatira code, and the one on which the elect of all the tribes can meet in friendly contest. A minor victory of the kotahitanga movement. <i>(Aug 24, 1935,Na To Hoa Aroha,3/200</i>) [NOTES: Ngata 1935 Aug 24 in 1988 Na To Hoa Aroha (Ngata-Buck Correspondence) 3/200]

>  Some aptitude for carving was expected of all men of rangatira status, .. <i>(1984,Te Maori,155</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a chief or noble person [ORIGIN: Maori, Commonly used by English speakers for people of public or private importance or mana]

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> I knew she was a rangatira's daughter when Te Rauparaha took her with others prisoner to Kapiti. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,216</i>)

>  He was explaining how the children of the rangatira around here were carted off to Tasmania and seriously sexually abused. <i>(Aug 14, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),33</i>)

>  BIG RANGITIRA EX-MINISTER of the crown and a champion of his people as well as their guide and mentor, Dr. Peter Buck (Te Rangihiroa) has returned to New Zealand for a brief season. <i>(Mar 27, 1930,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  An old rangatira was farewelling the young warriors, explained Mr. Bennett, and in doing so remarked that he had heard the Germans were in sore straits for food. <i>(Dec 17, 1914,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  <b>COLLINS</b> Etang-a-te[acute]da Eti-ket-ti-ca, means a principal chief, or a man in very great authority. ... Etanga-roa, or E-ta-hon-ga, is a priest; whose authority in many cases is equal, and in some superior to the Etiketica... `Etan-ga-teda Epodi, a subordinate chief or gentleman.' `Ta-ha-ne Emo-ki, a labouring man.' <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,344</i>)

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> Rungateeda. .... A friend <i>(1807,Some Account of NZ,77</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> Duaterra immediately recognised [the chief], and ... behaved to him with particular respect, touching noses with him, the usual mode of salutation in this part of the world, when any honour is to be paid, and which he did not do with the other chief. The latter he contemptuously described as an ittee ittee rungateeda, or a chief possessing very little power. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/227 He assisted us ... but reflecting at intervals on the rank he held among his countrymen, which was that of a rungateeda, or gentleman, he would several times shake his head, and cry out against this indignity, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/292 All the males belonging to the family of the chief are styled rungateedas, and these have generally servants of their own to wait upon them. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/339 A gentleman Rungateeda <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/91</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> ...saying, `that if it were the child of a rungateeda, or gentleman, it should be laid in tabbooed ground, and every funeral rite paid to it; but that a cookee could not be allowed even the decency of burial, ... <i>(Apr 26, 1820,Journal,87,1957 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> R|anga t|ira; A gentleman or lady. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,200</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> Pomarre[acute] .. .. Rungatyeeda no te Kaaingha. E Wharre[acute] .. .. Whaheene[acute] no Pomarre[acute]. Poaka .. .. Tamyte[acute] no Pomarre[acute]. ... E Po .. .. Koeteedo no Pomarre Whaheene no Tenana. <i>(1820,Journal,27,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEGG</b> [| = macron precedes] ... giving them to understand Capt K was a Rangata|ra Nue (great Chief) ... <i>(1979,World of John Boultbee, The,196</i>) [NOTES: 1827 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 196]

>  [They] exaggerated ... amongst their own countrymen, giving them to understand Capt.K. was a Rangatira Nue (great Chief), ... <i>Ibid.</i> 112a chief - rangata'ra [rangatira] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,79,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: >c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 112]

>  <b>McNAB</b> They are divided into two classes, viz., ranghateeroos (or chiefs) and their relations of different degrees of consanguinity, and cookees (slaves), who are nearly black, and much shorter, and appear a different race of people. <i>(1908,Historical Records,1/696</i>) [NOTES: 1829 Mar 2 Atkins's Narrative in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/696]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> You may see the Cookee [kuki] or Slave / doing that, and throwing it to the Chief or Rangitara [rangatira] or his Wife. [fn] Merely a Maori form of the English `cook'. Polack gives this explanation ... [NZ 2/110-111]. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),37</i>)

>  <b>WAITE</b> `I, Toawick, are now become Rangatera or Chief of these Southern territories', and he affixed his moko (the tattoo marks on his face) to the deed. <i>(1940,Port Molyneux,37</i>) [NOTES: 1835 Dec 31 Tuhawaiki's confirmation in 1940 Waite Port Molyneux 37]

>  <b>McNAB</b> To all whom it may concern be it known that I Toawick [Tuhawaiki] are now become Rangatera or Chief of these Southern Territories ... Witnesses ... George Moss, Mowry, x his mark. Tomarama, Mowry x his mark. <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days,96</i>) [NOTES: 1835 Dec 31 Deed in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 96]

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> It appears that if two tribes are at war, and the chief of most consequence in one tribe were to marry the only daughter of the corresponding `rangitera' of the other, that would not do anything towards making peace between the two tribes; ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,33,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SARAH GREENWOOD</b> He is so much liked by all [the natives] especially by the rangatiras or gentlemen. <i>(Nov 10, 1844,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Nov 10 Sarah Greenwood Letter in 1960 Drummond Married Gone NZ 79]

>  <b>MANING</b> ... all these qualifications combined made him unquestionably `a great rangatira', and, as he had never been defeated, his mana was in full force. <i>Ibid.</i> (viii) 120 The fighting men and petty chiefs, and everyone indeed who could by any means claim the title of rangatira - which in the sense now use it means gentlemen - were ... possessed of this mysterious quality. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(vi) 90</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> `No, no!' exclaimed the native vehemently. `Martinera is a raugaturah[chk sic =rang-](chief), he will not betray Okinga; see, there is the pah.' <i>(1864,Martin Tobin,1/67</i>)

>  I then called out to them not to keep a rangitira bound with ropes but to put the handcuffs on me ... <i>(Jul 4, 1868,Chudleigh Diary,224,1950 ed.</i>)

>  ... the two Pakehas who had come to live with them were Rangatiras. <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk3/2/141</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> Accompanied by a score or so of the rangatira of his hapu, the ariki rode over to the young settler's place. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/175</i>)

>  Te Kooti is at Puketapu with many Rangatiras. <i>(Dec 21, 1893,Otago Witness,11</i>) [NOTES: (Morris)]

>  Rangatira, n. Maori word for a chief, male or female; a master or mistress (Williams); therefore an aristocrat, a person of the gentle class, distinguished from a tau-rikarika, a nobody, a slave. <i>(1898,Austral English,379</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,216</i>)

>  ~ n. a chief, male or female; an aristocrat; a person of rank; an employer, the master or mistress. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  rangatira, a chief, male or female: rangatiratanga, evidence of breeding and greatness <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  rangatira: chief. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> As Inspector of native schools, I was much attracted by the old type of Maori rangatira, both men and women ... <i>(1948,Taina,vii</i>)

>  The Maori word for a chief or the members of a chief or the members of a chief's family, an aristocrat. (Pronounce the ng as in `hanger'.) <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),329</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> When he died he was buried in the little Maori Cemetery on Taylor's Hill, where Tu Taylor, and other Maori Rangatiras are also interned; which was certainly something of an honour for a pakeha. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,83</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Within each tribe the population was further divided into slaves, who were mostly prisoners of war, tutua or commoners, and the numerous class of chiefs, rangatira. At the head of the tribe there was a paramount chief, the ariki. <i>Ibid..<i> 302 [Maori glossary] rangatira Chief <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,20</i>)

>  <b>FRANCES</b> He was a rangatira and, above a chief, he was a tohunga pouwhiro, high priest and teacher ... <i>(1964,Johnny Rapana,2</i>)

>  ~ n. Maori Chief, an aristocrat [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  rangatira ... n. Maori Chief, an aristocrat [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  rangatira n. N.Z. a Maori chief, of either sex. [-->[1983 Otago] - <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,946</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 626; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 403]]

>  <b>BELICH</b> rangatira chief <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> The chief also, having made some enquiries ... - such as, whether I was a rangatira, if I had plenty of taonga (goods) on board, ... gave me a most sincere welcome. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(i)6</i>)

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> He [a Maori] also said that one of the Militia officers that was killed here was a very brave Rangatira as when lieing mortally wounded a Maori struck him on the back of the head with a Tomahawk and was leaving him for dead when he saw the wounded man turn on his back ... <i>(Nov 18, 1863,Journal</i>)

>  <b>GUSTAFSON</b> Maui our Ancestor fished up this Aotearoa of ours from the dark waters of Kiwa. Today it is our Rangatira Savage who is fishing up this Maoriland of ours from the gloom and slime of depression .. <i>(1986,Cradle to Grave,[facing 232</i>) [NOTES: >1938 Labour pamphlet for Northern Maori in 1986 Gustafson Cradle to Grave]

>  <b>CRAIK</b> ... Rungateedas, or, as it has been more recently written, Rangatiras, which appears to answer nearly to the English term gentry. <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,204</i>)

>  Rai.nga-tii.ra (ra.i.ng&adot.-t&emacr.i.r&adot.), n. [Maori.] A Maori chief; a magistrate; lees exactly, a leading citizen. [New Zealand] <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2029</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> rangatira: chief, male or female; well-born noble. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,194</i>)

>  The natives shrewdly observed that he was the only Rangatira, or gentleman, among them and that the rest were only cookees or slaves. <i>(1839,New Zealand in 1839,30</i>)

>  They all jabbered and cut capers when they knew the taurikarike pakeha had mated up with the daughter of such a rangatira. <i>(1900,Auckland Weekly News,Xmas No. / 17</i>)

>  The "rangatira" handed round the beer in a bucket, the source of supply being an 18-gallon keg... The [Maori] witness explained that the Maori who ordered the beer and called the meeting was the "rangatira" for the occasion... On Christmas Day at the .. kainga, everyone was more or less a "rangatira." <i>(Mar 4, 1916,Wairarapa Daily Times,4</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [of status, from Maori to general NZ use]

>  Mr SEDDON is not, we beg to say, the chief rangatira of the pakehas. So long as the Queen is represented by a Governor, that Governor will be our chief rangatira, save only under the pen of a "Ministerial journal." <i>(Apr 12, 1894,Otago Witness,3</i>)

>  <b>F. MATHEW</b> I first visited a native Pah ... and I made myself known to them as a Rangatira or chief in the service of Her Majesty. <i>(Mar 14, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Mar 14 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 80]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> I took care to tell them that the rangatira, or `chief' missionaries, would come out with the settlers. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/173</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Though roughly drest and travel-stained,/ A `Rangatira' - of the sort/ Who paid for all attentions shown:/ [fn] `Chief or Gentleman'. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,ii/289</i>)

>  rangatira <i>(Oct 19, 1918,Featherston Camp Weekly,17</i>) [NOTES: (Les Cleveland)]

>  <b>BAKER</b> rangatira, a leading citizen, a magistrate (taken directly from he Maori), in very early use. <i>(1941,NZ Slang,59</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include; ... rangatira: leading citizen <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  <b>BACON</b> rangatira-A chief; a leader amon men. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,18</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> The big rangatira standing by him's the meat inspector. <i>(1964,Watersiders,36</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Remember, I'm a chief, Ken. Too right I am.' `Is that dinkum? Dinkum? You're a - rangatira?' `Too blimmin right I am. ...' <i>(1965,Outcasts,91</i>)

>  <b>BOYD</b> A buggy became the status symbol of a rangatira. <i>(1984,City of the Plains,10</i>)

### rangatira girl
 
 <i>n.</i> a high-born young Maori woman [? a coinage of Miller's in 1958 Miller Early Victorian NZ 141 or does it echo or copy an original in Chapman]

>  <b>H.S. CHAPMAN</b> [quotation marks enclose Chapman's original] Unable to bear the solitary life, not a few acquired `Maori wives', which was `the conventional name for a Maori mistress'. The native women often welcomed the squatters with bouquets of flowers and became very attached to their young men. The high-born Rangitira girls were sometimes richly beautiful, displaying a lithe elastic figure, soft brilliant eyes and rose brown complexions, set off by glossy black hair falling to the knees. <i>(Nov 24, 1847,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Nov 24 H.S. Chapman letters to His Father [TS, ATB] in 1958 Miller Early Victorian NZ 141]

>  <b>MILLER</b> :cf MAORI WIFE] The native women often welcome the squatters with bouquets of flowers and became very attached to their young men. The high-born Rangitira girls were sometimes richly beautiful ... <i>Ibid.</i> 166 Always quick to imitate new fashions, the young Maoris would turn out for their own flat and hurdle races attired in true jockey style ...; while the attractive Rangatira girls also took to the saddle, equipped with gauntlets and silver whip, habit, hat and plume. <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,141</i>) [NOTES: [possibly reflecting Chapman Letter 24 Nov 1847; with cap: is this Miller or reflects Bell in Stafford Papers 21/12/1851]]

### rangatira landlord
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  There are many of these rangatira landlords, who have leased large blocks of first-class land at a good figure. <i>(Mar 12, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)

### rangatira Pakeha
 
 <i>n.</i> a well-to-do European [ORIGIN: Maori, Also, rarely, 'rangatira Pakeha-Maori']

>  "Lady Stout suggests," said Mrs. Pomare, "that the Maori girl could learn domestic work and the rudiments at least of the art of housewifery at the domestic college, and then? - and then go out to service in rangatira pakeha families to finish her education. .. Will any of the ladies of the rangatira pakeha families have the girls sitting at their tables with them? I think not." <i>(Jul 20, 1908,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  In such opinion, we concur, not only on that account, but for the further reason, that the natives cannot long keep a secret, - and now these rebels have all received full pardon themselves, they will not scruple to talk of their <i>rangatira pakeha</i> correspondents. <i>(Jan 31, 1846,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  She then told me that Mr Hyman (Jack, as she called him) would be home by five o'clock and his dinner was being cooked then, but she expected he would bring some fish with him, as he always caught some on his way back from Herd's Point, where he had gone with a lot of poultry and sucking pigs for the hotel, as a party of rangatira pakehas were on their way there. <i>(Mar 19, 1898,Southland Times,6</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I replied that I wanted to stay with ... my father, who was a rangatira pakeha, just as my Mother was a rangatira Maori. <i>(1948,Taina,134</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> Rangatira - A chief, a gentleman, a warrior. Rangatira pakeha - A foreigner who is a gentleman (not a tutua, or nobody, ...), a rich foreigner. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,236,(Auckland edn ed.</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> The excitement consequent upon our arrival, which extended through the whole tribe, was not simply because two Pakehas had arrived, but because they were two Rangatera Pakehas who had come to live amongst them. The ordinary Pakehas the natives had hitherto come in contact with had been for the most part runaway sailors - ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,3/2/140</i>)

>  <b>GRIEVE</b> And here was the rangatira Pakeha a whole day before his time. Aue Aue. [fn] rangatira - a chief. Pakeha - a white person. Aue - a wailing expression of grief. <i>(1939,Sketches from Maoriland,46,1961 ed.</i>)

>  The Maori look to Makarini, the rangatira pakeha-Maori, and he gets blamed by both parties, Maori and pakeha, when anything goes wrong. <i>(Jul 30, 1873,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

### Rangatira spider
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a spider found only on Rangatira Island in the Chathams [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The island is also a haven for various rare plants, insects and the Rangatira spider, one of the largest known in New Zealand. <i>(Aug 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 52/ 88</i>)

>  [caption] The threatened Rangitira spider. <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 13</i>)

### rangatira wahine
 
 <i>n.</i> a high-born Maori woman [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>DOBSON</b> This good whata belonged to Purua, a rangatira wahine, who lived here with her servants <i>(1930,Reminiscences,68</i>)

### rangatiratanga
 
 <i>n.</i> chieftainship; (Maori) sovereignty, self-determination [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [tino rangatiratanga](ten-tot.md#tino-rangatiratanga))

>  A New Zealander's "Mana" is his "Rangatiratanga"; that is, his Influence, or his Respectability. <i>(Jun 5, 1860,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  This, and the inherent pride of the Ngatimaniapoto people, has given rise to jealousy amongst them of the attempt on [sic] the King to establish a rangatiratanga or chieftanship over the land. <i>(Jul 5, 1870,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  Rangatiratanga is a collective concept. You can't sell your rangatiratanga. In the Treaty of Waitangi, the word "rangatiratanga" appears in article 2. It is translated as "ownership" in the English version. Maori are guaranteed their rangatiratanga over lands, forests and fisheries. <i>(Sep 4, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Judging from his speeches to the natives, it would appear that it is their absolute independence he requires - nay the "rangatiratanga," the chieftanship for Israel, who are the Maoris, over the Gentiles, who are the Europeans, in the Parihaka vocabulary. <i>(Sep 1, 1880,Hawera & Normanby Star,3</i>)

>  <b>T.S. GRACE</b> Some time before we came to Taupo a handsome `Pataka' (viz. a Maori store for food) had been burned. Sir George Grey when on his visit to Taupo saw the said Pataka and told Te Heuheu (no doubt in a jocose way) that it was a sign of his `Rangatiratanga.' <i>(Mar 24, 1858,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Mar 24 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 76]

>  <b>BELICH</b> rangatiratanga sphere, authority, of the chiefs <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,337</i>)

>  The Principle of Self-Management The Rangatiratanga Principle The iwi have the right to organise as iwi, and, under the law, to control their resources as their own. <i>Ibid.</i> 11 The New Zealand Maori Council has explained the concept of Rangatiratanga as meaning more than `possession': `it means the wise administration of all the assets possessed by a group for that group's benefit: in a word, trusteeship' (Te Wahanga Tuatahi, 1983, p 5) In the Crown's view, the First and Second Articles of the Treaty are both strong statements which necessarily qualify one another. Kawanatanga is subject to a promise to protect Rangatiratanga. Rangatiratanga is subject to an acknowledgment of Kawanatanga. The working out in practice of the balance between the two Articles must depend upon a case by case consideration. ... It is the right of all citizens, Maori, Pakeha, and other New Zealanders, that there be effective Government or Kawanatanga in such matters. <i>(1989,Principles for Crown Action,7</i>)

>  More insidiously, the actual terms of the treaty such as "rangatiratanga" and "full and exclusive possession" are being redefined to proscribe Maori rights. <i>(Nov 19, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  The first, on behalf of the Whakatohea iwi, is seeking rangatiratanga and kaitiakitanga - authority and guardianship - over a 50km stretch of Bay of Plenty coastline east of Whakatane. <i>(Sep 30, 2005,NZ Herald,A2</i>)

>  <b>Michael King</b> To Heke, it seemed that the rangatiratanga promised to the chiefs in the Treaty had been usurped... <i>(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,163</i>)

### rangaturah
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [rangatira](ra-red.md#rangatira))

### range
 
 <i>n.</i> (esp. as the ranges) mountainous or high country not necessarily forming a single divide

>  Snow was to be seen upon the Tararua range on the 6th of May, for the first time this year. <i>(May 9, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  In the direction of W. by S. we thought to distinguish Kapiti about 60 miles distant, and the sea from which several ranges of hills separated us. <i>(Sep 12, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> The tops of the high range of hills are covered with snow.] <i>(1840,Papers,15</i>) [NOTES: (T.M. Partridge)]

>  ... to the Northward the Range called [Tararua] banded our view.) <i>(1840,Journal of Ensign Best,229,1966 ed.</i>)

>  ~ n. a mountain.-rang'y a. hilly, mountanous. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> Mr Parkins and Stan were to leave a 3 a.m. for the range, the former working his two dogs and the latter his faithful Pat. `Stan, you know your beat,' announced Mr Parkins ... <i>(1968,Turn of the Tide, The,63</i>)

>  The land between the Inch Clutha and these ranges, much of which is swampy, is laid off in section extending 16 miles from the coast, and containing nearly one half of the Otago block. <i>(Jan 1855,Australian & NZ Gazette</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Jan Australian and NZ Gazette in 1856 Fitton NZ 180]

>  As the roads always run on the tops of the leading ranges (as they are styled), ... a more extensive prospect than the eye can embrace is frequently presented. <i>(1862,Otago: Its Goldfields and ReSources,34</i>)

>  It is a fine sight you see the river flowing down between mountains 3 and 4000 ft., the [70] lofty back ranges looking blue and the tops covered with snow. <i>(Dec 13, 1962,Chudleigh Diary,69,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ASHWELL</b> ... Wooded ranges. <i>(1863,Letters & Journals,IV/460</i>)

>  <b>BARRINGTON</b> This place forms a basin with low ranges and made hills. [ed fn] Presumably gravel hills - i.e. the remnants of high gravel terraces formed (made) by the river in the past. <i>(Mar 27, 1864,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Mar 27 Barrington Diary in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 404]

>  The others went down the sides of the ranges, clearing the sheep off as they went... <i>Ibid.</i> 202 Mustered down to the Coal Gully and camped there. <i>(Oct 4, 1864,Chudleigh Diary,146,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MUELLER</b> The greatest drawback with beach diggins is, they are so quickly washed out. Unless they find gold in the Ranges, and have deep sinking, there is very little prospect of the diggers now at the Coast remaining long. <i>(Jan 2, 1866,My Dear Bannie,100,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... F-- asked me if I would like to ride across the hills, and pay my first visit to some ... old friends ..., who were among the earliest arrivals in the province, and who have made a lovely home for themselves ... on the other side of our range. <i>Ibid.</i> 117 Behind the lower hills ... rises a splendid snowy range ... <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,75,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> Hence a `Spear-grass Scrub' on the hill ranges is frequently most formidable ... to men and horses, though it forms one of the favourite hunting grounds of the Wild Pig. In the Matukituki Valley, `Bayonette-grass' forms an undergrowth in a dense scrub of prickly thorn ... <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,49</i>)

>  <b>ST JOHN</b> The most violent sentiments ... were naturally expressed by [Opotiki] men who saw that their lives ..., and the safety of their houses and crops, were daily in peril at the hands of savages inhabiting the back ranges. <i>(1873,Pakeha Rambles Through Maori Lands</i>) [NOTES: 1873 St John Pakeha Rambles in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 560]

>  The two made little excursions up and down the Gorge, and over the ranges, and when tired they returned to their anchorage; .. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,1/viii/30,(1889,1974) ed.</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> After crossing the steep ranges which I have names as forming a rugges barrier between the harbour and the open country, we struggled up ... to the banks of the river .. <i>Ibid.</i>. 50 ... we found ourselves clear of the ranges, on level country, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 135 ... this done, and our greetings over, one of the sheep was seized and (while C., who had been on the ranges all day, was refitting himself) we killed it by starlight ... <i>Ibid.</i> 204 The cattle ... were half-wild, restless brutes, imported from Australian back-stations, or bred on the equally wild ranges to the north of our own island. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,11</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> The wild dogs had been among the sheep, and were evidently camped in the ranges at the back of the outstation, as we could hear them howling quite close at night. <i>(1935,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,65,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 65]

>  <b>WOOD</b> c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 65 <i>(1875,Waiting for the Mail,43</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The view from Major Scott's station is really very striking. First, Lake Coleridge lying like a deep blue mirror amongst the curiously shaped ranges, formed the centre of the landscape. A number of high hills, which from their remarkably regular form, have aptly been designated Sugarloaves by the settlers, appear in several directions; <i>Ibid.</i> 125 High snowy ranges ... rose above, but owing to their distance ... they did not impress the mind with their truly gigantic dimensions ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,121</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> ... an abrupt succession of ranges, gullies, and basins. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/11 The logs are easily collected ..., as they can be sent down the sides of ranges by means of screw-jacks, rollers and slides. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,1/179</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> Ranges, n. the usual word in Australia for `mountains.' Compare the use of `tiers' in Tasmania. <i>(1898,Austral English,379</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> [In February 1864] he suceeded in getting commissariat through to the troops ... by rapidly cutting a pack-track from Raglan Harbour over the ranges to the Waipa ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/303</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> It was a good life on the hydro. Men dragged home quarters of deer from the ranges to fill the cook-pots, ... or learned from the Maoris to make eel-traps, wickerwork structures which caught the big black-sided fellows down in the stream. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,126</i>)

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> For not content with settling the flat lands and accessible ranges, in their frenzy they laid bare even the steepest and most inaccessible mountains, ... <i>(1939,Present Without Leave,9</i>)

>  Willie and his old man were panning for uranium in the ranges at the time. <i>(Mar 1952,Here & Now,27</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> English topographical words which are much commoner here are gully, flat, terrace and ranges. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,161</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Real cobbers up in the ranges, Brian Brooks climbing Woolshed Hill, yarning around the camp fire, the possums' eyes gleaming above. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,172</i>)

>  FOUND ON THE RANGES. <i>(Aug 10, 1906,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to inspect or supervise stock or activity [e.g .dog range, bush range, forest range.]

>  <b>HINDMARSH</b> He ranged the bush blocks and employed the predominantly Ngapuhi bush gangs ... <i>(1997,Come Be a Pioneer,79</i>)

 <i>v.</i> [(horseracing)]

 <i>n.</i>

### range gum
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The [kauri-gum] samples analysed were - (a) Resin recently secreted by the tree; (b) resin which had been a long time exposed to air or lying in store (bush gum); (c) fossil resin (range gum); (d) resin obtained from peat-bogs (swamp-gum). <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/303</i>)

### range maggot

 <i>n.</i> a type of kauri gum

>  ... one ingenious individual, knowing that white `range nuts' were twice as valuble as black `nuts', obtained a quantity of the latter and carefully shook them up with some white pipeclay from the hillside. <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,7/96</i>) [NOTES: (Firth)]

>  ... fossil resin (range gum) <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,11/303</i>)

### range up
 
 <i>v.</i> of a racehorse, runner etc, to come up within range, or level with, another; used also of a rugby player, to come up in support of a runner [cf. range alongside]

>  <b>GEE</b> <i>(1966,Short Stories (various authors),277</i>) [NOTES: [get quot: Check OEDS.] 1959 Gee in 1966 Short Stories 277]

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> At the turn it was the same except that she was pleased to hear Royal Return had ranged up on the outside of Conformist. <i>(2000,First Past the Post,240</i>) [NOTES: Maurice Gee "The Losers" in Landfall 50 June 1959]

>  Laughing Prince went up to Admiral Drake, and Vertigern also ranged up <i>(Sep 16, 1929,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  At the turn it was the same except that.. Royal Return had ranged up on the outside of Conformist. <i>(Jun 1959,Landfall,13,No. 50/153 ed.</i>)

>  Ranging up on the outside, Autosweep won going away from Eupatrid. <i>(Aug 31, 1940,Taranaki Herald,24</i>)

### ranger
 
 <i>n.</i> [general or unspecified, implies official role of warden]

>  Poaching is rampant at the upper end, and yet the upper ranger never brought a charge home to any one during the whole season. <i>(Aug 20, 1896,Timaru Herald,3</i>)

>  Stop. I am a ranger. You are breaking the law. <i>(1989,Chosen Place,6</i>)

>  <b>49 Vict. 30/77</b> Any ranger or other person may drive any cattle or other animal.. to the nearest public pound. <i>(1885,NZ Statutes</i>)

>  The Minister may.. appoint such honorary rangers as he thinks fit for the purposes of this Act, and all persons so appointed shall be deemed to be Forest officers within the meaning of this Act. <i>(1921,NZ Statutes,12 Geo.V 43/338</i>)

>  That on and after November 1 the Ranger be instructed not to impound cattle, sheep, or horses, grazing on roads within the Otangaki, Hiwinui, Taonui, Makino, Raumanga, and Mangaone ridings, between the hours of 7 a.m. and 5 p.m.; this, however, not to apply to entire animals nor swine; rangers are instructed to inform the county solicitor of cases of swine and entire animals grazing on roads, and the owners to be prosecuted; the rangers are further instructed to impound at all hours animals wandering on the river beds and so destroying the settlers' protective work. <i>(Oct 10, 1908,Feilding Star,4</i>)

>  In this district the services of Rangers and Surveyors were requisitioned to make inquiries with a view to supplying those in need with grass-seed to replace the grassed land that had been destroyed. <i>(1908,AJHR,C-1/22</i>)

>  Witness said he was a ranger for the Auckland Acclimatisation Society. <i>(Apr 6, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,45</i>)

### rangership
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Having passed accounts for Papanui and Hooper's Inlet roads, and deferred the consideration of several tenders for the rangership, the Board adjourned to Monday, 28th December. <i>(Dec 12, 1874,Otago Witness,10</i>)

>  Applications for Rangership of Havelock Commonage close at Mr. F. Oudaille's, Havelock. <i>(Jan 7, 1880,Tuapeka Times,2</i>)

>  ST LEONARDS JULY 9. - The cattle nuisance has altogether ceased since the rangership was handed over to the police constable. <i>(Jul 12, 1894,Otago Witness,23</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> But there can be no finality to a system of inspector, valuer, and rangerships; where coercion is juxtaposed to base compliance; ... where `good enough' is labelled on loop-hanging wire fences, [303] despondent cowsheds, dogleg stockyards, wire-hinged field-gates, ... <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,302</i>)

>  Those duties would generally be to impound all unlicensed cattle found trespassing on Crown Land or public roads within the limits of his Rangership, but not to impound cattle of licensed persons in one Hundred merely overstepping the boundaries of an other, except in cases of constant and extensive encroachment. <i>(Jan 31, 1851,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  At all events, it is high time that the rangership be established on a better footing. <i>(Aug 31, 1866,North Otago Times,2</i>)

### ranges
 
 <i>n.</i> [see also at range n.]

>  <b>BARKER</b> It was a true New Zealand day, ... the ranges of mountains in the distance covered [18] with snow, and standing out sharp and clear against this lovely glowing heaven. <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,17</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> No longer solitary are the waters of the Waitemata, stretching away from our primitive whare on the beach to the foot of the western ranges - ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,Bk4/7/300</i>)

>  He has had a rough time of it surveying a block on the ranges up the Wai-o-hine in the Wairarapa ...[sic] Once ...[sic] they had nothing to eat but a tui and a more-pork. ... They were stewing the two birds when a kuku-pa (bush pigeon) flew up and perched. Bob shot it ... This is not exactly what the Britishers call `sport' - but there is more of nature about it than 30 gents in pink and tops ... waiting for a wretched little fox who, when he is killed, is not equal for culinary purposes to a more-pork. Once coming up the Waikato in 1851 your Uncle James got a pot-shot at a lot of parera (grey duck) ... <i>(Mar 22, 1894,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/597,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>HARPER</b> There is also the rata tree, the prince of firewoods, ... miki-miki, kamahi, or so-called red-birch, white and black birches (which are really beeches), the mountain broom, ribbon wood, broad leaf, totara, and many others, ... and always available in the Ranges [sc. Alps]. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,35</i>)

>  <b>VIRTUE</b> The hills were called the Ranges. The Ranges spread along the skyline all the way to Wellngton. <i>(1987,Redemption of Elsdon Bird, The,34,1984 ed.</i>)

### Rangiatea Church
 
 <i>n.</i> the historic Otaki Anglican church built in 1851, destroyed in 1995 by fire, and re-built and opened 23/11/2003 as a replica of the original.

### rangiora
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Often overlooked as a vase choice, a few stems of the native Rangiora Brachyglottis repanda blooms look very classy by themselves in a large glass vase... <i>(Nov 2, 2002,Dominion Post,F14</i>)

>  <b>FARRELL</b> She takes a rangiora leaf and wipes Tia's watery eyes. <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,273</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a broadleaved evergreen New Zealand tree. [Maori] <i>Brachyglottis repanda</i>, broadleaf, pukapuka, a shrub

>  The <i>Wharangi kura</i> does not produce gum in the North; there is, however, a tree of the same variety callled <i>Rangiora</i>, which does. <i>(Oct 3, 1857,Taranaki Herald,6</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Rangiora, <i>Col[enso]. Brachyglottis repanda. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>36</b> Puka puka or Rangiora <i>(1868,TrNZI</i>) [NOTES: (Botany of North Island)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>B[rachyglottis] rangiora, Buch. ... Rangiora. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,367</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Plants of this genus [Brachyglottis] are well-known to the Maoris, under the name Rangiora. The leaves were much used by them for application to wounds and old sores. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,435,1940, p. 472 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> ... such species as lancewood, karamu, toro, ramarama, koromiko, kanono, kohuhu, kawakawa, hangehange, rangiora, five finger, patete, toru, tree daisies, broadleaf, kamahi, mahoe, tarata, karo, lacebark, wineberry, fuchsia, kowhai, with tree ferns, weki, and mamuku. Toetoe, phormium, cabbage tree, with swamp coprosma and hupiro may figure as a developmental phase on wet soils. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,87,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> On one occasion the buggy horses were tethered to some rangiora bushes. For something better to do while they were waiting, they had a good old feed of leaves from the bushes, which, of course caused them to become intoxicated. <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,68</i>)

>  rangiora n -s [Maori] : a New Zealand shrub or small tree (<i>Brachyglottis repanda,</i>) of the family Compositae having stout branches and lower leaf surfaces densely covered with white tomentum and small heads of flowers crowded in terminal clusters <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1881</i>)

>  <i>rangiora [s] Brachyglottis repanda</i> Forst. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,62</i>)

>  <i>rangiora n.</i> a broad-leaved shrub of New Zealand. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,946</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 626; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 403]]

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Accompanying species may be the two common larger-leaved coprosmas both known as karamu: <i>C. robusta</i> and <i>C. lucida</i>, the bubbly-leaved ramarama (<i>Lophomyrtus bullata</i>), wharangi (<i>Melicope ternata</i>), the `tree daisies' heketara (<i>Olearia rani</i>) and the familiar large-leaved rangiora (<i>Brachyglottis repanda</i>). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,103</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] an indigenous tree of the spp. Brachyglotti, known to hunters, musterers, and trampers as 'bushman's friend'.

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Later, appeared slender matapo .. rangiora. <i>(1921,Tutira,51</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> On one occasion the buggy horses were tethered to some rangiora <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,68</i>)

### rangiriri
 
 <i>n.</i> inanga, a smelt [([Ngai]Tahu.)]

>  <b>MANNERING</b> Another Maori name for the smelt is `rangiriri'. I have witnessed the autumn migration of these fish to the sea, both in Hawke's Bay and Taranaki, and seen Maoris filling sugar bags with them ... They usually call them `Mother Whitebait'. <i>(1943,Eighty Years,158</i>)

### Rangitata goose
 
 <i>n.</i> paradise duck

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> PARADISE DUCK T<i>adorna variegata Other names</i>: Putangitangi, Rangitata Goose. Description: 25' Of intermediate size between the ducks and the larger species (Canada Goose and swans). <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,87</i>)

### Rangitata skink
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a skink discovered in 2004, resident only on Mt Harper in the Rangitata gorge; closely related to and coexisting, but not interbreeding, with the Long-toed skink. [<i>Oligosoma "Rangitata"</i>]

>  <b>Jewell</b> This skink is known from a single mountain, but unless specimens are examined up close they are easily mistaken for the much commoner McCann's skink, so Rangitata skinks could well have been overlooked at other localities. <i>(2008,Reptiles and Amphibians of New Zealand,83</i>)

>  Rangitata skink: Discovered in Canterbury this year. Much larger relative of the long-toed skink. Chestnut brown with prominent pale stripes. <i>(1999,BLANK ENTRY</i>) [NOTES: Date fabricated no details for this citation. Looks to be same format as Sinbad Valley skink.]

### Rangitawa pumice
 
 <i>n.</i> Geol. defn

>  Another widespread silicic airfall tephra reported from southern North Island is the Rangitawa Pumice and its possible correlatives, the Omahina Tephra (Pillans 1979), the Lower Finnie Road Ash (Seward 1974 ; Pillans & Kohn 1981), and the Mangaroa Ash (Neeser et al. 1980), all with fission-track dates in the two-standard deviation age range of 370 +- 70 ky BP (Pillans & Kohn 1981; Kyle & Seward 1984; Kohl 1986, oral presentation). The site 594 tephra could therefore be equivalent to the Rangitawa Pumice and its correlatives. <i>(Nov 1988,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,82/36</i>)

### rangiterer
 
 <i>n.</i> [transf. to an upper-class colonist; a local (Wellington) leader] (See [rangatira](ra-red.md#rangatira))

>  <b>MANSON</b> There is not many days but some of our rangiterers are walked to jail for debt. I hope I shall soon be a rangiterer but not go to jail. <i>(1971,I Take Up My Pen,27</i>) [NOTES: 1842 S. Retter in 1971 Manson I Take up My Pen 27]

### Rangitoto
 
 <i>n.</i> the iconic Auckland Harbour island volcano

>  From the air, Rangitoto's 258 m high sunmmit looks more like a green poached egg than a rugged volcanic cone. Molten lava spewed from around the crater as recently as 600 years ago and plenty of raw rock is still visible between the clumps of encroaching trees. While the eruptions left Rangitoto with a black and scaly skin, ash deposited on neighbouring Motutapu Island.. smoothed the topography and endowed it with up to two metres of rich topsoil. <i>Ibid.</i> 24 ...[Tamatekapua's] fate endures in the island's full name - "Te Rangi I Totongia A Tametakapua" or "the day the blood of Tametekapua was shed"... The early eruptions of Rangitoto were the most ferocious, driven by seawater flashing steam as it encountered fresh magma at 1200 degrees C. <i>(Jul 2004,NZ Geographic,No. 68/21</i>)

### Rangitoto Yank
 
 <i>n.</i> a non-Auckland nickname for Aucklanders

>  Waihi. The Rangitoto Yanks. - S. Island name for Aucklanders, c.1960-1970 <i>(Apr 19, 1984,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Submitted by David More]

>  <b>McGILL</b> It seemed downright peculiar to be leaving what Dad called Auckland's `dolsi vita' ... `Do you know what they call us in Wellington?' Dad asked on the way. `What?' `Rangitoto Yankees.' I had never heard it, but I figured it was a small price, ... to pay for what Dad reckons is Paradise. <i>Ibid.</i> 171 `I miss that underground mutton.' `Eh?' `Rabbit. You are back in 10 minutes with your supper.' ... We need an Eric Partridge to record these Kiwi colloquialisms like underground mutton, Rangitoto Yankees, leather ladies (possums hit by trains in tunnels, where the flies don't get to them and they petrify) and the Waiouru blondes (sheep). <i>(1985,G'day Country,49</i>)

>  `It's always good to beat the Rangitoto Yanks,' [the Wellington rugby coach] said, `and a lot of Wellington guys feel really strong about that. <i>(Apr 11, 1987,Dominion,40</i>)

>  The rest of New Zealand has always hated Auckland and Aucklanders, seeing us variously as parasites and `Rangitoto Yanks' ... <i>(May 1987,Metro,66</i>) [NOTES: Roger `Auckland Life']

>  <b>McGILL</b> Rangitoto Yankee Aucklander, allegedly of the brash imitation American variety, equivalent of Fitzroy Yank in Melbourne, Woolloomooloo Yank in Sydney. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,91</i>)

>  Not for me a bout of fisticuffs when provoked by natives of Canterbury bleating, "Our power keeps you Rangitoto yanks afloat." <i>(Oct 1992,Metro,56</i>)

>  With some justification, Wellingtonians are pleased to call us "Rangitoto Yanks" and affect to scorn Auckland's big-city excitement and glamour. <i>(Feb 6, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

### rap
 
 <i>n.</i> a halfpenny

>  For the penny nicknames used were copper and brown (sometimes Brownie), while the halfpenny was called a half-copper a mag, or a rap. <i>(Oct 1977,NZ Numismatic Journal,16</i>)

### rap jump
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Kiwis have this thing about vertical rubber experiences. They can't wait to leap from bridges with an elastic band on their feet, fling themselves from aircraft with pros strapped to their back like copulating dragonflies, jump into underground rivers with rubber rings around their middles, "rap jump" - abseil down the outer walls of hgih-rise buildings - and "zorb", roll down hills inside transparent rubber spheres... Even tiny Takaka not only has bungee jumping but serious "swinging", the latest in fear fads involving a 60 mph, 50 yd flight over an abyss. <i>(May 11, 1997,Sunday Times (UK),6.5</i>)

### Rap, the
 
 <i>n.</i> the Wairarapa [aka the Wrap] (See [Wrap](../W#wrap),[the](../T#the))

### rapt
 
 <i>adj.</i> very pleased (See [cf. wrapped](../W#wrapped))

>  PRAYER AND PATRIOTISM. .. Through baffling cloud shall rise, past binding bars, / Swept onward ever by insurging seas, / Rapt to high heaven by hope and memories / Of sainted pilgrims carried to the stars! <i>(Jan 13, 1904,Otago Witness,59</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> Candles shone like gold sequins above the altar ... We were rapt. <i>(1981,Black Beech & Honeydew,65</i>)

>  <b>JONES</b> `Aw, I hoped so. Terry'll be rapt.' <i>(1985,Gilmore's Dairy,206</i>)

>  rapt ... NZ colloq. enthralled, very pleased (I won three bottles of champagne and I was rapt). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,627</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 947; also 1363, WRAPPED, sense 2; cf. 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 403]]

>  When the customers heard of the new lines [of stock] they were rapt. <i>(May 20, 1987,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Sally Hayes `The Other Side of the Counter']

>  .. And the family is rapt [headline] EVER-LOYAL parents Ivan and Greta Chamberlain are `absolutely thrilled' that American superstar Meryl Streep is on their side. <i>(Sep 20, 1987,Sunday News,19</i>)

>  `Peru received one shipment of our waste paper and they were rapt with it' <i>(Jan 16, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>) [NOTES: 1987 Dec 15 NZ Herald in 1988 Jan 16 Listener (Life in NZ)]

>  Passengers aren't exactly rapt with the InterCity express - but they say they'll keep using it. <i>(May 21, 1988,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  <b>McGIL;L</b> rapt overjoyed , short for enraptured; eg `We were just rapt to be selected, yeh, it's a great honour, you know.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,91</i>)

>  Anderson ...was rapt to have won his opening bout ... on a unanimous points decision. <i>(Jan 26, 1990,Dominion,14</i>)
