### Rarey-fy
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  the latters filly Rareified and taken away. Bullock drivers taking a spell. <i>(Oct 23, 1959,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> Although, there were plenty of station horses, ... I invested in a couple for my own private use. ... [One] bucked me off more times than I can remember, and I began to get `full' of him. George, my mate, proposed Rarey-fying him. The process consisted in breathing up his nose ... One of Rarey's theories was that the breath of man inhaled into the horse's nostrils intimidated him. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,38</i>)

### rari
 
 <i>n.</i> butterfish, marari

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> R|adi, s. A fish so called. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,198</i>)

>  <b>HAMILTON</b> ... with the following results: 581 eels (from 1ft. to 4ft. in length); 8 dozen flounders, of various sizes, large numbers of aua, or kataka (mullet); about 70lb. or 70lb. weight of pilchard, or mohimohi; a few snapper and kahawai; hundreds of red cod and rarii; and a number of kokopu or rawaru. <i>(1908,Fishing and Sea-Foods,198</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BUTTERFISH ... <i>Other names: Coridodax pullus</i>; greenbone; marari, rari (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,256</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BUTTERFISH [<i>Coridodax pullus</i>] ... The term greenbone is widely used in southern waters referring to the bone colour. The Maori names marari, and rari are seldom used. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,28</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Mainly a vegetarian the Butterfish will take small sea animals and shellfish on occasions. Kelp-fish and Kelpie are names sometimes used as are the Maori names `mairari' and `rari'. No relation to his Australia namesake. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,33</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [Williams nf but prob. related to 56 <i>Hokarari ... Genypterus blacodes</i>, the ling.: =HOKA Taylor's rarihi is prob. an error, phs confusing the large LING with the Dolphin (AIHE) of his informants]

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The <i>Rari</i> , a fish often found eight feet long, the body is large and tapers suddenly into a arrow tail, which is surrounded by a fin above and below, as that of the eel, without having any separate caudal one, the eye is large, the upper part of the head and back covered with spots, the lips are thick, from the lower one are four barbules, the fins red. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,628</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Rari - the ling. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Rari - the ling. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Rari - the ling. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>) [NOTES: c1880 South Island Maori Lists `Wahi Mahika Kai' in 1945 Beattie Maori Place-names of Canterbury 62]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Genypterus blacodes</i> Frost. Ling, or Rari <i>(1886,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,304</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> <i>Aihe</i>, is a large fish, twenty-four feet long, having a small head like the porpoise, with similar teeth; it probably belongs to that order, and is synonymous with <i>rarihi. </i> <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,411</i>)

### rarihi
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [rarii](ra-red.md#rarii))

### rarii
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mair Fish in Piako River ... <i>large numbers of aua or kataha</i> (Agonostoma forsteri); <i>about 60 lb. ... weight of pilchard or mohimohi (Clupea sagax)</i>, two varieties; ... hundreds of young red-cod, rarii (<i>Lotella bacchus</i>), and what I believe are young rock-cod, or kokopu or rawaru (<i>Percis colias</i>). The red-cod were from 3 in. to 4 in. in length, and the rawaru, or, as the natives here call them, `toitoi' or `panepane', from 2 in. to 6 in. long. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/319</i>)

### rark up
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  But Labour dared not rark this up, for fear of being tarred with the "instability" brush. <i>(Jun 8, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),14</i>)

>  This is Iti in his element: 15 hours a week on the Te Mangai Paho-funded Auckland radio station to rark up listeners, spread his word, and blast techno music through the traditional waiata for the hell of it. <i>(Oct 21, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  Zoe and I have always been easily rarked up but until recently this was tempered by Kel's sweetness and Ioane's wisdom. <i>(Feb 27, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D5</i>)

>  <b>A11</b> They're middle-aged and sensible, long disillusioned with the sanctity of marriage, and the vice that really rarks them up is smoking. <i>(Mar 19, 2000,Sunday Star Times</i>)

>  Some accept the meaning I use : to infuriate someone by teasing, to the amusement of those in the know. But for one who agrees, three or four insist <i>rark</i> refers to a justified telling-off. Ted Anderson writes to say he grew up in Nelson and his wife Anne in Thames, and the pair of them have always used <i>rark</i> to mean stirring one up when they deserve it... Donald Macintosh Campbell... ferreted out Fr P S Dineen's <i>Dictionary of the Irish Language</i> which gives <i>radharc mo shul do bhaite</i>, meaning essentially "to fool me" and shortened to <i>radharc</i>. Since the letters <i>dh</i> in the middle of <i>radharc</i> are silent, like the <i>gh</i> in night, the pronunciation was <i>rarc</i>. Either way, it's fun to be rarked up. <i>(Apr 22, 2000,Dominion,20</i>)

>  But it's the family sense of humour to rark people up... My God, you could rark him up so easily. <i>(May 27, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  Anne Budd as Trish and Kelly O'Sullivan as Tara build a good contrast as the bolshie pair endlessly rarking each other up. <i>(Jul 16, 2002,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

>  Radiohead rark it up in Paris with material from "Kid'A" and "Amnesiac". <i>(Jul 28, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),80</i>)

>  I didn't need to be told that I couldn't dance on the bar and rark it up in here. <i>(Dec 8, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,7</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  They were rarking it up and yelling and yahooing - they were just awesome. <i>(Mar 27, 2000,VicNews,10(2) : 7</i>)

>  I suppose I like rarking people up ... it's just fun. <i>(Jan 19, 2002,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  A wonderful word finding increasing currency is <i>rark</i> .Most people know what it means, but no one knows where it came from. When a person is <i>rarked up</i>, he's unaware the information he is being fed is phoney. Those in the know are giggling behind their hands while the victim gets angrier and angrier. <i>(Apr 15, 2000,Dominion,20</i>)

>  One-eyed Canterbury fans will be here in force. We will be rarking it up. <i>(Jan 13, 1997,Evening Post,1</i>)

### rark-up
 
 <i>n.</i> [New Zealandism. Internet search Jan 2006: 320 Global, 720 NZ hits.]

>  Michelle Kidd, of the Methodist Mission Northern, told the judge she would give Woolright a "rark up". <i>(Jul 19, 2005,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

>  You pretty much train for six months, and live like a month, so it's nice to have a bit of a rark-up afterwards. <i>(Jun 25, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,12</i>)

>  And then you round up the usual suspects, so that everyone has a crack, Consumers' Institute, a sharemarket analyst, an expert from defence, the Trade Union Council, Women's Refuge, Mikey Havoc, plus a fact-free rark-up on the talkback radio circuit. <i>(Jul 7, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

### rarkie
 
 <i>n.</i> causing the wheels of a vehicle to spin vigorously before moving off

>  Davies says she learned to do "rarkies" (spinning the wheels as the car takes off) before she even learned how to change gear properly. <i>(Apr 3, 2002,Capital Times,p5</i>)

>  Davies says she learned to do "rarkies" (spinning the wheels as the car takes off) before she even learned how to change gear properly. <i>(Apr 3, 2002,Capital Times,5</i>)

### Raro
 
 <i>n.</i> a drink

>  <b>FARRELL</b> 'Mmmm,' she said, careful lest she hurt Peg's feelings. But could she have some Raro instead? <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,74</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) a Rarotongan, or a Pacific Islander

>  In Auckland, for example, you can take one look at someone and say to yourself: Oh yeah, she's a Sa, he's a Raro, he's a Toke. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 67</i>)

>  `MAORILAND ENTERTAINERS' [in Egypt, WWI] ... In the Rarotongans, we have representatives of a race naturally endowed with song, and nothing pleases a New Zealand audience more than to secure items, wonderful in the harmonious blending of the part singing and most moving in vivid expression, from their doughty fellow soldiers and fellow-countrymen, the `Raros'. <i>(Aug 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,Second Series 2/14,1981 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BERRY</b> The Islanders were Coconuts or Raros to the biased Maori; the Maoris were Black Bastards to the bigoted Pakeha; the militant Maori spat `Paak-ia' as an obscenity .... <i>(1980,First Offender,42</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) short for Rarotonga

>  <b>Dryden</b> The story was typical of what I was to hear that week on Rarotonga ...A day later I was to put the question to Mrs King as we filmed young Lisa riding round Raro on her bike ... <i>(1978,Out of the Red,48</i>)

>  It began with the question, many months ago. `Sir, will you organise a trip for us to Raro?' <i>(Aug 1988,Metro,111</i>)

>  <b>Geary</b> Did you go to church in Raro? I hear it's quite amazing. They sing without an organ and that. <i>(2003,Man of the People, A,81</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal) Rarotongan

>  Then you put your Raro music on and you'd do the hula. Son, I never forget those wonderful days... <i>(Dec 15, 2005,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

### Raro-route
 
 <i>n.</i> channelling of money through the Cook islands

>  For winebox buffs, the tax inquiry has been rich in jargon; we have heard about "Raro Routes" (for Cook Islands money-go-rounds) and "poison pills" (for payments triggered if the IRD or media get wind of deals) and learnt the new verb to "tax aggress", meaning to push the IRD to its limits of tolerance. <i>(Nov 18, 1995,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  The deals were routed through the Cook Islands... The so-called "Raro-routes" used available law on deductibility of interest from loans, and inter-company share investments that were tax exempt... IRD would not disclose.. how it got the necessary information, nor the audit methods it used against the companies and individuals using the Raro-route schemes... "The Raro-route schemes were separate from those in the winebox"... <i>(Sep 14, 1996,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

 <i>n.</i> name of one-time popular aerated mineral drink

### RAS

### rat
 
 <i>n.</i> the rats have been (etc.) in: said (often humorously) of bludgers, scroungers etc. who have used up (or are about to share) one's supplies (of beer, food etc.) [ca1945 (Students and ? Soldiers) eg. Keep that beer out of sight or else the rats will be in as quick as look at you.]



 
 <i>v.</i> to ransack, rob [cp RAT THE PETER)]

>  I am made to have said, that Mr. Masters member for Grey Valley, "ratted". What I said is, that under certain conditions, ratting was excusable, especially when rats carried away a golden tail. <i>(Jan 20, 1880,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Nevertheless, Mr. Clendon and Mr. Porter gave it their support, and are smartly rebuked for their ratting by the Auckland Editor. Mr. Clendon's conduct is the subject of the following remarks: - .. His grant of of land ought to have made him in every sense of the word an independent man; ten thousand acres of good land immediately adjoining the capital is no bad exchange for three hundred acres of inaccessible rock at the "Governor's Folly." <i>(Mar 9, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,2</i>)

>  That night as the old man slept the reprobate son crept into his bedroom, `ratted' the old man's pockets, and lit out for another glorious orgie. <i>(Aug 4, 1906,NZ Truth,59/7/7</i>)

>  <b>LEE</b> He saw a New Zealander stoop to rat a Hun who had lost his backside. <i>Ibid.</i> 194 There must be a lot of dead Huns to rat. <i>(1937,Civilian into Soldier,157</i>) [NOTES: [1914-18] 1937 Lee Civilian into Soldier 157]

>  <b>McCALLUM</b> And old bluebeard had the plenty trouble to stop [them] ratting the cupboard for the small drop of plonk which he kept for the toothache and other things. <i>(1963,Half-Gallon Jar,27</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> I made an instant soup with a pint of milk and ratted through Vic's medicine box until I found a sedative; seconal I think. <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,109</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> [to strip] ... he searched the civies Darby had got him for a smoke. Not even a butt. The bastards must have ratted him. <i>(1975,Breathing Spaces,98</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> rat steal or plunder; John A. Lee's Civilian into Soldier, 1937: `There must be a lot of Huns to rat.' NZA <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,91</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> kiore, the bush, Maori, native New Zealand rat often so-called. [in special use]

>  The Maori rat, as I know it, is rather smaller than the common or Norwegian variety. It has a very long tail - at least one third longer than the ordinary; - its coat is as fine as velvet, and is of a bluish colour, showing at first sight a black shade, while the head is also a more pleasing type. <i>(Jan 29, 1908,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  We saw no four footed Animals either tame or wild or signs of any except Dogs and Ratts and these were very scarce especialy the latter. [f@n1] ... The so-called Native Rat or kiore, thought to be identical with <i>Rattus exulans</i> of Fiji is now almost extinct. <i>(Oct 29, 1769,Cook's Journals,1/186,1955 ed.</i>)

>  The country is certainly destitute of all sorts of beasts either wild or tame except Dogs and Ratts, [sic] the former are tame and live with the people who breed and bring them up for no other purpose than to eat and ratts are so scare that not only I but many others in the ship never saw one. <i>(Mar 31, 1770,Cook's Journals,1/277,1955 ed.</i>)

>  rat - kior|ee [kiore] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,110,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: >c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 110; also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 283 (|= macron precedes) rat, kio|re [kiore]]

>  The Secretary read the following extracts from a letter of the Rev. W. Colenso to Ronald C. Gunn, Esq., of Launceston, dated Waitangi, Hawke's Bay, New Zealand, 4th September, 1850:- `I have procured two specimens of the ancient, and all but quite extinct, New Zealand Rat, which until just now (and notwithstanding all my endeavours, backed, too, by large rewards) I never saw. It is without doubt a true Mus, smaller than our English black rat (Mus Rattus), and not unlike it. This little animal once inhabited the plains and Fagus forests of New Zealand in countless thousands, and was both the common food and great delicacy of the natives - and already it is all but quite classed among the things which were.' <i>(1851,Papers & Proc. RS Van Diemen's Land,1/301</i>) [NOTES: [Morris]]

>  <b>WALLACE</b> [...Kiore Maori...] <i>(1880,Island Life,445</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> ? a swindle

>  We are now in the thick of the cross examinatn. by Sheehan of the chiefest of the apostles - Tizzey the Tanner (ko Tanera). ... We have not found anything that can be called a rat; nor even got the scent very strong. But I must not report upon Heret[aunga]. <i>(Apr 6, 1873,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/347</i>) [NOTES: (C.W.Richmond, Napier)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the introduced Norwegian rat

 <i>n.</i>

### rat pack
 
 <i>n.</i> ration pack [in ODE as South African]

>  [Hdg] Kiwis spice up rat packs. Kiwi troops in East Timor are bored with their "rat packs" and are concocting their own delicacies. <i>(Oct 17, 1999,Sunday Star Times,A2</i>)

>  The dreaded and much maligned "rat pack" that keeps soldiers fed in the field may be replaced by a microchip menu. <i>(Mar 25, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),4</i>)

>  <b>HUGHES</b> We officer cadets normally subsisted on the third kind - ratpacks. These were individual bags containing food and expendables necessary for 24 hours of hard physical work. <i>(1999,Shooting from the Lip,24</i>)

>  [Hdg] Chip may end Army 'rat-pack' rations... The days of the much-maligned "rat pack" which keeps soldiers fed in the field may be numbered, replaced by a microchip menu. <i>(Apr 3, 2000,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  For a couple of weeks, troops had to live off ration packs - "rat-packs" - with tinned processed cheese. Crackers and canned meat. <i>(Jul 15, 2000,Dominion,29</i>)

 <i>n.</i> french knitting [ca1930 (Havelock) (children's)]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SORENSON</b> HAKE, BLUE (a) Scientific name: <i> Macruronus novaezealandiae</i> . (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Blue Hake. (c) Other common names: Whiptail; Rat-tail. (d) Principal localities where taken: Wellington, Nelson, Southern New Zealand. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,29</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i> Grenadiers or rattails</i> A family (Macrouridae) of deepwater fishes, characterised by a large head, short body, and long tapering tail. The last feature gives rise to their alternative common name of rattail, the name most commonly used in New Zealand. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,62</i>)

>  [species name] rattails .. [scientific name] <i> Macrouridae</i> .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The Rat-tails, which have the tail tapering off to a point, belong to the same order, and two of them (<i> Coelorhynchus australis </i> and <i> C. aspercephalus</i> ) have the mouth placed below the sharp snout in a very shark-like manner. <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,200</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 200]

>  <i> Coelorhynchus australis</i> (Richardson). Javelin-fish (rat-tail). <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/404</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Rat-tail .. .. see WHIPTAIL, HORI <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> [The order ANACANTHINI, true codfish] includes rat-tails (big, rare, spiny fish with spear-shaped heads. <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,213</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> RATTAILS: <i> Macrouridae</i> The fishes in this family are collectively known as rattails, a name suggested by the greatly elongated pointed tail. Other names commonly applied to this group are whiptails and grenadiers. ... About 25 species are found around New Zealand ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,156,1984 ed.</i>)

>  The fish, known as the `rattail' or Coelinchorus, were caught off the coast of ... Taranaki, in the orange roughy grounds at a depth of 800m ... The rattail also known as the grenadier is closely related to the codfish family, feeding on the bottom of the ocean ... Of the 40 known species of Coelinchorus 11 of them seem to be indigenous only to New Zealand says Mr Pauling. <i>(Jul 22, 1988,Evening Post,14/9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> grass

>  The botanical name of rat-tail or Chilian grass is. . Sporobolus elongatus. .. Land full of rat-tail is a terror to plough, as too many settlers know <i>(Mar 26, 1892,Auckland Weekly News,31</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i> STRIPED RATTAIL Coelorinchus fasciatus </i> ... This species is dull grey with a series of darker vertical bars on the back and flanks. It is one of the most abundant rattails in New Zealand ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,162,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i> Two-banded rattail Coelorinchus biclinozonalis</i> One of the more common species ... <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,62</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i> MAHIA RATTAIL Coelorinchus matamua</i> (Mahia matamua) ... The few known specimens are all from ... off Gisborne. [?sc Mahia peninsula] <i>(1982,Collins Guide,163,1984 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

The long-nose rattail is the smallest of a group of three very similar species all of which are characterised by having a tapering pointed snout that is almost twice as long as the diameter of the fish's eye. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,160,1984 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i> DEEPSEA RATTAIL Bathygadus cottoides </i> ... [157] Deepsea rattails are occasionally trawled in depths from 1000 to 1500m on the continental slope off north-eastern New Zealand. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,156,1984 ed.</i>)

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### rat-tail knitting
 
 <i>n.</i> French knitting (See [French knitting](fly-fuz.md#french-knitting),[Dolly down the reel](dig-dou.md#dolly-down-the-reel))

### rata
 
 <i>n.</i> a) a large native forest tree with bright red flowers. b) a lofty-climbing forest vine. [Maori rata[a's-macron]] [General, largely unspecified rata. or with descriptive epithet <i>Metrosideros robusta </i>(formerly <i>M. florida</i> Hook.f.; usu. RATA or NORTHERN RATA and <i>M. umbellata</i> (usu. SOUTHERN RATA, and prob. dominant on w. coast of S.I.) forest trees, as distinct from the lianes (usu. AKA q.v.) <i>M. scandens</i> (formerly <i>M. perforata</i>), <i>M. albiflora</i> (?often WHITE RATA and formerly <i>M. diffusa</i> A.Cunn.) and <i>M. diffusa</i> Forst. f. (formerly <i>M. hypericifolia</i> A.Cunn.) named RATA in Maori as distinct from AKA, the RATA-VINE (<i>M. scandens</i>), see CLIMBING RATA, CLINGING RATA 3. below plural <i>ratas, rata </i>] (See [for Metrosideros spp. for tree-forms](../F#for-metrosideros-spp.-for-tree-forms),[and occas. for the liane see also IRONWOOD](../A#and-occas.-for-the-liane-see-also-ironwood),[RATA-VINE](ra-red.md#rata-vine))

>  rata : sometimes you meet with a rata, which here is one of the highest in New Zealand... <i>(1841,NZ Journal,51</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Genus Metrosideros</i> ... (Name from the Greek, meaning <i>iron-hearted</i>, in allusion to the iron-like hardness of the timber). Plants of the genus are usually known to the colonists [bushmen] by the Maori name <i>Rata</i>. 12 sp. Williams 327 <i>Ra[a]ta[a] ... 1. Metrodiseros robusta</i>, a forest tree. ... 2. <i>Metrosideros excela</i>, locally south of East Cape. ... = po[o]hutukawa.: early spg <i>lahtah, rattar </i> <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,278,1940 p. 292 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEGG</b> (|= macron precedes) ironwood, la|htah [rata] <i>(1979,Boultbee Journal,283</i>) [NOTES: >1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 283]

>  Ironwood - la'htah [rata] <i>(1826,Boultbee Journal,110,1986 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> The rattar[as-acute] is a beautiful, lofty, spreading tree; very hard, stringy, and tough; close-grained, and in appearance not unlike the live oak. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,20,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Rata (Genus unknown) - This is a fine and useful tree, producing a heavy, close-grained, durable red wood ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),50</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The Rata [a's-acute] (Calistemon Zelandicae), is an invaluable wood to the shipwright. RATA <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/392</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> In 1835, an influential priest was bribed by us to dispose of an ancient adze, called toki pu tangata[a2-acute] by the people; it was extremely ancient, and had been buried in a sandy soil for many years; the place of its interment was only known to the priest, who had noted the spot by the branching of a [71] particular tree called Rata[as-acute]... 1/71 fn The adze was formed of a blue granite inserted in a handle of the rata or red pine-wood, carved agreeably to native taste. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/70</i>)

>  <b>BIDWILL</b> The Rata, [fn Metrosideros robustus] in my opinion the monarch of the New Zealand forest, ... <i>(1841,Rambles in NZ,36 and fn,1952 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HODGKIN</b> Arata [sic] is a tree which grows from 60 to 70 feet in height ... <i>(1841,Narrative of Eight Months Sojourn in NZ, A,27</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The red pines are the Totara, Towa, Mahi, and Rata, all of which are close-grained, and moderately hard. <i>(1842,New Zealand (Heaphy),42</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The rata also is common (Metrosideros robusta). It is the king of New Zealand trees. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/75</i>)

>  <b>1968</b> There is also rata, tawa, honeysuckle &c., which, though of harder nature, may hereafter be applied to many useful purposes. <i>(1843,Letters from New Plymouth,58</i>) [NOTES: (1842 Nov 27) 1843 (1968) Letters from New Plymouth 58]

>  (And) <i>Metrosideros robusta </i> <i>(Jul 29, 1844,Report of Select Committee on NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1844 July 29 House of Commons Report of Select Committee on NZ]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> 10. Rata, (<i>Metrosideros robusta</i>.) There are several varieties of this tree; one grows at first as a parasite, creeping in numerous stems, like ropes, up the trunks of the other forest trees, gradually enclosing them till they perish, and then uniting to form a noble tree taller than that which it has destroyed, with an enormous trunk, but hollow within. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,142</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> 10. Rata, (<i>Metrosideros robusta</i>.) There are several varieties of this tree; one grows at first as a parasite, creeping in numerous stems, like ropes, up the trunks of the other forest trees, gradually enclosing them till they perish, and then uniting to form a noble tree taller than that which it has destroyed, with an enormous trunk, but hollow within. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,142</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Rata, a tree <i>(1848,Leaf from Natural History,21</i>)

>  Her timbers are of Picked Rata, and the said vessel is planked with the best Mai. <i>(Apr 12, 1851,Lyttelton Times,8</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> Among the trees of N. Zealand perhaps the `rata', Metrosideros robusta is the most remarkable. ... In its early growth it is a climber ... <i>(Feb 14, 1853,Traveller under Concern,78,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Metrosideros <i>florida</i>, Sm. ... Nat. names, `Raka pika', <i>R.Cunn</i>.; `Rata', Middle Island, <i>Lyall.</i> A stout-trunked plant, climbing the loftiest forest trees.<i>Ibid.</i> 68 Metrosideros <i>robusta</i>, A.Cunn. ... Nat. name, Rata', <i>Cunningham</i>. A tall tree, 60-80 feet high, ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 66</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The rata, <i>metrosideros robustus</i>, flowers in February. <i>(1856,Traditions & Superstitions,13fn</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Amongst them the Metrosideros robusta (rata of the natives) and Metrosideros lucida are most abundant and conspicuous. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,146</i>)

>  <b>LADY CAMPBELL</b> ... as if ... holly and misletoe [sic] ... instead of the ... coral-like bunches of the roto [sic]. <i>(1864,Martin Tobin,2/136</i>)

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> The sentries were stationed - and reclining underneath a bushy Rata, in the comfortable lair of an old boar, pipes alight, we felt excessively comfortable ... <i>(1864,Memoranda,16</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Rata, <i>Col[enso]. Metrosideros robusta</i>. Rata, Middle Island, Lyall. <i>Metrosideros florida. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> [We] were running right into what appeared to be a grove of rata-trees growing at the water's edge. The rata only grows in the hills and near water ... When the bowsprit touched the rata-branches, ... H-- made a signal to lower the mast ... <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,118,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> The scarlet rata was still in flower ... This curious parasite ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,18</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> here is the cabbage-tree palm, with bare shank and top-knot; the nikau palm, with weird and wondrous frondage; the lancewood, ...; the fern-tree, .... There is the twisting, squirming rata; the gaunt ... kahikatea; the golden kowhai; the dark velvet-covered rimu; the feathery red tawai; the perfumy mangiao; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 2/192 The Rata (<i>Metrosideros robusta</i>) is ... [w]hen young a creeper and a parasite, then called Ratapiki. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/187</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Rata .. .. <i>Order</i>, Myrtaceae; <i>Genus</i>, Metrosideros; <i>Species</i>, M. robusta. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/301</i>)

>  <b>LADY MARTIN</b> I saw, for the first time, a grand, old pine dying in the vigorous, cruel embrace of the Rata. <i>(1884,Our Maoris,30</i>)

>  [Native name] Rata .. [Settlers' name] Rata .. [Family] Myrtaceae [Native name] Rata .. [Settlers' name] Ironwood .. [Family] Myrtaceae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>FEATON</b> METROSIDEROS FLORIDA (<i>Smith</i>). ... This beautiful species is a lofty climber in all stages of its existence, ... It is commonly called `Rata' and is often confounded with its ally, M. robusta, the Rata proper; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 161 METROSIDEROS ROBUSTA (<i>A.Cunn.</i>) ... The `RATA'.- This beautiful ... tree is almost confined to the Northern Island ... It is generally affirmed that the plant is a climber, ... [162] The tree is sometimes though improperly designated the `Female Pohutukawa'. <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,158</i>)

>  English Name Native name Botanical Name Rata Rata Metrosideros Lucida <i>(1889,Catalogue NZ Exhibition,35</i>) [NOTES: 1889 Canterbury [Catalogue Canterbury Court, Exhibition) 35]

>  <b>KIRK</b> It is invariably erect and never climbing, although bushmen and settlers frequently state that it climbs the loftiest trees, and sooner or later squeezes them to death in its iron clasp. In proof of this they assert that, when felling huge ratas, they often find a dead tree in the centre of the rata: this is a common occurrence, but it by no means follows that this species is a climber. The error is simply due to imperfect observation, which has led careless observers to confuse [<i>M. scandens</i> the Aka], which is a true climber with <i>M. robusta</i>. <i>(1889,Forest Flora,263</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Rata, n.</i> Maori name for two New Zealand erect or sub-scandent flowering trees, often embracing trunks of forest trees and strangling them: the Northern Rata, <i> Metrosideros robusta </i> , A. Cunn., and the Southern Rata, <i> M. lucida </i> , Menz., both of the N.O. <i> Myrtacea </i> . The tree called by the Maoris Aka, which is another species of <i> Metrosideros (M. florida)</i> , is also often confused with the Rata by bushmen and settlers. In Maori, the adj. <i> rata </i> means red-hot, and there may be a reference to the scarlet appearance of the flower in full bloom. The timber of the Rata is often known as <i> Ironwood </i> , or <i> Ironbark </i> . The trees rise to sixty feet in height; they generally begin by trailing downwards from the seed deposited on the bark of some other tree near its top. When the trailing branches reach the ground they take root there and sprout erect. For full account of the habit of the trees, see quotation 1867 (Hochstetter), 1879 (Moseley), and 1889 (Kirk). <i>(1898,Austral English,380</i>)

>  [`Native Trees'] [sic]: -->`Rata, or Ironwood. It would be supposed that almost every colonist who has seen the rata in bloom would desire to possess a plant.' [also other citations: check if necessy] <i>(Nov 10, 1892,Otago Witness</i>)

>  The timbers of commercial value are totara, rimu, miro, matai, rata, and kamahi, in mixed bushes; but Fagus fusca and other beeches predominate on the high lands between the lakes and the fiords on the west coast. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,30</i>)

>  <b>WHITE</b> The Rata proper (M. robusta) makes excellent fuel, and is used quite as much as coals, especially for cooking. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,88</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> Rata (Rah-tah): A Bush-tree, covered in summer-time with glorious scarlet blossom. <i>Ibid.</i> 54 Kauri and Totara, Rimu, and Mataiand Maire,/ Red-as-blood Rata, and bright-as-blood Pohutukawa,/ Manuka dark-ey'd, Convolvulus star-ey'd - / The glittering of you that morning! [f@n2 attached to the italicised names] 2. Kauri, etc. (Kow-ree, To[acute]-ta-ra, Ree[e acute]-moo, Mah[a acute]-ti, Mi-reh, Rah[a acute]-tah, Po-hoo-too-kah[a acute]-wah, <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,19</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> We have climbed above the pines [on the West Coast]; ... and up here on the mountain side the vegetation is the flowering <i> rata </i> , the <i> akeake </i> , the native alpine broom, the white blossomed ribbonwood, ... the flax-like<i> kiekie </i> , and the semi-tropical <i> neinei </i> , half-shrub, half-palm, and whole forests of fern trees. <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 95</i>)

>  <i> ra[a]ta[a],</i> a tree (Metrosideros spp.), crimson flower that covers the trees with sheets of colour: it may start in a fork of any tree, and sends down rootlets that grow in size till they surround the [146] tree and strangle it, when the rata stands on its own. Rata (no macrons) has quite other meanings. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/145-146</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i> rata </i> : tree with scarlet flowers. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  The Maori name for several trees and climbing plants of the myrtle family; most of them have brilliant scarlet or crimson flowers. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),330</i>)

>  <i> ~ n </i> . two New Zealand flowering trees, often embracing trucks of other trees by dropping seed in their forks, and strangling them [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  <i> rata ... n.</i> two N.Z. flowering trees, often embracing trucks of other trees by dropping seed in their forks, and strangling them [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <i> rata n.</i> a New Zealand tree with red flowers. [< Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,948</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 627; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 404]]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> (gloss.) rata: a New Zealand tree with spectacular red flowers. <i>(1949,I Saw in My Dream,273,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> RATA Tree. <i> Metrosideros Robusta.</i> [sic] <i>(1967,Evil Day,381</i>)

>  Sometimes you meet with a rata, which tree is one of the highest in New Zealand, and the diameter of its trunk is rarely exceeded by that of other species. <i>(Sep 12, 1840,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  What really should be celebrated as the Wellington Christmas tree is its cousin, the norhtern rata. Metrosideros robusta. <i>(Dec 24, 2004,Dominion Post,C9</i>)

### rata blossom
 
 <i>n.</i> [-]

### rata grub
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also rata tree caterpillar](ra-red.md#rata-tree-caterpillar))

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> ... I recall his [the botanist-Inspector's] chuckle when we showed him ... the huge white caterpillar known throughout bushland [in North Auckland] as the `rata grub', so-called because it was always found at the foot of a rata and was supposed to be the origin [101] of the tree itself. It is a curious creature - a great caterpillar with a strange tree-like fungoid growth, growing sometimes to a height of several inches, from its body. I think everybody believed in the legend of the rata-tree caterpillar; ... <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,101</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a vegetable caterpillar.

>  We then talked about the rata grub. The gentleman told us it was only a tale about it growing into a rata tree, and the tale had arisen from seeing a seed of the tree sprouting in the grub. <i>(Apr 29, 1908,Otago Witness,85</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> .. a huge white caterpillar known throughout bushland as the 'rata grub.' <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,101</i>)

Alps]. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,35</i>)

### Ratana
 
 <i>n.</i> the Maori religious movement founded by T.W. Ratana in 1918: the Ratana church; a member of that church [incl. attrib. uses]

>  Considerable excitement exists amongst the natives at the Ratana Maori settlement, near Turakina, where a prominent native man of means claims to have performed marvelous cures through faith healing. Natives have come from all parts of the Island, and they have returned home discarding their crutches, invalid chairs, etc. <i>(Nov 22, 1920,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  Ratana has about 70,000 religious members and more than 140,000 people follow its religious and secular teachings. <i>(Jan 11, 2005,Dominion Post,1</i>)

>  Some busloads of Ratana adherents left for home late today. <i>(Jan 26, 1968,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  The Ratana Church is seen by many Maori, especially the younger ones, to be an expression of organised Christianity that has not been imported by missionaries; it is not colonial... [He] believes Ratana has become a form of Maori expression that has replaced politics as the race breaks its traditional ties with the Labour Party. <i>(Aug 30, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,3</i>)

>  There is no mention of Iriaka Ratana's most important political achievement, for example, that of healing the breach between the Ratana Movement and the Kingitanga. <i>(Jan 1992,Metro,152</i>)

>  A crescent moon and stars, emblems of the Ratana faith, can be emblazoned at night in electricity. <i>(Sep 14, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,9</i>)

>  People say are you a Ratana? Are you a born again Christian? <i>(Oct 7, 1992,Capital Times,2</i>)

### ratbag
 
 <i>n.</i> OED: Austral. and N.Z. slang. A stupid or eccentric person, a fool; an unpleasant person, a trouble-maker. Also attrib., stupid, idiotic, uncouth. [ca1951 R.Gilberd (Okaihau) `a common derogatory term suggesting that (1) a man is worthless or (2) he falls short of his pretence, i.e. is a humbug.' 1984 17 M E99 Pakuranga Coll. 3 RATBAG]

>  A newspaper headline a few months ago, "Scientists' Code to hit Ratbags", introduced the new Code of Ethics produced by the Royal Society of New Zealand. <i>(Nov 1997,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,114/2</i>)

>  As it stands, it won't catch many ratbags. <i>(Nov 1997,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,114/3</i>)

>  ...it took Maori back, in one swoop, to the early history between us, when white colonists didn't listen and Maori were not heard;... when the Maori who was on your side was the "good" Maori, and all the rest were ratbags. <i>(Feb 8, 2004,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> How did you ratbags get here, a highbrow joint like this? <i>(1956,Sullen Bell,19</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> Proper ratbag of a joker he is, too <i>Ibid.</i> 164 This'd be the best scrapper among you bunch of ratbags, wouldn't it? <i>(1961,Hang on a Minute, Mate,?</i>)

>  A bit late to find that out, you snobbish ratbag wowser. <i>(Oct 12, 1970,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13b</i>)

>  "A few ratbags have held us back," says Northland ostrich and emu farmer Ross Pennell. <i>(Sep 5, 2003,Dominion Post,C5</i>)

>  Rat-traps in her hair, old ratbag, can I shoutcha down ... <i>(Jan 1952,Here & Now,16</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> With a cold fury that good men died for a ratbag like this I shove the safety-catch foward and pull the butt up. <i>Ibid.</i> 206 An easy-going boy kicked to death by ratbags. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,140</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> That's Sir Walter Peach. He's an old ratbag, really, I suppose what I've heard about him, but he's not a bad stick. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,193</i>)

>  <b>JOSEPH</b> Do you think that one ratbag is going to ruin that? <i>(1962,Pound of Saffron, A,241</i>)

>  <b>CASEY</b> `What do you think you're going to do with the old ratbag [sc. a horse] now that you've caught him?' <i>(1963,As Short a Spring,161</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> Specifically New Zealand expressions that have encountered adverse rulings [as unparliamentary] are `ratbag' and `half-pie wowser'. Ratbag in Australia means `an eccentric' but in New Zealand is much more pejorative and suggests an utter scoundrel. <i>(1966,English as it is spoken in NZ,48</i>)

>  <i>(1967,Landfall,21/259</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER`</b> I haven't got the time to be sitting on me acre or playing games like you lot. I've got to clean up after these ratbags. Muggins is me middle name. <i>Ibid.</i> 162 There's a fair few of those sort of ratbags about - <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,103</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> ratbag: Bag of rats, or M.P. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,183</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> [spg RAT-BAG] That screwed-up half-witted ratbag in the two-piece suit, the hairy ape with the dead-cod stare: <i>(1974,Maori Woman,117</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> I'd never seen him as anything more than a ratbag, so I was glad for her sake. <i>(1976,Pacific Star,157</i>)

>  ... he believed what he called `a posse of Mafia ratbags' would be waiting in his room. <i>(1979,Loosehead Len's Gluepot Greats in $1,154</i>)

>  <b>JOHNSTON</b> `Too right it is, Pop. Government ratbags.' <i>(1981,Fish Factory,87</i>)

>  ratbag n. Sl. chiefly Austral. & N.Z. a contemptible person: used as a term of abuse. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,948</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 627; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 404]]

>  <b>GEE</b> Give me some bloddy flower ter sniff and I seen what a ratbag I am. <i>(1982,Halfmen of O,65,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> `Whew! What plonk! Where'd you get this rotgut?' `Don't be a ratbag, Len.' <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,62</i>)

>  [Wal to goat] I'm gonna chuck you off ya niggly old ratbag! <i>(Oct 27, 1986,Evening Post,30</i>) [NOTES: (Footrot Flats)]

>  The editor of the Taupo Times wrote back to tell me that I was a fool to want to ... follow a ratbag career like jounalism. <i>(May 1987,Metro,61</i>) [NOTES: Roger `Auckland Life']

>  <b>McGILL</b> ratbag troublemaker or uncouth person or engaging non-conformist; eg `The fellow's an utter ratbag, I'd keep away if I was you.' ANZ. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,91</i>)

>  "But she's a real ratbag and if I wasn't there she would kick the side out of the float and do herself an injury." <i>(Sep 10, 2004,Dominion Post  Sport & RaceForm,1</i>)

>  "Shit, no," he said. "Ron was too rat-bag for Mum and Dad."... It strikes me now that what was "rat-bag" and what wasn't is as much as anything else at the core of Morrieson's fiction. <i>(Jun 12, 1982,Listener (New Zealand Listener),100</i>)

>  <b>EDWARDS</b> They used to tread on my feet with their big army boots, and laugh if I yelped, the ratbags! <i>(1992,Metro,137</i>)

>  One farmer told, that he was going to offer 60l. and rations (sixty pounds!) for a ploughman, ... <i>(Dec 17, 1848,Letters from Otago,21,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHAW</b> Many of the working men obtain thirty to forty pounds, with board and lodging besides, or rations as they term them in that part of the world. <i>(1852,Tramp to the Diggings,302</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> .. shearers' rations <i>Ibid.</i>83 RATES OF WAGES, April 13th, 1855. ... farm servants [84] (with rations), from 35l. to 50l. per annum; <i>Ibid.</i> 220 The shearing of the sheep is generally done by contract; ... at so much per 100 sheep shorn - their [sc. hands'] rations, in addition to their pay, being provided for the shearers. <i>Ibid.</i> 292 Shepherds and stock-keepers [sic] wages are high in New Zealand; about [pounds]40. a year with house room and rations ... <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),?</i>)

>  On December 24, 1866, the diarist remarks: `Sent rations to Stotburn hut.' I understand `rations' was the common term, although I did not see its use elsewhere in the diary, the word stores being used quite a few times. On May 20, 1867, we note that `J. Gavin drove to Drysdale's with provisions' - the solitary use of this word. <i>(Dec 24, 1866,Puketoi Station Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Dec 24 Puketoi Station Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 115]

>  <b>PRENTICE</b> [see HUT-KEEPER] <i>(1870,Tale of NZ,37</i>)

>  <b>CAIRD</b> ... stores and rations <i>(1874,Sheep Farming in NZ,42</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> Contrary to the custom of most run-holders in New Zealand at that time, Walker had introduced the Tasmanian and Australian plan of rations, or `rashuns', as the men called them. He would not allow his shepherds to kill their own meat. Sometimes Earnest had to take potatoes and firewood. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists</i>)

>  <b>PASH</b> The rations [for farm labourers] differ somewhat with locality. ... [There is] more food [that at Home] under hut and rations system. <i>(1883,Report on NZ,6</i>)

>  <i>(1898,Report on NZ</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> ... labourers got 7s. to 8s. a day; ... farm servants, [pounds]40 to [pounds]50 with rations. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,186</i>)

>  5(a) The rate for shearing by hand shall be not less than [pound]1 per hundred, with rations. <i>(1912,Sheepowner's Handbook, The,25</i>)

>  Rations.-. Pronounced to rhyme with `nations', not with `passions' as in the army. The r. system went out of general use in Canterbury in the 'seventies. The old scale was 16lb meat, 10lb flour, 2lb sugar, l/4 lb tea a man a week; or sometimes 12lb meat, 12lb flour, 2lb sugar, and 1/4 lb tea. (C.) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> A pioneer told me that a bullock team ... might cart in `provisions' or `supplies' for a station, but the provisions became `rations' (which he pronounced `rayshuns') when served out to the men at the `shepherd's huts' (or out-stations), and these rations became `tucker' when apportioned as meals. ... Another pioneer, in referring to the food the men got on the stations, said it was called by the ship term `rations' (which he pronounced `rashuns') <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> They were very generous and if anyone visited their camp they would cook their best rations for their visitors, ... <i>(1964,Doctor in the Mountains,84,1974 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> of a medal or decoration, common and worthless [WWI]

>  <b>LEE</b> <i>(1937,Civilian into Soldier,200,1963 ed.</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> rations phr. cut one's rations ultimate uxorial threat to deny conjugal rights; eg `Freddy's got a face as long as last week, the missus must have cut his rations again.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,92</i>)

### ratpower
 
 <i>n.</i> (humorous or ironic) of a weak engine etc, it's two etc ratpower (or occas. catpower) (R. Mason) (play on horsepower)

>  <i>(May 30, 1946,NZ Farmers' Weekly</i>) [NOTES: nf P]

### rats and rabbits and rips
 
 <i>phr.</i> [children's game]

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. West Coast: Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; Wellington: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, Isaac and Rebecca. Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; Auckland: My Fair Young Lady, Rushes and Reeds, Surrender the Tower, Walls, Ball Hopscotch, ball games, Solomon Says; Hawkes Bay: Wolf Wolf Go Home, Homai; Canterbury: Poor Old Tom, Rats and Rabbits and Rips, Steps, Skin the Snake, Steal a Peg, Toodle-em-Buck, The Gag; Golden Bay: Who's That Walking Round My House Tonight, I've Come to See Poor Mary Jane, Steps and Stairs, Creeping, Branding, O'Leary; Otago: Sir Roger de Coverley, Who's That Knocking at My Door. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,231</i>)

### ratshit
 
 <i>adj.</i> useless, bad; unwell [(1981 Feb Avondale College (Auckland) Slang Words in Use (Goldie Brown Collection) ratshit: no good) also as adverb (= badly etc.)]

>  I've written the odd poem but I'm pretty rat-shit at it. <i>(Jun 5, 2002,Capital Times,5</i>)

>  I could never allow myself to be sober - that is without being on a high - because I felt I had to blank out my mind because I had a rat-shit life. <i>(Feb 16, 2002,Evening Post,24</i>)

>  ratshit ill or ghastly, from depression or hangover perhaps. -broken or useless. - no good; eg `I feel ratshit', `That spanner is ratshit', `This tucker is ratshit'. All NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,92</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> 'My life is already ratshit. One extra thing on top of it won't make it any worse.' <i>(2000,Uncle's Story, The,296</i>)

### ratstail
 
 <i>n.</i> broad-leaved plantain

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BROAD-LEAVED PLANTAIN (<i>Plantago major</i>), also called ratstail, birdseed, lamb's tongue, and greater plantain, is common on roadsides and trodden places throughout the country and in pastures in the King Country. ... From the middle of the rosette ascends the flower stalk ... , which afterwards give[s] place to seeds, and produce[s] the ratstail appearance that gives the plant one of its trivial names. The long spikes of seed pods are often used for feeding caged birds. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,161,1967 ed.</i>)

### ratstail grass
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The flower, stem, and leaves, enclosed in the above letter when I received it, were those of the sporobolus elongatus, in some districts called "ratstail grass," in others "Chilian grass," and probably a dozen or two local names, for all I know to the contrary. It is a native of the province of Auckland, and of late years has been spreading rapidly, in consequence of the extension of agriculture. <i>(Aug 31, 1874,Daily Southern Cross,3</i>)

>  .. he believed that where the land was not wanted to go under the plough, the rats-tail grass would thrive. <i>(Feb 11, 1875,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>)

>  On such lands as the Rangiriri hills and Komakorau, where clay obtains and little or no soil is seen, the ratstail grass and some of the poas are almost the only grasses that can be grown. <i>(Mar 25, 1880,Waikato Times,2</i>)

### rattan
 
 <i>n.</i> early name for supplejack

>  <b>YATE</b> ... and even here [on the beaten track in the forest], on account of ... the danger of being caught by the chin by some strong fibrous creepers, the ratan, or supple-jack, which are suspended from the branches, ... it is no easy task to make good your way ... <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),16</i>)

### rattar
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [rata](ra-red.md#rata))

### rattle
 
 <i>n.</i> (for sheep) [suggested by HWO hxs this is a nonce-word, coined at Orari Gorge Station in 1860s. -->[refce 1868-9] However, I know it from South Taranaki in the 1930s and 1940s (DEH)]

>  <b>HARPER</b> With a "rattle" (pieces of tin attached to a circle of wire), Sandy kept the sheep up to the shearers. <i>(1967,Kettle on the Fuchsia, The,87</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to drive sheep downhill.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Here and there a dog, revelling in the opportunity to work off its exuberance, would shoot out and slew sheep down a clear spur; full-tongued it would rattle them helter-skelter down to the man below. <i>(1949,High Country Days,160</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> I put a dog in behind them, rattled them well down, and took off down the ridge. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,12</i>)

### rattle gun
 
 <i>n.</i> a hand-held machine for tightening or removing wheel nuts on a vehicle

>  The flash tools you get in tyre centres, what do you call them? Rattle guns? <i>(May 3, 2005,TV1</i>) [NOTES: Fair Go]

### rattle one's bones
 
 <i>phr.</i> [1984 16 F E38 Pakuranga Coll. 6 RATTLE THOSE BONES cf P: (and EDD) rattle move, run, drive quickly 17c slang: 1850 dial ShI, Sc. Yks Dors Sam]

### rattle one's dags
 
 <i>phr.</i> hurry up, get a move on [usually in address, Hurry up! (also e.g. RATTLE YOUR DAGS OUT OF HERE cf. ca1950 (Wellington) Les Cleveland -Come on, rattle ya dags. We haven't got all day ca1951 Wellington dental nurses ca1952 Wellington (Tom Griffin) 1960 16C F B4 Wanganui Girls C. 6 RATTLE YOUR DAGS'! 1984 14 F E144 Wgton Girls C. 6 RATTLE YOUR DAGS]

>  But he didn't exactly fail to cooperate when his caucus friends began sending him for grooming makeovers and encouraging him to rattle his dags in the House. <i>(Oct 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),13</i>)

>  "Was ist das 'rattle your dags'?" (cartoon caption). <i>(Aug 8, 1987,Listener (New Zealand Listener),49</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> You'd better rattle your dags, then. <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,328</i>)

>  Their chairs barely warm, they are rattling Labour's dags for it already. <i>(Jan 1, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> [Just then ... in the indistinct light we could just make out our old friend C. ... bringing in a small mob of rattling sheep.] <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,135</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> `I'll be with you in two shakes of a lamb's tail.' ... `You'd better rattle your dags, then.' <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,328</i>)

>  <b>MORRIESON</b> Let's get moving. Rattle your dags, McGhee. <i>(1976,Pallet on the Floor,135</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> rattle your dags: HURRY ALONG there. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,85</i>)

>  rattle ... rattle (one's) dags NZ sl. hurry up. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,628</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  Wearing the traditional black singlet and sacking moccasins, he first mops up the dags which have been spread out to dry, rattling them like gravel as he sweeps them into the corner. <i>(1987,Country Calendar (TVNZ),123</i>)

>  [Advt] Rattle your dags. <i>(Apr 27, 1992,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>)

>  time to rattle his dags, he figured, and pull finger. <i>(Aug 27, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> Told him another time he'd better rattle his dags. <i>(1980,Prisoner of Mother England,21</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> I'm not overstruck on that new cop. - Told me to rattle my dags out of there. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> rattle your dags: a command to get moving. It is often, for example, the packy's call to breakfast, a high-country wakey-wakey. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,65</i>)

### rattle one's furlined tweeds
 
 <i>phr.</i> hurry up (euphemism and rhythmic variation on more widely used rattle one's dags =get a move on) [ca1950 Dental Nurse (Southland) 1950 I]

Certainly they [the swaggers] appreciated the idea of going to work on shanks' pony and coming home in a train with a cheque. <i>(1964,Shiner Slattery,27</i>)

### ratty
 
 <i>adj.</i> angry, bad-tempered

>  I am afraid the fear of infection has made me quite ratty. <i>(May 20, 1902,West Coast Times,2</i>)

>  Evidently, Smith preferred to dine at Dodd's and objected to continuing his journey, but when he found that he would have to do a fast if he remained where he was, as there was no chance of being served, he got "ratty" and told Dodd what he thought of him, and the Salvation Army. <i>(Jan 11, 1919,NZ Truth,6</i>)

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Muggins was calling him "ratty" and soothing his young'un as it shook and sweated under the hand. <i>(1904,Sons o' Men,20</i>)

>  <b>PICARD</b> Slush was ratty, and plucked them first, and slaughtered them [29] after ... <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,28</i>)

>  <b>FANNING</b> `The referee was ratty.' `We had a moral victory.' <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,22</i>)

>  <b>DEVANNY</b> She began to get ratty <i>(1926,Butcher Shop, The,61</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> A person is ... ratty when he is stupid or angry [Austral. borrowing] <i>(1941,NZ Slang,62</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> [?not on this page] <i>(1960,Maori Girl,239</i>)

>  ratty adj. angry or mad. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  ratty adj. -tier, -tiest. 1. Brit. & N.Z. sl. irritable; annoyed. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,949</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 628; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 404]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> cracked, dotty; mad

>  I would ask, Are the Auckland members asleep, like the Howickers, or are they gone "ratty" on the Stratford route? <i>(Oct 25, 1898,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  He is as ratty as they make them in regard to all forms of gambling, and the result is thet he is never likely to be able to command anything more than he has at the present moment - a bare billet. <i>(1906,NZ Truth,76/8/2</i>)

>  His Worship: `Was the accused sober?' Witness: `Well, he looked muzzy.' His Worship: `What do you mean by that?' Witness: `He looked either drunk or a shingle short.' His Worship: `A what?' Witness: `A screw loose. `His Worship: `I don't understand you.' Witness: `He seemed to have a rat in his garret.' His Worship: `Oh, speak English!' Witness: `Off his dot - balmy [sic] - dilly.' Sergeant: `He says the man was mad, your Worship.' Witness: `No, I didn't. I said he was either drunk or ratty.' <i>(Apr 16, 1910,NZ Truth,251/1/6</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> ratty silly, stupid or slightly eccentric; eg `The guy's ratty.' NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,92</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>adj.</i> mad about

>  <b>McGILL</b> ratty on/ratty over infatuated; eg `He's ratty over Mildred.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,92</i>)

### rauhuia
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> <i> Linum monogynum</i>, Forst. ... Nat. name, `Rauhuia', <i> Colenso</i>; `Kaho', Cunn.; `Nao', <i> D'Urville</i>. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 28</i>)

>  Rauhuia, <i>Col[enso]. Linum monogynum.</i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  Rauhuia. Linum monogynum, Forst. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/40</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> LINUM MONOGYNUM (Forst.) ... The `RAUHUIA' or `KAHO'.- This interesting little plant is common, especially on rocky coasts, throughout both Islands ... <i>Ibid.<i> 178 Rauhuia [Maori name] .. Flax [Settlers' name] .. Linum monogynum .. Herb <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,68</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i> Linum monogynum (The True New Zealand Flax)</i> ... Native name Rauhuia. This is the true New Zealand flax; the plant which is usually so called being a lily. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,218,1940 p. 229 ed.</i>)

>  <i> rauhuia</i>, a plant (Linum monogynum), a good rock-plant with its long soft-leafed branchlets white-tipped like huia-feathers (whence the name). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i> rauhuia [s] Linum monogynum</i> Forst.f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,63</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> Rauhuia, New Zealand flax, or <i> Linum monogynum</i> was the country's first indigenous export, if you don't count dried heads. <i>(1980,Ghost Towns of NZ,221</i>)

>  <b>TAYL;OR</b> RAUHUIA (<i> Linum monogynum</i>) Spindly native ... common in some regions especially on rocky or broken ground. <i>(1981,Weeds of Roadsides,117</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i> Linum monogynum</i> RAUHAUIA <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,144</i>)

 <i>n.</i> An aromatic plant used as a scent; Nothopanax edgerleyi. ... 2. The scent from the same.:

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Raukawa, <i> Col[enso]. Panax Edgerleyi. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)
