### raunu
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a New Zealand-bred cultivar of ryegrass.

>  All the seed is certified, the Raunu is mother seed and the Huia is first generation. Here is the mixture he uses per acre: Ryegrass (Raunu) 4lb ... <i>(Oct 1974,NZ Journal of Agriculture,33</i>)

### rauparaha
 
 <i>n.</i> native convolvulus (See [also pohue](per-pop.md#pohue))

>  Of course, the introduced, white-flowered convolvulus is not to be confused with our native convolvulus - raparaha or pohue - the small edible namesake of awesome Ngati Toa leader Te Rauparaha. According to the <i>Dictionary of New Zealand Biography</i>, soon after Te Rauparaha's birth, a Waikato warrior who had killed and eaten a relation of his, threatened to eat the child as well, roasted with rauparaha leaves - and the child was named Te Rauparaha in defiance of this threat. <i>(Nov 1, 2003,Dominion Post,E13</i>)

>  Introduced white-flowered varieties [convolvulus]are not to be confused with our native convolvulus, rauparaha or pohue. This small edible plant gave its name to the awesome Ngati Toa leader, Te Rauparaha... A coastal plant, rauparaha has curled leaves in a rounded heart-shaped, quite thick and waxy, with pink striped flowers of the distinctive morning glory form. <i>(Nov 8, 2001,Contact (Wgtn),18</i>)

### raupatu
 
 <i>n.</i> (Politics) confiscation [usually of land] [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  Like many Maori, they appear to regard such a law as another raupatu, another forcible seizure of Maori property rights, and are understandably upset. <i>(Dec 14, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  As Maori, we had stories about land we <i>used</i> to have and the raupatu was a point of shame for us. <i>(Dec 1998,Mana,37</i>)

>  Beulah, my mum, is a product of raupatu. <i>(Oct 1998,Mana,No. 24: 21</i>)

>  A claim was also made to the land laying [sic] to the north of Waikawa stream, through the "raupatu" of Pakanui and Rongopakeiwi, and also from occupation. <i>(Jul 10, 1885,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  One fine old chief said that "If Mahuta climbed a tree he would do the same; if Mahuta jumped over a precipice, he would follow; but they would have none of Messrs. Heke and Ngata's Bill," which they termed and attempted "raupatu," or conquest. <i>(Sep 17, 1900,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  The Whatonga people had fled and though the mana of the different incoming tribes over land had been at least partially determined by "take raupatu" (conquest), rights were still subject to challenge. <i>(May 20, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  "As one who grew up on a farm on raupatu land in the Waikato, where our family's presence felt like it had been for ever, I cannot even begin to imagine the scale of loss felt by Waikato Tainui from the mid-19th century, but I hope that I and my government have played our part in putting right" she [Helen Clark] said. <i>(Apr 9, 2009,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  A claim was also made to the land laying north of Waikawa stream, through the "raupatu" of Pakanui and Rongopakeiwi, and also from occupation. <i>(Jul 10, 1885,Poverty Bay Herald,2</i>)

>  We are still open and anxious to meet them and combine for the good of the native race, but we are not going to submit to a raupatu, conducted by a small party, who don't own the bulk of the land, or, at any rate, of marketable land in the North island. <i>(Sep 17, 1900,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  The Raupatu Commission has recommended your Taranaki people a fund of &#8356;5000 per annum, Waikato &#8356;3000, Whakatohea &#8356;300, and N'Kahungunu, Wairoa &#8356;300. <i>(May 6, 1928,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/90 and fn</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>SORRENSON</b> But the most notable of the grievances was the raupatu - the confiscation of Maori land during the New Zealand wars of the 1860s in Taranaki, Waikato, Bay of Plenty, and the East Coast. <i>(1986,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/33</i>)

>  In his judgement, Justice Cooke noted the Crown did not challenge that the raupatu of Tainui lands in 1864 was in breach of the treaty. <i>(Nov 20, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),126</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> confiscation

>  The iwi were reluctant to sign off on a deed of settlement until they had a report from the tribunal ... on the raupatu [confiscation] aspects, to guide both parties and confirm the historical background. <i>(Oct 29, 1999,Dominion,3</i>)

>  Nabb's report.. provides a snapshot of the fading of a dream to take the $170 million raupatu settlement and quickly turn it into a legacy of wealth for generations to come. <i>(Feb 9, 2000,Evening Post,5</i>)

### raupeka
 
 <i>n.</i> the native Easter orchid. [Maori]

>  <i> raupeka</i>, a fragrant orchid (Earina autumnalis), flowering in autumn as name suggests. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i> E[arina] autumnalis .</i>.. Brides' Bouquet. RAU PEKA (branch plant). <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,22</i>) [NOTES: [spg RAU PEKA]]

>  <b>SALE</b> Here [in the bush] we found, growing on a fallen log, the spiked fronds and the tiny deep-scented flowers of the New Zealand Easter orchid - raupeka or Ereaua autumnalis. <i>(1982,Four Seasons,116</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i> Earina autumnalis</i> EASTER ORCHID ...Raupeka <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,288</i>)

### raupeti
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Raupeti, Col[enso]. Solanum nigrum. <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  Also, the cooked leaves and herbaceous tops of [32] the Toi (<i>Barbarea Australis</i>), and of the Poroporo, or Raupeti(<i>Solanum nigrum</i>); and the baked inner stems and sago-like pith of the large black fern tree, Korau, or Mamaku (<i>Cyathea medullaris</i>). <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/31-32</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <i>raupeti</i>, a plant (Solanum nigrum), doubtful if native or introduced; but name and habit suggest native. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/157</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

### raupo
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> Had they shown the same assiduity in raupo-ing the walls of the house as they did in smoking their pipes we should have had no cause to grumble, ... <i>(1881,Poenamo,4/4/276</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> [attrib. See also raupo hut, r. cottage, r. church, r. house, r. whare, et al]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The sail is made of raupo flags or kiakia [a's-acute], grass, etc., of triangular shape; it is fastened to two small rickers or poles, which serve for both mast and yards, and is fixed upright between the gunwales. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/23</i>)

>  <b>CAMPBELL</b> ... a great number of `rapeoo forras' ... <i>(1840,Present State of NZ,17</i>)

>  <b>JENKYNS</b> ... rapu bushes. <i>(1892,Hard Life,213</i>)

>  ..sea-borne breeze/ Trill on the lute-edged toi-toi spear,/ And passing up the valley to the falls/ Curtseys the rapa reeds the creek. <i>(May 25, 1942,NZEF Times,9</i>) [NOTES: 1942 May 25 NZEF Times 9 in 1979 Cleveland The Iron Hand 23 .]

>  Another highly curious article of vegetable food was the <i>pungapunga</i>, the yellow pollen of the <i>raupo</i> flowers- the common bulrush, or cat's reed mace (<i>Typha augustifolia</i>... To use it as food it is mixed up with water into cakes and baked. It is sweetish and light, and reminds one strongly of London gingerbread. <i>(1881,TrNZI,XIII : 28</i>)

>  Understanding I needed plenty of; oldest boy in a family of eight on a raupo-ridden dairy farm; in the sugarbag years. <i>(Mar 31, 1993,NZ Tablet,12</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The circumstance of the burning of Capn. Duke's house has made us somewhat thoughtful relative to raupo buildings. ... This question will require some consideration but wattle and dab is exceedingly expeditious and very durable. <i>(Dec 17, 1827,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,91,1961 ed.</i>)

>  What was once boxthorn-covered farmland near the cottage, with its raupo-lined pond,. is being tamed as the couple plant natives in accordance with a planting guide. <i>(Mar 8, 2003,Dominion Post,F5</i>)

>  Another measure which has excited surprise and alarm in this District, is called the Raupo House Ordinance, by which the Governor is empowered to issue a proclamation fixing a day after which all houses constructed wholly or in part of thatch, reeds, flax, and other materials extensively used in building, are subjected to the enormous tax of twenty pounds per annum. <i>(Apr 2, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [Ma.] a swamp-growing bulrush Typha orientalis or T augustifolia.

>  <b>MILLER</b> Although the Maoris had burned only 'a few bundles of <i>raupo</i>', this was made the occasion of an armed expedition proceeding to the Wairau to arrest the chiefs on a charge of arson. <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,70</i>)

>  The roof was thatched with raupo and strips of totara bark called "flags." <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,205</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]

>  A shadow would sometimes darken those eyes coloured light brown, like the velvety heads of raupo. <i>(Mar 13, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),45</i>)

>  Houses are built by the natives for the settler in the bush of a species of rush called "Raupo," which is found in abundance in all the swamps, this being cut and dried, neatly tied with strips of "Koradi" or native flax, to a frame-work of wood, and the roof is thatched with a tough species of grass; they are sometimes very tastefully lined with reeds. <i>(Oct 28, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,4</i>)

>  Now it was a mud-filled hollow, with just the tips of raupo showing above cracked mud. <i>(May 29, 2004,Dominion Post,E16</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> Flaggy grass Roupo <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/339</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> Raup|o, s. A species of reed of a soft and spungy nature. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,201</i>)

>  <b>EARLE</b> `Another party was collecting rushes, which grow plentifully in the neighbourhood, and are called raupo.' - <i>(1827,Narrative of a Nine Months Residence in NZ</i>) [NOTES: 1827 Augustus Warle, `Narrative of Nine Months' Residence in New Zealand,' `New Zealand Reader,' p. 67: (Morris)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> He was left for dead, but ...he crept amongst some rushes, and afterwards constructed a canoe of <i>raupo </i>, and crossed over to the main. <i>(Mar 31, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,304,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> ... the Thatch is the Toie Toie [<i> toetoe </i>] a kind of Flags that is used for the Huts, and an other one more like what our Coopers use for putting between the seams of a Cask, called Rappoo [<i> raupo </i>]. <i>Ibid.</i> 37 [cf COOPERS' FLAG] Rappoo is a Flag or Marsh Reed, used in England by Coopers to put between Staves of Casks; ... <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),35,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MATHEW</b> Fairburn's house is built by the natives of what they call `rapou', that is, long reeds matted thickly together and tied ... <i>(Feb 26, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Feb 26 F.Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 63]

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... and a flax-house at [186] Uwoua, eighty feet long by twenty-four broad and forty feet high to the ridge-pole, was built by a chief and a large company of natives for a small keg of powder 25lbs. in weight. The house was extremely water-tight and warm, and several streams and marshes had been denuded of their rushes (<i> raupo </i>) to cover the roof and sides; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 1/206 ... a thick coating of bulrushes (<i> raupo </i>) cut from the marshes in the spring, and left to dry during the summer, packed up in long bundles. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/185</i>)

>  `The raupo, the reed-mace of New Zealand, always grows in swampy ground. The leaves or blades when full grown are cut and laid out to dry, forming the common building material with which most native houses are constructed.' <i>(1842,NZ Reader,122</i>) [NOTES: 1842. W. R. Wade, `A Journey in the North Island of New Zealand,' `New Zealand Reader,' p. 122:]

>  <b>DOMETT</b> `Section 2. ... there shall be levied in respect of every building constructed wholly or in part of <i> raupo, nikau, toitoi, wiwi, kakaho </i>, straw or thatch of any description [... 20 [pounds]' <i>(1843,Collection of Ordinances</i>) [NOTES: 1843. `An Ordinance for imposing a tax on Raupo Houses, Session II. No. xvii. of the former Legislative Council of New Zealand': -->[From A. Domett's collection of Ordinances, 1850.]]

>  ... lined with rapo, a kind of bulrush, ... <i>(Feb 16, 1842,Letters from New Plymouth,5</i>) [NOTES: (1842 Feb 16) 1843 (1968) Letters from New Plymouth 5]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The same species [Typha augustifolia] is common to both hemispheres. Under the name of repo, or raupo, it is a most useful building-material to the natives ... [bundles of which] they tie together with the mangi-mangi, a climbing fern, the Lygodium articulatum of Achille Richard. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/426</i>)

>  <b>BROWN</b> rapou (cooper's sedge) <i>(1845,New Zealand and Its Aborigines,66</i>)

>  <b>ANGAS</b> Close to Pipitea is a <i>ware karakia </i>, or chapel, belonging to the Christian natives, which is built of raupo and tohi-tohi grass, according to the native fashion. <i>(1847,Savage Life,1/235</i>)

>  Another chief comes and stays with us ... He and I go and get <i> rappoo </i>, that is the native name for firewood; he takes a tune on the trump; different natives can play, they say is is capi, that is, good. <i>(Jul 20, 1848,Letters from Otago,16,1978 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> Together with the succulent root of the raupo (Typha angustifolia), a kind of bulrush which grows in the swamps, the fern-root affords an excellent maintenance to the wild hogs. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,148</i>)

>  [The Waikato river] runs through a rich level of deep fern fringed with raupo (a bullrush). <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/80</i>) [NOTES: J.C. Richmond]

>  Raupo! Raupo!! Raupo!!! The Undersigned is prepared to supply the Inhabitants of the town with this invaluable article. It can be warranted free from maggots, &c. &c.. A bed made from this article will be found a colonial substitute for goose's down. <i>(Jun 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times,2</i>)

>  <i> Typha angustifolia </i>, L. ... Nat. name, `Raupo', <i> Col. </i> <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 238</i>)

>  <b>FITTON</b> The land ... growing flax or `raupo' (a weed like a bulrush, which generally grows on peaty soil,) is generally the most fertile, .. <i>(1856,New Zealand (various authors/titles),261</i>)

>  Went for rapu <i>(Apr 10, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>VON TEMPSKY</b> sp. raupu The troops had hutted themselves for the winter in huts built of a sapling framework, walls of `raupu' (a species of rush in New Zealand swamps) and roofs of long grass. <i>(1864,Memoranda,181</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Raupo, <i> Col[enso]. Typha angustifolia. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  Raupo. Typha angustifolia, Linn. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/49</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Typha latifolia Raupo <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/126</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>TINNE</b> There was some difficulty in picking one's way ... through the tall raupo (rush) and tohi-grass; ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,28</i>)

>  <b>ANON</b> `It was thatched with raupo or native bulrush, and had sides and interior partitions of the same material.' <i>(1877,Colonial Experiences,10</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Green rushes (<i> Raupo </i>) ..... O. Typhaceae; G. Typha; S. T. angustifolia. `Extensively used for making walls.' <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/303</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i> Raupo, n </i>. Maori name for a New Zeland bulrush, <i> Typha angustifolia </i>, Linn. The leaves are used for building native houses. The pollen, called <i> Punga-Punga </i> (q.v.), was collected and made into bread called <i> pua </i>. The root was also eaten. It is not endemic in New Zealand, but is known in many parts, and was called by the aborigines of Australia, <i> Wonga </i>, and in Europe `Asparagus of the Cossacks.' Other names for it are <i> Bulrush, Cat's Tail, Reed Mace </i>, and <i> Cooper's Flag </i>. <i>(1898,Austral English,382</i>)

>  <i> Ra&#769;.u-po </i> (ra.&#769;.u-po.; <i> colloq.</i> rou&#769;.po.), n. [Maori.] (Bot.) A very common bulrush (<i> Typha angustifolia </i>), much used by Maoris and New Zealand settlers for thatching roofs, and for making hurdles, rafts, etc. [<i> New Zealand </i>] <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2030</i>)

>  The roof was ... covered with raupo - sometimes with an inner sheeting of totara-bark - while an upper layer of toetoe-grass, secured by ropes of mangemange ..., preserved the raupo from wet. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/22</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i> T[ypha] angustifolia, Linn. ... Bulrush; Raupo.</i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,743</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i> Raupo (row-po)</i>: A kind of bulrush. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,184</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> a plant like a bulrush, used for building huts, thatching, etc.; the source of punga-punga. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,9</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> What we used to call flags then [in the 1840s], but now known as bulrushes or by the Maoris called rapo (or raupo), were used by the coopers then. They were inserted between the joints and kept the oil casks as tight as a drum. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,29</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> The word is commonly mispronounced `rapoo', and was so pronounced and spelt as far back as 1840. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,276</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> <i> raupo </i>, a waterside plant (Typha angustifolia), bulrush; much used for thatch: often mispronounced rapoo. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> The <i> poi </i> is an egg-shaped thing, made of the pith from a swamp plant covered with a woven cover of raupo, somewhat like the leaves of the cattail. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,18</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Early in 1859 we read [in the Puketoi Station Diary] of .... the cartage of what is variously spelt `rapu, rappo, raupu, and raupo' <i>Ibid.</i> 111 Raupo, which we had always heard called `rapu' <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,77</i>)

>  <i> raupo </i>: bulrush. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  At one time the gully slopes were covered with toitoi, rapu and scrub. <i>(Dec 30, 1954,NZ Farmer,3</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> Sometimes the walls themselves consisted only of <i> raupo </i>, a plant resembling the bulrush, or even of reeds. <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,101</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> You can see the swamp and the raupo and toetoe. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,215</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The vocabulary of New Zealand English has been influenced by ... the adoption of Maori words and phrases. ... Examples of the native words now in general use are the names of birds, fish, plants, and trees: <i> kea, tui, hapuku, tarakihi, koromiko, raupo </i>, and <i> kauri </i>. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/769</i>) [NOTES: (Wall NZ Speech)]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Raupo (bulrush).. .. koreire, raupo .. .. <i> Typha muelleri </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i> ~ n.</i> New Zealand giant bulrush, the Australian wonga (q.v.) [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  <b>BRATHWAITE</b> RAUPO Type of reed. - <i>(1967,Evil Day,381</i>)

>  <i> raupo ... n </i>. N.Z. giant bulrush, the Australian <i> wonga (q.v.)</i> [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>63</b> <i> raupo [s] Typha orientalis </i> Presl. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> The inner parts and roots of the raupo (Typha orientalis) provided a meagre amount of food, but the seed heads were prized for their bright yellow pollen which was made into loaves known as <i> pungapunga </i>. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,5</i>)

>  <i> raupo n. </i> a marsh reed common in New Zealand. [<Maori] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,950</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 628; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 404]]

>  <b>WILSON</b> In my spare time I went continually down to our muddy creek among thick blackberry bushes, dark soft raupo and high teatree. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,7</i>)

>  By the banks, the typha angustifolia, or raupo, flourishes. <i>(Nov 5, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  Several days search in a boat in the beautiful bays of Lake Ianthe, where raupo grows rank,.. gave the same results. <i>(May 18, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> On Tutira grew a few acres of tussock-grass (Poa caespitosa) a few score acres of flax (Phormium tenax) and of raupo (typha augustifolia). <i>(1921,Tutira,104</i>)

>  <b>TAIT</b> The peat still grew raupo, flax, niggerheads and rushes. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,24</i>)

>  <b>KEENE</b> Trying not to panic, I ran along the ridge skirting the raupo swamp. <i>(1978,Mimiwhangata Station,25</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [ellipt. for raupo hut or cottage]

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> <i>(Mar 20, 1840,Journal</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> Reached Mr. Lusk's raupo cottage about 1/2 past 12.) <i>(Oct 26, 1850,Auckland Journals,30,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LUSH</b> Reached Lusk's raupo about 9. <i>(Apr 25, 1851,Auckland Journals,1971</i>)

### raupo bread
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  'Smell'.. 'Musky. Spicy. Raupo bread and a horseblanket spread on a summer hillside.' <i>(1999,Sport,40</i>)

### raupo church
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  For several years previous the Roman Catholics of the district were without a clergyman, and the only indication of Catholic worship in the parish was a rather delapidated [sic] old raupo church erected by some natives a few years before, at the instigation of Father McDonald, brother to the popular Father Walter of Howick. <i>(Aug 5, 1889,Bay of Plenty Times,4</i>)

>  The earth which covered the bodies of the brave of both races who had fallen in battle was still without vegetation, and the rough, hard boards, carved by loving, faithful comrades, indicated the names of the fallen in the little graveyard which surrounded the raupo church at Rangiriri. <i>(Apr 5, 1909,Taranaki Herald,7</i>)

>  .. in a short space who will there be that can recollect the raupo church on Mount Elliot ... <i>(Feb 27, 1864,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  A raupo church, 25 x 18 feet, was built, ... <i>(1940,Matthews of Kaitaia,58</i>)

### raupo cottage
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Has about 35 acres under cultivation; all fenced with 5 sod ditch and bank; 3 acres manuka cleared; wooden house and outhouses erected; a considerable quantity of draining done; raupo cottage erected on north side of farm. <i>(Feb 28, 1877,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

>  AUCTION SALES. PROFITABLE INVESTMENT. <i>CONNELL & RIDINGS</i> WILL SELL BY PUBLIC AUCTION, At their Mart, Queen-street, on WEDNESDAY, 2nd August, at 11 o'clock, unless previously disposed of .. 1 ? acres of land fenced and cultivated, and well stocked with choice fruit trees, together with a Raupo Cottage thereon; of this lot immediate possession can be given. <i>(Jul 29, 1848,Daily Southern Cross,1</i>)

### raupo house
 
 <i>n.</i> [also raupo-house]

>  140 acres of land had been laid down in grass, for which he claimed GBP140; five head of cattle lost, GBP50; two cows, GBP30 .. raupo house, GBP60 ... <i>(May 2, 1865,Daily Southern Cross,5</i>)

>  He had not been gone above a few minutes when a loud scream was heard from the raupo house. <i>(Jul 24, 1915,NZ Truth,9</i>)

>  Another measure which has excited surprise and alarm in this District, is called the Raupo House Ordinance, by which the Governor is empowered to issue a proclamation fixing a day after which all houses constructed wholly or in part of thatch, reeds, flax, and other materials extensively used in building, are subjected to the enormous tax of twenty pounds per annum. <i>(Apr 2, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  Mrs Bowen lived with her parents in a raupo house until her father drowned in the gusty harbour. <i>(Oct 30, 1900,Star, The,1</i>)

>  The immigrants by the Amelia Thompson and Oriental squatted down on land near the beach at the foot of Mount Eliot, living in tents and raupo houses built by the Maoris. <i>(Jul 2, 1891,Taranaki Herald,1</i>)

>  JOSEPH WYLIE SENR., 280 acres - About 30 acres in cultivation; fenced with 4 sod ditch and bank, set with thorn quicks, at present engaged in ploughing and clearing; good garden; resides in raupo house, intends erecting a wooden house; has plough horses[,] 2 cows, and states he has cattle on the way from Auckland. <i>(Feb 28, 1877,Bay of Plenty Times,2</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Gave instruction for the erection of a small raupo house for any of us who might be up there. <i>(Mar 29, 1831,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,174,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> It was determined ... to erect some raupo houses. <i>(Sep 3, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,329,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> The natives had prayers this evening by themselves, and while singing the hymn, the Europeans, who were near us in a raupo house, and who rank amongst the New Zealand merchants, struck up their vocal powers, and gave us the well known ballad, Old King Cole ... <i>(Nov 14, 1833,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,347,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> A raupo-house, forty feet by fourteen, was erected [by the New Zealanders] in a few days; in which both [missionary] families lived during the first year. <i>Ibid.</i> 205 ... to engage the natives to build raupo, that is, rush-houses; .. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),176</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> it [an earthquake] made our raupo house creak & shake <i>(May 1843,Journal,18</i>)

>  Each labouring emigrant has had one-eighth of an acre allotted to him ..., and with the assistance of the natives some raupo-houses (a typha, sort of bullrush) are springing up. <i>(Apr 1847,Establishment of New Plymouth Settlement,65</i>) [NOTES: 1847 Apr Establishment of New Plymouth Colony in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 65]

>  They have a large raupo house, very roomy and very draughty. <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/81</i>) [NOTES: (H.R.Richmond)]

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The</i>)

### raupo hut
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MILLER</b> Surrounded by a rough, genial camaraderie of native attendants, surveyors, sawyers and a sprinkling of ex-convicts, Jerningham now lived in a large <i>raupo</i> building with a long table down the centre and a cask of rum at each end for each to help himself from. <i>(1958,Early Victorian NZ,56</i>)

>  They return to the filthy raupo hut, to occupy an apartment (in which dogs and fleas struggle for the mastery) with all and sundry who choose to spread their mat or blanket there - and so from year to year. <i>(Jul 11, 1865,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  The procession now moved forwards to the rendezvous - a kind of square formed by the ranges of low raupo huts and tents. <i>(Aug 7, 1858,Hawke's Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  Pieces of raupo hut, some in a state of good preservation, were found, while close by were the remains of a fence or palisade, of long pointed stakes of manuka, bound together with vines. <i>(Oct 30, 1915,Evening Post,9</i>)

>  The Turuturu-mokai redoubt was but a tiny work, so small that the officer in charge, Captain Ross, had to live in a raupo hut built outside the walls. <i>(Oct 22, 1906,Star, The,4</i>)

>  When we first came to New Zealand the Maoris would retire to their raupo huts as soon as it got dark, being frightened of the 'taipo' or devil. <i>(May 29, 1935,NZ Free Lance,18</i>)

>  <b>MRS WILLIAMS</b> Within an enclosure of paling stood our raupo hut, which had, except in shape, the appearance of a beehive. [15] On Sunday, Mr. Williams opened another raupo hut for a chapel. <i>(Sep 15, 1823,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1823 Sep 15 Mrs Williams Journal in 1940 Tales of Pioneer Women (1988) 14, 15]

>  <b>SARAH MATHEW</b> It contains a number of Rapoo houses or huts, and one of the neatest looking is a church where native missionaries perform the services. <i>(Apr 12, 1840,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1840 Apr 12 Sarah Mathew Journal in 1940 Founding of NZ 109]

>  <b>LUSH</b> ... an empty Raupo cottage which a Captain had lately vacated, and this he had immediately fitted up with bedding ... Cooper escorted me to my Raupo hut - where I had a glorious night's rest - <i>(Mar 14, 1866,Waikato Journals,72,1982 ed.</i>)

>  The excess of my care of both bottles caused me to place them on a shelf in the raupo hut ..., and one day some `old hands' (surveyors' men) were having dinner in the hut ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/173</i>)

>  <b>COAD</b> Lord of his own lands, king of his own castle; at least, owner of his own raupo hut. <i>(1931,Such Is Life,29</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> And the last of the raupo huts/ Let down its light behind./ <i>(1937,Selected Poems,48,1984 ed.</i>)

>  Their first act of protest was to burn the raupo huts in which the surveyors were camped, but to their honour, it must be recorded that before doing so they removed to the boats lying upon the beach, all the food and the instruments belonging to the surveyors, for they admitted that what was not produced from the land did not come under their jurisdiction. <i>(Sep 24, 1896,Star, The,4</i>)

>  The bell tents, raupo huts, cook and storehouses were dotted about on the tops and sides of the hillocks, and from one point of view alone I counted 40; there were two rows of 8 bell tents each on the top of a hillock which had been an old Maori pa and which was shaded by luxuriant karaka trees. <i>(Jan 20, 1881,Nelson Evening Mail,2</i>)

>  Clinging tenaciously to his raupo hut, old Patrick Carr saw no reason why he should vacate it at the behest of the authorities. <i>(Jul 25, 1929,NZ Truth,9</i>)

>  Moreover, the Church enshrines a Wesleyan tradition in Wellington that began in 1840 in a raupo hit on the Hutt River. <i>(Dec 11, 2002,Capital Times,2</i>)

### raurekau
 
 <i>n.</i> Coprosma australis (formerly <i> C.grandifolia </i>), =BULLOCK'S KAI Maori

>  At noon we stopped at a small settlement called Kiatoka, where we made a meal of some <i>tuies</i> toasted in the leaf of a tree called <i>herarekau</i>, these leaves will not burn, and they are wrapped round the bird so that none of the fat can escape, the bird cooked in this manner is really very delicious. <i>(Jun 29, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  We had some eels for breakfast, cooked in a way which was new to most of us, called kohe or kope, and which is done in the following manner:- fern stalks are run down the whole length of the fish (which is not skinned or in any way prepared for cooking) from the mouth to the tail - then two eels thus skewered are wrapped in leaves of the raurekau tree, and tied together with flax, when they are roasted before the fire with one end resting on the ground and the other leaning against a stick supported in a horizontal position in front of the fire for the purpose. <i>(Sep 4, 1850,New Zealander, The,3</i>)

>  .. I will not wrap up my speech in raurekau leaves, but say that this man Rusden is telling lies! <i>(Mar 12, 1884,Wanganui Herald,2</i>)

>  I visited Day's Bay for the first time last week; and, within half a mile of the sea, and below 150 feet above sealevel, I saw, without leaving the footpath, the following trees, shrubs, etc. : - Koromiko (several species), kohekohe, mahoe,.papauma (not Griselinea), kotukutuku, manuka (Leptospermum scoparium), karamu, taupata, raurekau ... <i>(Aug 29, 1912,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  <b>KELLY</b> [The eels] are then covered with the broad leaves of the raurikau (or bullock's kai as it was known by the early settlers). <i>(1892,Journey Through Waitara,3</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The officiating priest stuck a twig of the shrub (<i> raurekau </i>) in the middle of the stream ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,45</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> It was an appetising display ... a mound of potatoes, haangi steamed, ... alternated with stacks of eels enclosed in wrappings of `raurekau' leaves (coprosma grandifolia), baked on a framework of rickers over a clear fire, ... delicate of flavour. <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,253</i>)

>  <b>MASON</b> An old rock garden, writhing with weeds. A jungle of raureka and gorse, ... <i>(1970,End of the Golden Weather, The,27</i>) [NOTES: 1970 Mason `Golden Weather' in 1981 Solo 27]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i> Coprosma [australis (The Southern Coprosma)]</i> ... leaves ... broad ... Maori name Raurekau. [1907, <i>C. grandifolia</i>] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,394,194-, p. 421 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(1949,Landfall,3/1: 31</i>)

>  <b>WODZICKI</b> Miss Mason identified the leaves of the following plants [Egmont National Park]: ...both leaves and fruit or seed of haumakaroa (<i> Nothopanax simplex </i>), ...raurekau (<i> Coprosma grandifolia </i>), ...and broadleaf (<i> Griselinia littoralis </i>)... <i>(1950,Introduced Mammals of NZ,164</i>)

>  RAUREKAU/ <i> Coprosma australis </i>/ <i>(1982,Field Guide to Common NZ Trees & Shrubs,26</i>)

>  <i> C[oprosma] australis raurekau </i> <i>(1975,Tane,21/8</i>) [NOTES: (Wright Vegetation Mt Maungatawhiri)]



 
 <i>n.</i> rangiora, Brachyglottis repanda [ORIGIN: Maori]

 <i>n.</i>

### rauriki
 
 <i>n.</i> <i> Sonchus oleraceus </i>the prickly sow thistle [Maori] MAORI CABBAGE (cp PUHA) [ca1935 Havelock.]

>  The little boys often carried to their father for his mid-day lunch ... a basin of boiled rauriki (sow-thistle), which was used as a vegetable. <i>Ibid.</i> 207 For vegetables there was plenty of pora, the Maori turnip, and puwha (rauriki) or sowthistle. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,78</i>) [NOTES: Written of 1940]

>  LEAFLETS FOR GARDENERS AND FRUITGROWERS. No.2.- MOTH-CATCHING PLANT, CRUEL PLANT, CODLIN MOTH PLANT (aranja albens). BY T.W KIRK, F.L.S., &c., Lond. .. I have reared specimens from groundsel, meliolotus, and the recently introduced Neogeora burr, while the common sow thistle or rauriki is everywhere attacked, and seems to be the favourite food plant. <i>(Apr 4, 1895,Otago Witness,4</i>)

>  Snails, meaning the shellback tribe, do not harbour under weeds so much as slugs, but they shelter among such things as cabbages, and large weeds like rauriki; but look for them more beneath hedges. <i>(Jun 14, 1913,Evening Post,12</i>)

>  The native wild cabbage and `rauriki' (sow thistle) were used as vegetables until gardens had been made. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,21</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i> S[onchus] oleraceus </i>, Linn. ... Sow-thistle; Pororua; Rauriki. ... Perhaps not truly native. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,338</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> SOUR THISTLE [SOWTHISTLE] (<i> Sonchus oleraceus /) is frequently called milk thistle, from its milky juice, and in the north is quite commonly given its Maori name of Rauriki. It is exceedingly common and well known in all gardens and cultivated fields. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,209,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(1944,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn)</i>)

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> By vegetables, the Maori ... tends to think of wild turnip-tops, wild water-cress, or puha (known to pakehas as the sow-thistle weed or rauriki) boiled up with meat and potatoes ... <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,97</i>)

>  <b>DE MAUNAY</b> By vegetables, the Maori ... tends to think of wild turnip-tops, wild water-cress, or puha (known to pakehas as the sow-thistle weed or rauriki) boiled up with meat and potatoes ... <i>(1949,Huntsman in His Career, The,122</i>)

>  <b>LYON</b> The younger pigs did little harm, except to graze on the raoriki, which the Maori boils and as `puha' uses as cabbage ... . <i>(1952,Faring South,198</i>)

>  <b>WALL</b> <i> Sowthistle </i>. Here I note a case of what is called `folk etymology'. I hear this native plant (and introduced weed) as `sour thistle'. The speaker mistrakenly supposes the plant to be named from its taste. It is actually edible and much used by the Maoris as rauriki, corruptly `radicky'. <i>(1958,Queen's English, The,48</i>)

>  <i> rauriki n -s [Maori,</i> fr. <i> rau </i> leaf + <i> riki </i> small] <i> NewZeal </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1886</i>)

>  ... the sow thistle, better known as `rauriki' in the Wellington district, which, marketed [in East Coast and Rotorua districts] as `puha' finds a ready sale. <i>(Nov 22, 1962,Evening Post,72</i>)

>  If he were sure he could recognise it, writes Mr T. P. Moloney, he would gather rauriki and get his wife to cook it, for he enjoyed it years ago in Maori cooking, in the King Country, where it was called by the pakeha `Maori cabbage.' <i> Rauriki </i> is the common succulent thistle, growing 2ft to 3ft high, that the children gathering it for pet rabbits and guinea pigs, call milk thistle, because of the abundant flow of milky white sap from it. Actually there are two species' Sonchus oleraceus, whose lobed leaves are only mildly prickly and flat, or at the most very slightly waved at the edges, and S. asper, very similar except that the leaves are much more prickly, and are waved or crimped around the edges. The flowers are yellow, rather like those of a dandelion, about three-quarters of an inch in diameter. <i> Chewing Gum </i> According to some people, rauriki is the Maori name of the former and puwha of the latter, but I do not know that the Maoris went in for such nice distinctions. Different tribes had different names for the same thing. Puwha, as well as being one of the names for the sow thistle, seems to have been also used sometimes as a general term for any cooked greens, of which sow thistle was the favourite. <i>(Jan 7, 1963,Otago Star</i>) [NOTES: (I.W.T. Munro on `Maori Cabbage')]

>  At Oxford, Makereti (Maggie Papakura) wrote: `I myself gather the sow thistle here and cook it to eat, as it reminds me of our own puwha, of which I am very fond. Puwha was used only as a vegetable ... The succulent stems contained a bitter milky juice which came to the surface when the flower tops were plucked off. This was left, on a sunny day, for a few hours, then gathered between the thumb and first finger, adding the thick creamy mass of several plants together. This was used as a sort of chewing gum, called pia or ngau, which tasted very bitter at first. But after a little chewing, the bitterness disappeared, and this gum was much enjoyed, especially by the women, who vied with each other in seeing who could make her pia crack the loudest.' <i>(Jan 7, 1963,Otago Star</i>)

>  <i> rauriki [=] sow thistle [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,63</i>)

>  <i> rauriki [=] sow thistle [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,63</i>)

>  rauriki n. NZ = PUHA. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,628</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 404]]

>  A decoction made from the <i> rauriki (Sonchus)</i>, and the expressed juice of the `pig's ear' (<i> Mesembryanthemum </i> sp.) were used locally ... <i>(1905,TrNZI,37/67</i>)

[Is the <i> or </i> disjunctive or correlative] <i>(1952,Faring South,195</i>)

### rautawhiri
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> `From this point back to Moawhango the track, for some mile, leads through a lovely piece of bush where again native mistletoe rautawhiri and other lovely shrubs were in bloom.' <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails</i>) [NOTES: c1880s an anonymous tourist in 1959 L. Masters Tales of the Mails 49]

>  Amongst smaller trees and shrubs the most prominent are the horoeka(<i> Panax crassifolia</i>), and its congeners <i> Panax simplex</i> and <i> P. edgerleyi</i>, both exhibiting such excessive luxuriance as frequently to require an examination of the fruit before their identification can be determined; the rautawhiri (<i> Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, var. <i> colensoi</i>), the kotukutuku (<i> Fuchsia excorticata</i>), the mako (<i> Aristotelia racemosa</i>), the mapau (<i> Myrsine australis</i>), the pokako (<i> Eloeocarpus hookerianus</i>), also<i> Carpodetus serratus, Myrsine divaricata</i>, both of which are abundant. The remarkable `weeping tree', <i> Myrsine montana</i>, with its crowded recurved branches and small foliage, affords a picturesque effect <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/216</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk)]

### rautini
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) an endangered indigenous shrub <i>Brachyglottis huntii</i> [ORIGIN: Maori, P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 215. *(Chatham Islands) also known as Chatham Island Christmas tree]

>  A noticeable feature of the bush just now is the rautini (senecio Huntii), tree groundsel, indigenous to the island. <i>(Jan 24, 1902,Star, The,4</i>)

>  These plants are classified as nationally critical, meaning they are at very serious risk of becoming extinct .. Rautini (<i> Brachyglottis-huntii </i> ): A rare tree daisy, sometimes known as Chatham Island Christmas tree. <i>(Mar 28, 2009,Dominion Post,A11</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> In the neighbourhood of these [Chatham Island] bogs the margin of the forest often consists entirely of the rautini (<i>Senecio Huntii</i>), a mag-[123]nificent tree-groundsel, which produces immense bunches of yellow flower-heads. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,122</i>)

>  <b>SHAND</b> Inihina - Hinahina or Mahoe, in Maori - was considered the best wood for the rubber [in firemaking]; but Karamu, Karaka, Ake, Rautini, and Kokopere (Maori, Kawakawa) were used as the <i> Kahunaki</i>, or grooved piece of wood. <i>(1911,Moriori People of the Chatham Islands, The,8</i>)

>  <i> Senecio Huntii </i> F. Muell .. Rautini. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/144</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> <i> Senecio</i> spg <i> Senecio .. Huntii</i> .. This tree-groundsel has a stout erect trunk .. RAUTINI. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,68</i>)

>  <b>HOLMES</b> <i> Senecio Huntii</i> (Rautini) - this endemic tree grows in the natural forest [at the south of Chatham and Pitt Islands] .. Flower-heads are clusters of yellow on almost every branchlet. <i>(1984,Chatham Islands,93</i>)

>  Unfortunately some rautini (<i>Brachyglottis huntii</i>) in the south-east were dead or showing severe dieback ... <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 7</i>)

>  Status : Nationally Endangered Species : Brachyglottis huntii Authority : Brachyglottis huntii (F.Muell.) B.Nord. Qualifier : CD, RC, HI, IE Family : Asteraceae Common Name : Rautini, Chatham Island Christmas tree Synonyms : Senecio huntii F.Muell. <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.nzpcn.org.nz/nz_threatenedplants/detail.asp?PlantID=55]

### rawaru
 
 <i>n.</i> [ORIGIN: Maori] (See [cod](chi-con.md#cod),[blue cod](bla-bri.md#blue-cod))

>  cod fish - rao'oroo [rawaru] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,113,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 113. Also >1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 286 (|= macron precedes) cod-fish, ra|ooroo [rawara]]

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> KOKOPU (<i> Galaxias fasciatus</i>). ... `Kokopu,' Dr. Hector says, `is the general Maori name for several very common fishes in the New Zealand streams and lakes, belonging to the family of <i> Galaxidae</i>. ... The kokopu of the stream is caught by them with bobs; but is not much cared for. Not more, as a rule, than one or two fish will be found in a pool; half a dozen at the most. It is also called rawaru and para. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,138</i>)

>  <b>HAM,ILTON</b> ... with the following results: 581 eels (from 1ft. to 4ft. in length); 8 dozen flounders, of various sizes, large numbers of aua, or kataka (mullet); about 70lb. or 70lb. weight of pilchard, or mohimohi; a few snapper and kahawai; hundreds of red cod and rarii; and a number of kokopu or rawaru. <i>(1908,Fishing and Sea-Foods,64</i>)

>  PARAPERCIS COLIAS (Forster). <i> Blue Cod; Rawaru</i>. A highly esteemed food fish; secured in water up to 100 fathoms. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,IV/123</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <i> Parapercis colias </i>(Forster). Blue cod. Rawaru. <i>(1938,TrNZI,68/413</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The above list of fish to be caught along the Canterbury coast has some notable omissions, as hoka (red cod) ... though I am not so sure about the rawaru (blue cod). Others omitted are the tarakihi, or terakihi, the moki, the komutumutu (trevalli), the puhaiwhakarua (red-jacket), koiro (conger eel), tuere (blind eel), whai (skate), and the koekohe [sic] (trumpeter). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  ... large numbers of aua or kataha (<i> Agonostoma forsteri</i>); about 60 lb. ... weight of pilchard or mohimohi (<i> Clupea sagax</i>), two varieties; ... hundreds of young red-cod, rarii (<i> Lotella bacchus</i>), and what I believe are young rock-cod, or kokopu or rawaru (<i> Percis colias</i>). The red-cod were from 3 in. to 4 in. in length, and the rawaru, or, as the natives here call them, `toitoi' or `panepane', from 2 in. to 6 in. long. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/319</i>) [NOTES: Mair Fish in Piako River]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE BLUE COD <i> Parapercichthys colias (Bloch & Schneider) </i>... Maori name: RAWARU <i>Ibid.</i> 141 This [Blue Cod], is the fish known to Captain Cook as the `Coal-fish', and it has frequently been referred to by older authors as the `Rock Cod' It was known to the Maoris as `Rawaru' or `Pakirikiri' <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,140</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BLUE COD ...<i> Other names: Parapercis colias</i>; coal fish; rawaru, pakirikiri, patutuki (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,264</i>)

>  <i> Blue cod </i> an edible fish; <i> rawaru</i> <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary,113</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> COD, BLUE (Rawaru) <i> Parapercis colias: .</i>. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,37</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> BLUE COD (Blue weever, rawaru) <i> Parapercis colias </i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,267,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> BLUE COD (<i> Parapercis colias</i>) Rawaru of the Maoris, is our most esteemed food fish. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,69</i>)

>  <b>STARK</b> cod-fish - raooroo [rawaru] <i>(1986,Journal,113</i>) [NOTES: quote from 1827-28 BOULTBEE; grave accent on "o" of "rao"]

 <i>n.</i> kanuka

A scarcely less common plant than [L. scoparium<i>], attaining a greater size (20 feet) ... <i>(1853,Flora NZ,II Flora Novae-Zelandiae I Flowering Plants 70</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Manuka or Manukau (<i> Leptospernum scoparium</i> et <i> ericoides)</i> is the `ti-tree' of the settlers. In one condition it is low shrubbery, not unlike heather, called then Rawiri by the Maori. ... There are red and white varieties. The Maori particularize it as Kahikatoa, when in tree condition. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/195</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### Rawleigh('s) man
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BACON</b> We sat talking, then before she left, the Rawleigh Man called and he stayed until lunch time. He only calls once in six months, and when he does come, it's a major social event. <i>Ibid.</i> 183 RAWLEIGH MAN-A travelling representative of a firm specialising in household requirements ranging from prepared foods to laundry soaps. In country districts, and in towns too, the Rawleigh Man is an institution, not only because of the service he renders, but because he is invariably a cheerful fellow, a good companion to chat with over a cup of tea. <i>(1963,In the Sticks,83</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> Well - not as though I'm a Rawleighs' man or something. I mean I don't have to sell anything. JACKO: Christ! Rawleighs' man! Ever known any? <i>(1985,Shuriken,22</i>)

### ray
 
 <i>n.</i> various fish species (see also STING-RAY, STINGAREE)

>  [species name] rays .. [scientific name] -- [other name(s)] unspecified. See also EAGLE RAY, ELECTRIC RAY, STINGRAY <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WILSON</b> BLIND TORPEDO RAY: see <i>Rays (Numbfish)</i>. <i>Ibid.</i>198 BLIND TORPEDO RAY A rare ray, <i>Typhlonarke aysoni</i>, as he is called in Latin, has the ability to emit sparks and make a needle magnetic - yet not to see. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,28</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> BLIND TORPEDO RAY OR NUMBFISH <i>Typhlonarke aysoni</i> (Hamilton) [ibid. 92]. This uncommon fish has several names, all of which relate to its manner of producing electric shocks, some of which are Numb-fish, Crampfish and Electric Skate. These vernacular names are given by fishermen on account of the fish benumbing or paralysing the person who unwittingly grasps one with his hands. The last name is from the somewhat similar shape to that of a skate or ray, ray being another common term for skates and also the scientific family name. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,90,2nd ed.</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Blind Torpedo-Ray <i>Typhlonarke aysoni</i> (Hutton) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,398</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 398]

>  <b>PAUL</b> In a separate family (Torpedinidae) [from skates] there is the torpedo or electric ray <i>Torpedo fairchildi, similar to an Australian species and to others elsewhere. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,35</i>)

>  [species name] electric ray .. [scientific name] <i>Torpedo fairchildi</i> .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> ray, electric .. .. whairepo .. <i>Torpedo fairchildi, Typhlonarke </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> ELECTRIC RAY <i>Torpedo fairchildi </i>The commonest and largest of the electric rays found in New Zealand. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,74,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [index to common names 283-293] ray, electric 310-27 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> BLIND ELECTRIC RAY <i>Typhlonarke aysoni</i> ... They are endemic to New Zealand ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,75,1984 ed.</i>)

>  [species name] blind electric ray .. [scientific name] <i>Typhlonarke</i> sp. .. [other name(s)] -- <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] ray, blind electric 324-7 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] ray, deepwater electric 315 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (See [grass](go-gra.md#grass),[ray grass](ra-red.md#ray-grass))

### razoo
 
 <i>n.</i> a small or worthless (imaginary) coin; a jot (usu. in neg. constructions) [>ca1920-1930 at least W.H.B. Orsman (brass razoo)] (See [also brass razoo](bla-bri.md#brass-razoo))

>  In reply to Mr. Curtis's further questioning, Maxwell admitted that he had not a razoo to pay the costs of the action against "Truth" if it went against him. <i>(May 20, 1926,NZ Truth,13</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> razoo ... is in currency for a small coin. The term also has wide use .us;in Australia and ... is heard mainly in the negative phrases I haven't a razoo or I haven't a brass razoo for `I have no money at all' <i>(1941,NZ Slang,52</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> New Zealand colloquialisms which are of Maori origin include; ... razoo: small coin; from the Maori raho (a trifle). E.g. I haven't a brass razoo <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),117</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> I hadn't a razoo left. <i>(1946,That Summer,78</i>)

>  <b>MUNDY</b> I haven't got a rarzoo. <i>(1948,There's Gold in Them Hills,93</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> No work, no custom. Nobody's got a razoo left anywhere, especially to buy fish with. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,177</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> When I was up before him that time last year he never found [sc. fined] me any fiver - never found me a dam' razzoo. <i>(1956,Green Kiwi,197,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> And I've got nothing in my pocket ... I haven't got two razoos to rub together. <i>(1964,Bell Call,142</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> razoo: hadn't a razoo left, was penniless. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,302</i>) [NOTES: Pearson Glossary]

>  <b>TURNER</b> <i>(1966,Austral English,133</i>)

>  razoo n. (Sl.) farthing, smallest coin; not a razoo, destitute. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  <b>DUGGAN</b> But the car, she questioned, surely he had some capital in that `Not a razoo, old thing. Borrowed.' <i>(1970,O'Leary's Orchard,100</i>)

>  This year, after two New Zealand tournaments he had not won a razoo. <i>(Dec 3, 1972,Sunday Times (NZ),9/1</i>)

>  razoo n. Austral. & N.Z. inf. an imaginary coin: not a brass razoo; they took every last razoo. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,950</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 629; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 404]]

>  n.(slang). smallest possible coin, especially in phrase not a brass razoo. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  <b>SARGESON</b> And she didn't give a razoo whether they knew already or they didn't. <i>(1976,Sunset Village,53</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> It benefits the petrol people, the shops and the butcher ... But the rank and file ... I don't think it affects us one razoo. <i>(1981,Speaking a Silence,127</i>)

>  Many of his expostulations dated from time spent in the Middle East during World War II: "brass razoo... shickered... buckshee... shufti... doolally" and "going butcher's hook". <i>(Jan 1992,North & South,88</i>)

>  The America's Cup was ours. We did it. Little ol' us, with a couple of bent razoos and some No 8 fencing wire, became only the second country to sail away with what is grandly known as sport's oldest prize. <i>(Feb 20, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C6</i>)

>  We've never had any grants. Not a razoo. <i>(Jun 1992,Metro,73</i>)

### razor gang
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Cabinet razor gang Chairman Doug Kidd, on his frustration in the state-owned enterprise and finance portfolios: We are constantly paying out on dead horses and disposing of the skeletons as they fall out of the cupboard. <i>(Apr 14, 1991,Dominion Sunday Times,8</i>)

>  A razor gang of Prime Minister Jenny Shipley, Deputy Prime Minister and Treasurer Winston Peters and Finance Minister Bill Birch have already met several times to discuss where cuts will be made. <i>(Jul 6, 1998,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Only the Razor Gang - the ministers who "gate-keep" (meaning cut) departmental spending - are saying anything much, and they have clearly all been at Ruth Richardson's old steroid supply. <i>(Apr 5, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  Prime Minister Jenny Shipley said Sir William - also known as King of the Razor Gang or Mr Think Big - would be hard to replace as he had made an outstanding contribution. <i>(Oct 8, 1999,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  [Hdg] Axing of razor gang cheers farmer groups. Agriculture Minister Jim Sutton's plans to axe a producer board hatchet team have been welcomed by farmer groups across the spectrum. <i>(Jan 18, 2000,Evening Post,17</i>)

>  By Scrooge-like behaviour toward the writers they might prepare the public, psychologically, for the wounding pre-Christmas cuts the "razor gang" would inflict. <i>(Jan 7, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  Mr Goff becomes a member of Dr Cullen's new "razor gang" scrutinising departmental spending. <i>(Oct 20, 2005,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

>  Greens co-leader Rod Donald said he did not know what the razor gang would do. <i>(Oct 21, 2005,NZ Herald,A6</i>)

>  The razor gang has been slashing public spending in areas such as health, welfare and super because the cupboard is supposed to be bare. <i>Ibid.</i> 20 In the name of cutting back on government spending, the MPs have agreed to buy their own magazines this year but have rejected appeals from the Parliamentary Service Commission and a razor gang of officials to limit their travel perks. <i>(Oct 14, 1991,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  Bolger had already, however, determined to set up a so-called razor gang to cut spending in three departments - Inland Revenue, Labour and Social Welfare. <i>(Nov 12, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  The ministers and their advisers feared it was a "razor-gang" exercise. <i>(Aug 24, 2001,Dominion,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Marine)

>  In the next hour we moved twice but caught nothing but members of the razor gang, along the way losing about 20 flasher rigs, four jigs and a fair few sinkers and hooks. <i>(Jul 8, 2005,NZ Herald,D19</i>)

### razor mussel
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POWELL</b> RAZOR MUSSEL (<i>Solemya parkinsoni</i>). A thin-shelled bivalve about 2 inches long covered with a dark chestnut coloured shining epidermis which extends beyond the edge of the shell as a scallop fringe. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,19</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> BIVALVES ... . 2. Razor mussel, Solemya <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,78 Fig. 20</i>)

### razor-backed
 
<b>1</b> <i>adj.</i> (Rural) [of ridges] steep and sharp spurred [common in hill country and difficult to muster.]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> .. the spurs and ridges are razor-backed, and the intervening gullies are of a similar formation. <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,70</i>)

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Along the razor-backed ridges of the high paddocks belonging to Bill Waikai a few flocks of goats pursued a carefree existence ... <i>(1962,Mutton on the Menu,84</i>)

>  <b>ALLEN</b> It was steep, razor-backed country, especially the Airimatia section which we called "The Peninsula." <i>(1980,Farm on the Wanganui River, A,7</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> He scanned higher and soon spotted a high, razor-backed ridge snaking to the tops - the razor-backed ridge overlooked Whale creek. <i>(1996,Great Hunting Yarns,190</i>)

### razorback
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a steep-sided, sharp-backed ridge, common in NZ hill country.

>  After walking over about three miles of fern hills, from which, at several intervals, we obtained various fine views of the country we had left behind us, as well as some very pretty peeps at the wooded gullies which ran down from each side of the razor-back ridge on which we were travelling, we entered the forest of Hautere at 7 a. m. <i>(Dec 18, 1850,New Zealand Spectator & Cook's Strait Guardian,4</i>)

>  .. to an ordinary observer, it [the country] would look like a series of ridges of a 'razorback' character. <i>(Aug 1888,NZ Farmer,298</i>)

>  A recently returned explorer, who after many adventures up the main Trunk Line, discovered Mangaweka in a swamp picturesquely surrounded by razor-backs on which even goats would starve ... <i>(Feb 27, 1906,Hawera Star,6</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> .. the softer rock has melted into cones, domes, razorbacks, and peaks. <i>(1921,Tutira,10</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> razorback: a spur on a mountain which falls away to either side 'as steep as a hen's face.' In places it could even be impossible to walk along its very crest. An unnerving piece of geography. [razor back; razorback] <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,66</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a wild pig.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> A lean old razorback with the long snout of the real New Zealand 'Captain Cooker', she was followed by a cluster of tiny offspring ... <i>(1972,Sheep Thief,71</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> Wally had told me that he had no experience in pig country, so when I saw a big pig up a side gully, I thought it would be a good chance to intitiate him into the noble art of wrestling razorbacks. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,111</i>)

>  wild pig: razorback. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a steep-sided, sharp-backed ridge; used in NZ chiefly for a sharp-backed ridge, or for a (wild) boar, a Captain Cooker, from the general English attrib `having a sharp-edged back' (OED

>  For Mr Lilburn, their introduction to his steep razoorback hill-country farm in 1998 was an act of desperation borne of the need to break free of an endless spiral of dispiriting hard work and debt. <i>(Jan 31, 2003,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  <i>Razorback</i> for a narrow steep-sided ridge or hill was used here..some twenty-seven years before the earliest O.E.D. example. <i>(Sep 1959,Tararua,13/47</i>) [NOTES: Cf. Haast 1861 <i>Rep. Top. Nelson</i> 131; Park <i>TNZI</i> 17 : 353]

>  <b>BAINES</b> At length ... from a high `razor-back', I had a magnificent view of the Waikato. <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,247</i>)

>  Supposing the traveller to be standing on a narrow spur, or razor-back leading, to the mountain-top. <i>(1889,TrNZI,110</i>)

>  <b>KELLY</b> On the best, the ridges are wide, on the inferior country they approach to razor backs. <i>(1892,Journey Through Waitara,6</i>)

>  Your section is behind that, You have two razor-backs and a gully and no way in except a cow-track. <i>(1902,NZ Illustrated Magazine,v/379</i>)

>  We entered on a long narrow ridge, like the roof of a church, ... It is what the New Zealand people call a razor-back. <i>(1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,7/91</i>)

>  The troops in December, 1861, marched along the Maori track over [250] the range called the Razorback, ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,1/249</i>)

>  Razor back.- Sharp ridged spur or hill. A very common local name. (C.) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> By the time we reached the end of the ridge which ended by pinching in to a sharp razorback, <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,138,1963 ed.</i>)

>  ... if he were only young again, by God he'd be with us too [working on the road], but looking ruefully at the razorbacks saying `but I'd need baghooks in the arse of my pants to get me up there!' and then `A man cannot live on scenery alone.' <i>(Aug 1954,Here & Now,17</i>) [NOTES: (R.Alley)]

>  razorback NZ & Austral. lean scraggy bullock or cow; steep-sided narrow ridge. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,629</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  At 4,700 ft. the `razorback' disappears, giving place to a rounded hill ... <i>(1910,TrNZI,42/20</i>)

>  I added to my gallery of mental landscapes an impression of a gloomy forest region of stupendous cliffs and birch-covered razorbacks through whose sombre masses a deep swift river darkling ran. <i>(Mar 9, 1899,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [attributive uses below]

>  ... the sharp, razor-back ridges that extend between Waikouaiti River and the Snowy Mountain .. <i>(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,1</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>CARMAN</b> The razor-back track ... . <i>(1925,Broad Stairway, The,174</i>)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> The top of the hill was a huge see-saw, razor-back ridge. ... Even on a fine day most people preferred to ...do the razor-back on foot ... <i>(1927,Follow the Call,111,1975 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> ... we carried Alf down ..., along the snow covered, razorback ridge from the ski hut ... <i>(1976,Pacific Star,141</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a wild pig with a sharply ridged back, a CAPTAIN COOKER or a bullock in poor condition

>  ~ n. bullock in poor condition. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),623</i>)

>  razor-back n. bullock in poor condition. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),16</i>)

>  ... Mrs Whip looked on the razor-back with brimming eye. <i>(Dec 1976,Spleen,6/19</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> An experienced pig-hunter would have classified him as a `razorback' pig, a type which was far more common in the South Island. <i>(1984,Razorback,25</i>)

>  razorback NZ & Austral. lean scraggy bullock or cow; steep-sided narrow ridge. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>POLACK</b> The RAZORBACK is so termed from that part being remarkably serrated; its snout is very much pointed, like to the porpoise. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),II/407</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> The RAZORBACK is so termed from that part being remarkably serrated; its snout is very much pointed, like to the Porpoise. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/407</i>)

### razz
 
 <i>v.</i> to give the raspberry to; to barrack; jeer at; (of dogs) to annoy, provoke (cattle)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> If the wild cattle didn't respond to these tactics on the first try, or try [sic] to break a wrong way, in would go the dogs like a flash to raz them again until they were glad to respond in the way desired. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,218</i>)

>  The crowd. - razzed the same batsman as he stretched a pad persistently to counter the Alabaster spin. <i>(Feb 8, 1961,Dominion,12</i>)

>  Brown told the police that he `sent off the blue' because the trainees had been `razzing' his `Hell's Angels' associates who had supported him <i>(Jul 20, 1971,NZ Truth,21</i>)

### razz up
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> Bill filled the lorry with benzine, went to the cookhouse and razzed the cook up for breakfast. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,172</i>)

### razzing
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MASTERS</b> In the meantime most of the rest of his team of dogs would be sent in to give the offending wild beasts a good razzing. Arnold would sneak in as close as possible to where the hullabaloo was going on and plant himself behind a tree or bank. Directly he thought the cattle had been given a sufficient razzing, he would give a low whistle. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,218</i>)

### RBA
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Reserve Bank Act

>  I can't begin to tell you how passionately some people feel about the Reserve Bank Act 1889... However, since the RBA was passed, both National and Labour politicians have sworn allegiance to it with the sort of fervour that Americans reserve for their flag... The RBA sets out how our central bank is to be run... <i>(Jan 29, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),38</i>)

### RBNZ
 
 <i>n.</i> Reserve bank of New Zealand

>  RBNZ frets about the effects of a slowdown on high-risk markets. <i>(May 18, 2005,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

### RCD
 
 <i>abbr.</i> rabbit calicivirus disease

>  However, in some pockets, especially in Oatgo, numbers were rebounding thanks to rabbits becoming immune to the virus, which was initially called rabbit calicivirus disease or RCD. <i>(Apr 15, 2004,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

### RD
 
 <i>abbr.</i> rural delivery (See [rural delivery](roa-ruv.md#rural-delivery))

>  We can still number by thousands the homes (not all of them R.D.) where meals are cooked on the wood range and where bathing the children means chopping the kindling, lighting the copper ... and bailing it out again. <i>(Oct 1949,Here & Now,17</i>)

>  The first run, RD9 is over for the day. RD8, seventy-nine miles begins after breakfast <i>(1987,MsCellany: Women Writers' Prose & Poetry,25</i>)

>  Next day, the RD man left a note asking us to put our box on the other side of the road for his convenience. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,65</i>)

>  Oh to be a farmer in England, where mail is delivered not to the RD box, but right to the front door. <i>(1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,86</i>)

>  Check if you would get RD out there. <i>(Oct 17, 2001,Lifestyleblock.co.nz</i>)

### RD run
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a rural delivery run [in which mail, newspapers, bread, groceries, farm produce and materials and recently milk are delivered.]

>  <b>GORDON</b> By this stage Snow had given up the farm and taken over the RD run and calling on the old chap was was part of his round. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,136</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> In late 1984 a couple arrived to lease the store and do the RD run, so Pat Harper feels that she really is retired! <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,138</i>)

>  Rural delivery run - for sale. <i>(Dec 7, 2001,Hawke's Bay Property Guide,47</i>)

### RD1
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a national chain of rural retail stores, owned by Fonterra dairy co-operative.

>  RD1 is a wholly owned subsidiary of Fonterra co-operative group and operates 51 dairy specialist rural retail outlets .. A rare opportunity has arisen to join our RD1 store based in New Plymouth. <i>(Jun 12, 2002,Taranaki Daily News,22</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [orig.] a rural delivery address [ now applied widely and symbolically to rural brands eg website, restaurant, vineyards etc.]

>  <b>MUDGWAY</b> Rural Deliveries: RD1. Otane-Pukehau-Anaroa-Argyll East. This rural delivery commenced in 1919 .. RD1.com. <i>(1999,125 Years Off the Beaten Track,16</i>)

>  The label worn by one of Central Otago's newest wines is about as Kiwi as they come. RD1, the wine is called, and the illustration is of a galvanised country mailbox, its flap half open. Coming up from RD1 are also a pinot noir, pinot gris and sauvignon blanc. <i>(Jun 9, 2001,Dominion,7</i>)

>  Often spilling out on to the winery lawn, this lunch-only restaurant offers seasonal produce in a slightly more casual ambience than its partner, RD1, across the hallway. ..Vicky Bruhns Bolderson, chef at RD1, Sileni Estates.[cp] <i>(Jul 2001,Cuisine,198</i>)

>  The food and beverage operation includes a fine dining evening restaurant RD1 and Mesa, a brasserie-style lunch operation. <i>(Aug 22, 2001,Dominion,38</i>)

### RD1.com
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a multi-functional world-wide-web site of New Zealand Dairy Group.

>  The rival New Zealand Dairy Group site, RD1.com, has reported more than 12,000 registered users. <i>(Apr 26, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,18</i>)

>  In effect Fencepost.com is competing with the rival dairy site set up by New Zealand Dairy Group, RD1.com, as only one of the two is expected to survive the dairy industry's mega-merger. <i>(May 10, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,14</i>)

>  Mr Allen said RD1.com had been a successful investment for his company. <i>(Dec 7, 2001,Dominion,12</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### REAC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Regional Employment Access Council

>  The most intransigent opposition to Te Roopu Rapu's continuance came from the Department of Labour and local Regional Employment Access Council, which approves funding of Access schemes. According to minutes of an interdepartmental meeting on November 20, chaired by Reac chairwoman Pat Harrison, Labour Department district superintendent ... said that a statement ... `that funding by Reac ... was embarrassing'. <i>(Apr 9, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),18</i>)

### reactor
 
 <i>n.</i> audience reactor

>  <b>Alastair Bull</b> Labour's Helen Clark was next with 6mins 29secs, though her effectiveness was hard to judge as her reddish coat suspiciously clashed with the colour of TV3's worm - now called an "audience reactor". <i>(Aug 12, 2005,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: accessed 04/01/06]

>  ..featured the "worm" - an audience reaction gauge (now called the Reactor) - which was dumped by TVNZ for this election. <i>(Aug 12, 2005,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

>  "keep your hands on your Reactors," the stage manager has told them sternly. <i>(Aug 12, 2005,Dominion Post,A3</i>)

### reader
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LELAND</b> reader: Literacy is assumed, but not required for this position, an academic rank corresponding with a U.S. PROFESSOR (who is not a department head). Another misleading N.Z. name for the same rank is Associate Professor. For those familiar with the English academic system a Reader in N.Z. is not the same as a Reader in England; as it is a teaching position in N.Z. rather than a research title as in England. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,85</i>)

### readied
 
 <i>adj.</i> (of a police case or evidence) manufactured [obs.; cf. ready n., ready up v.]

>  Two men (strangers to each other) recently came out of the Police Court after listening to evidence in a case. Said one: `It looks like a "readied" case'; to which the other replied: `Yes.' They said no more and went their several ways; but the joke of the matter lies in the fact that it came out later that the first man intended to libel the police, while the second sought to libel the defence. <i>(Aug 6, 1910,NZ Truth,267/7/2</i>)

### ready
 
 <i>n.</i> a have-on

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> I remembered that old saying `If you're not one up you're one down.' I am sure, however, that the auditor sensed that this was a bit of a `ready'. <i>(1975,Milford Road,120</i>)

### ready up
 
 <i>v.</i> (?) to contrive (in an underhand way) {??here ?ready cash], to arrange illegally cf OED Racing: to prevent (one's horse) from winning .... to secure a handicap in another race (1887), P: prepare, contrive illicitly or not honourably: Austr: 1893 (Morris) (as a noun, `conspiracy').

>  [Horse-racing] No matter how we readied up things .... some of us were getting pretty stoney. <i>(1896,Tom Bracken's Annual,38</i>)

>  <b>FANNING</b> They have no more notion of straight running then the jockeys in a `readied-up' race at an unregistered meeting. <i>(1910,Players and Slayers,25</i>)

### ready-cut
 
 <i>n.</i> sliced bread

>  <b>FINLAYSON</b> [A Maori speaks:] Mum baked the bread and Maori scones there. Somehow the bread she bake taste good. Look at the ready-cut there for the kids' school lunch tomorrow. <i>(1966,Brown Man's Burden,119,1973 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 'The New House']

### ready-made
 
 <i>n.</i> a tailor-made cigarette, a factory-made (cigarette) [ca 1935 at least (Havelock) -->ca1938 espec. Maori (Marlborough)]

Cigarette Paper - the thinnest of thin cigarette paper. You'll never want a ready-made cigarette again when you discover how much more you like your own. <i>(Apr 5, 1913,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  If you're a cigarette man.. you know what time it is by how many you've rolled.. No matter if you've bought nothing but ready-mades before you get busted. You'll roll them one-handed after a while. <i>(Jan 1952,Here & Now,2(2) : 32</i>)

>  [Advt] I want a tobacco with character. I've been used to 'ready made' cigarettes. <i>(Dec 9, 1940,Dominion,10</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> an off-the-peg garment

### rearea
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BULLER</b> ANTHORNIS MELANURA. (BELL-BIRD.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Mako, Makomako, Komako, Kokomako, Korimako, Kohimako, Kokorimako, Titimako, and Kopara. Of the above names, Korimako is most generally used by northern and Makomako by southern tribes. The Ngatiawas call this bird Rearea; and the natives of the Bay of Plenty distinguish the male and female as Kokorohimako and Titapu. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,91,{1888 2nd edn 1/85} ed.</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> = raring, in rearing to go (?from NZ ear/air merger)

 <i>n.</i>

<b>2</b> <i>adj.</i> anxious

### rearing shed
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shed in which newborn calves are reared.

>  Tony Gumboot ..it doesn't explain why some calves get spring eczema while still in the rearing shed and not been out on pasture. <i>(Feb 9, 2001,Fencepost.com</i>)

>  .. I haven't seen spring eczema in calves which are solely confined to a rearing shed, ie no access to outside at all. <i>(Feb 13, 2001,Fencepost.com,Hopkinson</i>)

### rebel
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical) a hostile Maori; an anti-Crown Maori, esp. one captured in Hauhau wars and sent to Dunedin jail [also adjective/attributive use below]

>  At a meeting of the Magistrates this day, it was decided that Mr. Spain should proceed to the coast, and have an interview with the rebel chiefs; he is the only man that can settle the disputes about land, and maintain a good feeling with the natives. <i>(Jul 15, 1843,Daily Southern Cross,2</i>)

>  This officer's advancement to the rank of Commander was made a reward for his conduct in command of a breaching battery, in an attack made upon a rebel New Zealand chief, named Kawiti, whose strongly fortified pah, situated many miles inland, was stormed on the 11th of January, 1846, after several days of labour and fatigue, and triumphantly carried, notwithstanding a desperate fight of four hours. <i>(Mar 2, 1850,New Zealander, The,2</i>)

>  Calling them traitors and factious demogogues, on evidence that a sober-minded man would have regarded with suspicion, he sent down Lieutenant Shortland with a body of troops to overawe the supposed rebels. <i>(Apr 9, 1842,New Zealand Gazette & Wellington Spectator,3</i>)

>  <b>GRACE</b> Had the Treaty of Waitangi been fully and faithfully carried out, the war would not have occurred; instead, it has been ignored until now that it is useful to make rebels of the Natives. <i>(Oct 23, 1860,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Oct 23 T.S.Grace letter in Grace Pioneer Missionary (1928) 94]

>  <b>MORGAN</b> A body of men has lately been formed to, whose express purpose will be to scour the bush, and follow up the rebels. <i>(Aug 1863,Journal,68,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TONKIN</b> Altogether some 76 rebel savages were transferred to Dunedin gaol, among them being the chief Taurau - <i>(1980,Dunedin Gaol,16</i>) [NOTES: c1875 Otago Guardian in 1980 Tonkin Dunedin Gaol 16]

>  Orakau [a poem] The swarthy rebels answered with a fierce defiant shout,/ `Ka whawhai tonu! Ake[e-grave]! Ake[e-grave]! Ake[e-grave]!' [fn] We will fight for ever, and ever, and ever. <i>(Jul 22, 1876,Saturday Advertiser,3</i>)

>  I used to hear how [Te Kooti] was a rebel and all that, and I didn't think I was connected with him. <i>(1987,NZ Journal of History,17</i>) [NOTES: (Binney) 17 -->[Hei Ariki Algie, 1983 Nov 26]]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> From these valleys ... the rebel natives under Rangihaeata were driven in the skirmishes of 1846 <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,112</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (usu. as the Rebels) an Otago-based netball franchise competing in the National Bank Cup.
