### receiving yard
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an outer yard in a set of stock yards, where stock are first driven.

>  CORRESPONDENCE. EGMONT A. AND P. SHOW GROUNDS. The few improvements which were asked for at the annual meeting last year have not been given effect to. Where is the circular judging pen you speak of? Have the committee erected a race for the pig pens and a proper place for loading? Has the drinking trough in the receiving yard been shifted? <i>(Nov 16, 1907,Hawera & Normanby Star,7</i>)

>  The dimensions of the yards, pens, &c., are as follows:- Receiving yard, 63ft. by 45ft.; drafting yard, 72ft. by 50ft.; sale pens (six) each 18ft. by 18ft. <i>(May 17, 1878,North Otago Times,2</i>)

>  At milking time, 6 a.m. and 2.30 p.m., the cows are gathered into the receiving yard, from which twenty at a time are drafted into the shed yard. <i>(Aug 26, 1886,Taranaki Herald,3</i>)

>  The City Engineer, reporting at the City Council last night on arrangements for the extension of the Municipal Abbatoir .. stated that the work consisted principally of (1) extension of the stormwater culvert, construction of a large receiving yard for cattle, to be partly covered ... <i>(Nov 4, 1910,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  WAITAHUNA FARMERS' CLUB. .. The President stated that a receiving yard was still required, but it would not cost much to put it up. <i>(May 27, 1891,Tuapeka Times,3</i>)

>  Councillor Melton said a receiving yard, to be attached to the pound, was much wanted, and there was sufficient material left from the old pound to make such a yard - the putting up of which, together with filling up the post holes at the old pound, could be done for about GBP1 10. <i>(Nov 25, 1868,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> [The sheep yards] should consist of at each end, forcing pen, and drafting race. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,16</i>)

>  A very large yard which sleep come into first when brought into the sheep yards (q.v.). (C.) <i>(Nov 18, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  In general large drafting yards embody the following subsections or divisions ... The receiving yards, into which the sheep enter, which usually have a large capacity .. <i>(Jul 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,7</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> Receiving yards. These must have the capacity to take incoming mobs. They communicate with the forwarding yards, or diamond pens, which are much smaller, and lead up to the crush pens or forcing pens. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,120</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> They [sheep yards] should consist of receiving yards at each end, forcing pen and drafting race. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,16</i>)

>  The receiving yards usually communicate with the forwarding yards or pens. <i>(Jul 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,07</i>)

>  <b>GASCOIGNE</b> The pattern of present-day construction has come down to us directly from these emergencies of our early settlement - the great receiving yards , the 'diamond' or 'crown' pen leading into the crush pens with the drafting race and gates opening into yards for the finally separated sheep. <i>(1965,From the Top of a Hill,12</i>)

>  The long summer morning's muster, beginning before the dawn, has finally ended at about 11 a.m. with the big herd of cows and calves safely confined behind the slip rails of the receiving yard. <i>(Mar 23, 1967,NZ Farmer,33</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> receiving yard: the large pen into which sheep or cattle arrive when brought into the yards for close handling. Naturally it adjoins the holding paddock. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,66</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a newly arrived prisoner, prisoner at arrival [(prison)]

### reck
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> reck a bad thing; eg `That stuff you got off Sibby, I tried it, it's reck, eh.' [Alison Gray] <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,92</i>)

### recolonial
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  Our chances are certainly better than they would have been during the depths of the recolonial era. <i>Ibid.</i> C5 These problems are less likely to be resolved if we fail to recognise their recolonial roots. <i>(Oct 28, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C1</i>)

>  The recolonial era hid or denied difference, emphasised harmony, and played up Britishness. Belich's book traces a Great Tightening that shaped much of New Zealand life during recolonialism. <i>(Nov 17, 2001,Dominion,11</i>)

### recolonisation
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Belich divides the period covered by the volume, the 1880s to the present, into three broad phases: the progressive colonisation which was the dominant feature of <i>Making Peoples</i>; the rise of recolonisation, which tightened the relations between Britain and colonies up to the 1960s and made them co-owners of imperial culture and heritage; and finally decolonisation, the period since the 1960s in which New Zealand has achieved economic diversification, greater independence in foreign policy and a great socio-demographic "coming-out" of difference. <i>(Nov 17, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

>  But one pattern looms high above the rest, recolonisation, whose rise, reign and fall is the master variable of modern New Zealand history. <i>(Oct 28, 2001,Sunday Star Times,C5</i>)

>  By recolonisation he means the powerful tightening of economic, social and cultural ties to Britain that began in the 1880s and lasted till the 1970s. It turned New Zealand into "a town-supply district of London" while "London became the cultural capital of New Zealand." <i>(Dec 8, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,12</i>)

### recolonised
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  In fact, New Zealand remained a colony, albiet a privileged one, from the 1880s till the 1960s. Belich dubs this era, which followed four decades of colonisation, as New Zealand's "recolonised" period. It was a time of tightened economic and cultural links to Britain and a role for this country as Britains "town supply district". <i>(Nov 17, 2001,Dominion,11</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a court recount and review of votes cast at an election following the Returning Officer's declaration of the result. [Jim Cameron writes : A recount in the UK is made by the Returning Officer on election night before the result is announced. Electoral Act 1993, s. 180, 181.]

 <i>n.</i> elliptical for red cent in negative construction (still extant) not to have a red cent (cf RAZOO)

### Red Admiral
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka kahukura]

>  We sometimes are surprised at the sudden appearance of a great Red Admiral butterfly in our rooms in midwinter. <i>(Jan 18, 1889,Otago Witness,37</i>)

>  The red admiral, as I have called this butterfly, though it is not the same as the insect of that name so well known to English lepidopterists, is said to live in the larval stage only on the nettle. In the face of the abundance of the mature insects and the scarcity of nettles, this is a statement requiring verification ... <i>(Apr 13, 1899,Otago Witness,61</i>)

>  I have even seen specimens of the large "Red Admiral" butterfly caught in the flowers. <i>(Mar 28, 1893,Bruce Herald,4</i>)

>  New Zealand butterflies and moths are likewise graced with pretty names. There's the Red Admiral (above), known in Maori as the Kahukura or "red cloak"; and the Yellow Admiral, aka the Kahukowhai or "yellow cloak." <i>(Sep 19, 2010,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),31</i>)

>  If your last sighting of a Red Admiral is a distant memory, it's likely due to the introduction of the <i>Pteromalus puparum </i> wasp in 1932, reckons butterfly enthusiast Rob Jones. <i>(Sep 19, 2010,Sunday (Sunday Star Times Magazine),31</i>)

>  New Zealand Red Admiral butterfly, characterised mostly by dark red, black and brown colours, usually deposits its eggs on a leaf of the common native nettle. <i>(Oct 28, 1924,Otago Witness,6</i>)

### Red Bands
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) gumboots, particularly those shorter forms made by Skellerup since 1958. [proprietary name]

>  Alongside red bands (gumboots) and "Swannis", a black woolly singlet is classic Kiwi-wear, worn by generations of good keen men and women. <i>(Mar 2009,North & South,108</i>)

>  Skellerup Red Bands celebrate 50 years as a cultural icon.[hdg] Launched in 1958, the Red Band broke away from previous gumboot designs - it was wider than a normal gumboot, shorter in the leg and able to be 'stepped into' easily. <i>(2008,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.betterbydesign.org.nz/news-and-resources/design-news/news-details?objI...]

>  Fonterra chairman Henry van der Heyden got the equivalent of a knighthood - a DCNZM .. but it wasn't for him.. "It didn't take me long to realise that this isn't about me," he told the NZ Herald. "This is a recognition of all the hard work that the farmers have done .." Well, he would say that wouldn't her Most people seem to, seeing as it's the Kiwi way to be humble or, at least, profess to be. It's that or to be thought to be too big for your red bands. <i>(Jan 2009,Internet - see Notes,2</i>)

>  There must be millions of them that over the last forty years or so have been pulled onto thousands of Kiwi feet, thrust into red bands, work boots, tramping boots and slippers. I'm talking about Norsewear socks. <i>(Oct 17, 2007,Wairarapa News,4</i>)

>  As part of the celebrations, Red Bands will feature in the Skellerup heat for the Rural Bachelor of the year competition at National Fieldays at Mystery Creek. .. We've also been asking Kiwis around the country for some of their memorable Red Band moments... <i>(Jun 16, 2008,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.skellerup.co.nz/fileadmin/newsletter/160608.pdf]

>  The red-band rule of thumb (a waterway that is deeper than a red-band gumboot or too wide to jump) allows for a farmer to opt out of planting these crucial small streams. On most farms, even a large river narrows to the point where it could be stepped across. Similarly there will be a wide point where even a larger river can be crossed wearing red-bands. <i>(Jul 7, 2008,NZ Herald,13</i>)

>  Often there is the hilariously quirky juxtaposition - Taranaki Regional Council general manager Basil Chamberlain seated midstream in a leopard-skin love chair, the water threatening to fill his Red Bands. <i>(2008,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.pukeariki.com/en/Library/rev_c_03.htm]

>  It's Christmas Eve at the bach, the children are dreaming of pavlova, and Santa turns up in Red Bands and a black singlet ... <i>(2004,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.bookcouncil.org.nz/education/brat/bratautumn2004.html]

>  Get out your red bands and swannies, it'll be a wee cracker! <i>(2006,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.port-nicholson-rotary.wellington.net.nz]

>  The contestants all regular attendees at the Stadium designed the concept of the Golden Gumboot while celebrating Mercy's win last Sunday. Racing over 200 metres the contestants are racing in a costume (including mask) of their choice and must wear red bands. <i>(May 23, 2007,Internet - see Notes,1</i>) [NOTES: www.greyhounds.co.nz/aticles/article_2007_05_23_2432.html]

### red beech
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Nothofagus fusca</i>, aka red birch, tawhai-raunui

>  <i>Fagus fusca</i>. - In Auckland this tree is called `black-birch'; in Wellington `red-birch'; in Nelson, Marlborough and Westland it is called `brown-birch'; and in Otago it is called both `red-birch' and `bull-birch.' The most suitable name is `red-beech.' <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/308</i>) [NOTES: (E. Phillips Turner)]

>  The chief indigenous trees of commercial value are: Kauri (<i>Agathis Australis</i>), Kahikatea or white pine ... red and hard beech (<i>Nothofagus fusca</i> and <i>Nothofagus truncata</i>), silver beech (<i>Nothofagus Menziesii</i>), black beech (<i>Nothofagus Solandri</i>) ... <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,578</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>N. fusca (The Red Beech)</i> ... North Island: mountainous districts; South Island: abundant. The <i>Red Birch</i>. A somewhat similar species is <i>N. truncata</i>, the Clinker Beech, often called the Hard-Beech. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,139</i>)

>  <b>LINKLATER</b> Ferns and tree-ferns flourish [on Stewart Island], ... supple-jack entwines your heels, and stinkwood is garlanded with little red flowers. There is red or black beech, there is mutton-bird scrub with thick green leaves that have white undersides, there are, perhaps, a dozen others. <i>(1953,Year of Space, A,203,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Five species of <i>Nothofagus </i>were formerly recognised in New Zealand, but more recently two of these have been combined as varieties of one species. ... <i>Nothofagus fusca</i>: red beech (the timber is reddish in colour). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,121</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> ... red beech (<i>Nothofagus fusca</i>) and hard beech (<i>N. truncata</i>) are regular components of the forests on the crest of the scarp above. <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,157</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Beech ... Red .. tawhairaunui <i>Nothofagus fusca </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/710</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Red or brown beech/birch, `steaming wood', good fill-in wood. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,60</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>NOTHOFAGUS FUSCA</i>. Red beech is widely but discontinuously distributed in the North Island and is dominant in extensive South Island forests. <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,80</i>)

>  (red-beech) <i>(1909,AJHR,C-6, Appendix D</i>) [NOTES: Jourdain Report on the Mokau River (And)]

>  (red-beech) <i>(1909,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Turner Botanical Examination of the Higher Waimarino District in AJHR C-11]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> ... <i>N.fusca</i> and <i>N.Menziesii</i>, called respectively the red and silver beeches, ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,49</i>)

>  <i>(1910,TrNZI,42/</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>(1916,Official Trade Register of Nurserymen</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  Built of red beech slabs, the hut had a tin roof, a clay floor, ... and accommodated eleven (in five sacking bunks). <i>(1971,VUWTC'71,20</i>)

>  <b>Crowe</b> As a timber tree, red beech has been put to much the same uses as hard beech - railway sleepers, mine props, house construction, wharves and bridges. <i>(2009,Which Native Tree?,32-33</i>)

### red birch
 
 <i>n.</i> [(?Wellington, Nelson, Otago, Southland). [1919 July 29] Ver.(WB). = Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W.Best, Otaki (And)]

>  The Tawhai, and Tawhai-rau-nui, or Black and Red Birches, (<i>Fagus Solandri</i> and <i>F. Fusca</i>,) often form large ... trees <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/44</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Nothofagus fusca</i>, Hook. Tawhai Tawhairaunui, Black-birch of Auckland and Otago (from colour of bark), Red-birch of Wellington and Nelson (from colour of timber). --This is a noble tree 60-90 feet high the trunk 5-8 feet in diameter. The timber is excessively tough and hard to cut. It is highly valued in Nelson and Wellington. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,125,3rd ed.</i>)

>  <i> [Notho]fagus fusca,</i> Hook. f. ... Tooth-leaved Beech. Black Birch of Auckland and in part of Otago and Southland. Black or Bull Birch of Lake Wakatipu. Red Birch of Wellington, Nelson, and in part of Otago and Southland. It is not so easy to see why any difficulty should have occured in the identification of this fine timber-tree apart from the misleading tendency of the common names generally applied. The thin yet firm texture of the leaf, the prominent veins, the sharply-toothed margins, are characters that can only be confused with those afforded by a careless observer. Yet merely owing to the use of common names based upon colour, and applied or rather misapplied to the leaves, bark, or wood at the fancy of the bushman, no species has been more misunderstood. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/300</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]

>  English Name Native name Botanical Name Red Birch .. Fagus Fusca Black Birch .. Fagus Solandri White or Silver Birch .. Fagus menziesii <i>(1889,Catalogue, Canterbury Court Exhibition,35</i>)

>  Red Birch of Otago, Black Birch of Nelson, Beech etc (Fagus Menziesii); Black Birch of Otago (F. fusca) White Birch of Otago (F. Solandri)[get full citation] <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/32</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  Tawhai-rau-nui - Red Birch. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/44</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> BLACK BIRCH of Auckland and Otago, RED BIRCH of Wellington and Nelson: HUTU-TAWHAI.- (<i>Fagus fusca</i>.) The true black birch is a noble tree, found from Kaitaia ... to Otago ... <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,15</i>)

>  <b>HETLEY & RAOUL</b> <i>(1889,Fleurs Sauvages</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>kir</b> <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  163 Test piece, red birch (tooth leaved beech) .. .. [525lbs.] <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,19</i>)

>  The timber on the western side consists of red- and white-pine, matai (or black-pine), totara, kawhaka (or cedar), rata, and occasional silver-pine, besides black- and red-birch (<i>Fagus fusca</i>). <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,19</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> <i>Nothofagus fusca</i> <i>(1905,Tree Culture in NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

A noble forest-tree 60-100 ft. high; ... bark dark-brown or black in old plants, deeply furrowed, smooth and greyish-white on young trees; ... rare in Canterbury and eastern Otago. ... <i>Tawhai; Tawhai-rau-nui; Black-birch; Red-birch.</i> ... >  <i>red birch n 3 a </i>: a valuable New Zealand timber tree (<i>Nothofagus fusca</i>) - called also<i> clinker beech, red beech b</i>: the hard wood of this tree <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1901</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> In the forests on the north side of the Waimakariri there are substantial stands of <i>Nothofagus fusca</i>, the red beech, which we called `red birch' in those days [late 1920's]. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,83</i>)

 <i>n.</i> = kamahi

Alps]. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,35</i>)

 <i>n.</i> Nothofagus menziesii, aka silver birch

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Fagus <i>Menziesii</i>, Hook. fil. ... HAB. Mountains of the Northern and Middle Islands. ... Nat. name, `Tawai', Col. `Red Birch' of the colonists. ... A very handsome tree, 80-100 feet high, ... <i>Bark </i>silvery, outer layers peeling off and exposing a red surface. <i>(1853,Flora NZ,I Flowering Plants 229</i>)

>  <b>KIRK</b> RED BIRCH.- (<i>Fagus menziesii</i>.) <i>(1875,Durability NZ Timbers,17</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> The Tawai (<i>Fagus Menziesii</i>), called `red birch' by settlers, is a favourite for fencing when young. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/190</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> <i>Nothofagus menziesii,</i> Hook. Tawhai, Red-birch (from the colour of the bark). --A handsome tree, 80-100 feet high; trunk 2-3 feet in diameter. The timber is chiefly used in the lake district of the South Island. <i>(1883,Handbook of New Zealand,125</i>)

>  [Notho]fagus menziesii, <i>Hook. f. ... Silver Beech. Brown Birch, White Birch, Red Birch. Silver Birch. Although this species exhibits a considerable amount of variation in the shape, toothing and texture of the leaves, it is the most easily recognized of all the New Zealand beeches, especially when the fruit is fairly developed</i> <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/298</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]

>  <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/307</i>) [NOTES: (E. Phillips Turner) see cit. 1 b. above.]

>  <i>Fagus cliffortioides </i>Hook. f. .. Mountain beech or birch. <i>[Fagus] fusca</i> Hook. f. .. Tawhai, black-birch. <i>[Fagus] Menziesii </i>Hook. f. .. Silver or red birch. <i>[Fagus] Solandri</i> Hook. f. .. White-birch. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/144</i>)

>  Colour Coding [heading] ... St Patrick's Day sports organiser, Mr Lyn Thompson yesterday inquired about the difference between red, black and brown birch. It was `clearly' explained for him by a prominent axeman, Mr Bill Evans. The bark of the red birch is silver, [?HWO thus <i>N. menziesii</i>] but the timber is pink when it's green. Brown birch often has black bark, but the green timber is red. Sometimes the bark of the black birch is white, but the timber is yellow and sometimes it's brown when it's green. <i>(Mar 11, 1972,Otago Daily Times,1</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]



 
 <i>n.</i> -

Black Birch of Wellington, Canterbury, and in part of Otago and Southland. White Birch of Nelson, and in part Otago. Black-heart Birch of Otago. White, Black, Red, and Brown Birch of Oxford and Alford Forests. ### red cod
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pseudophycis bachus</i> (also listed as <i>Physiculus bac(c)hus, </i> and in early texts <i>Lotella bacchus</i>), fam Moridae, an often abundant food fish of mainly SI waters; .in early writings under a listing <i>Lotella bacchus,</i> occas. and confusingly referred to as rock cod or yellowtail qqv. [Contrast bastard red cod.]

>  (Black Stewart Island) It was no unusual event ...to land ... Moki, <i>Latris ciliaris;</i> Trumpeter, <i>Latris hecateia</i>; Blue cod or Rock cod, <i>Percis colias</i>; and Red cod, <i>Lotella bacchus.</i> <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> 75. RED COD. ... <i>(Lotella bacchus)</i> is a well known fish on some parts of the coast, being the species that is cured and sold as Findon Haddock at Port Chalmers. <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,115</i>)

>  [Native or Common Name] Red Cod .. [Scientific Name] <i>Lotella bacchus </i> <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  <b>SENIOR</b> There is another fish somewhat resembling [the haddock] cured and sold in Dunedin as the Findon haddock. This is in reality a red cod, and is beautifully coloured. <i>(1880,Travel & Trout,298</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> RED COD (<i>Lotella Bacchus</i>). ... Red-cod ... is a well-known fish on some parts of the coast, being the species that is cured and sold as the Findon haddock. It is a handsome fish, with a brilliant play of metallic colours when alive. <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,16</i>)

>  Red cod (<i>Lotella bacchus</i>) <i>(1890,TrNZI,22/375</i>) [NOTES: (G.M. Thomson)]

>  Of the sea-fishes that are used as food in New Zealand ... while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butterfish, and red-cod are confined to the south. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The snapper, the mullet, and the trevally are essentially northern; while the trumpeter, the red cod, and the ling are just as essentially southern. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,71</i>)

>  ... hundreds of young red-cod, rarii (<i>Lotella bacchus</i>), and what I believe are young rock-cod, or kokopu or rawaru (<i>Percis colias</i>). The red-cod were from 3 in. to 4 in. in length. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/319</i>) [NOTES: (Mair Fish in Piako River)]

>  <i>Physiculus Bachus</i> ... Red Cod. <i>(1911,Records of the Canterbury Museum,1/183</i>) [NOTES: Waite]

>  <i>Hoka</i>, n. <i>Lotella bacchus, red cod. </i> <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,67</i>)

>  PHYSICULUS BACHUS (Forster). <i>Red Cod; Hoka. </i> <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/121</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>BUICK</b> Next in progression came the shoals of red cod - the <i>hoka</i> of the Maori - chased from the [Waitaki] river-mouth by the assaults of the voracious kahawai. <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,176</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The above list of fish to be caught along the Canterbury coast has some notable omissions, as hoka (red cod). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Red Cod <i>Physiculus (Pseudophycis) bachus</i> (Bloch and Schn) (Syn. <i>Blennius</i> and <i>Brosmius venustus</i> Richardson, <i>Gadus</i> and <i>Blennius rubiginosus<i> Richardson) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,403</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 403]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE RED COD <i>Physiculus bachus (Bloch & Schneider)</i> ... Maori name: HOKA. ...When removed from the water the grey colour rapidly gives place to a uniform red: hence the common name `Red Cod'. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,47</i>)

>  <i>red cod n 1</i> : a gadid food fish (<i>Physiculus bachus</i>) of Australia and New Zealand that is reddish gray above and pink beneath but quickly becomes red when removed from the water b : RED ROCK COD <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1901</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>COD, RED </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Physiculus bachus </i>(Family Moridae). (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Red Cod. (c) Other common names: Red Rock Cod (Australia); Cape Cod. (d) Principal localities where taken: Cook Strait, Nelson, Picton ... Lyttelton, Akaroa, Timaru, Port Chalmers, Greymouth. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,17</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> [Title] Cod, red his stout, reddish-grey fish, known to the Maori as <i>hoka</i>, more closely resembles its English counterpart than other New Zealand `cod'. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,70</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> Simon made the acquaintance of barracouta, ling, trumpeter, and rig, and red cod, kelp cod, and rock cod. <i>(1983,Bone People, The,251</i>)

### Red Fed
 
 <i>adj.</i> (derogatory) left-wing, socialist

>  [Hdg] Red Fedism. Akin to Prussianism... Professor J. Macmillan Brown... contended that the ideals of the I.W.W. and the Red Fed element resembled, in many cases, the Prussianism against which we were fighting. <i>(Apr 18, 1917,Wanganui Herald,23</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> When it comes to the pinch you can trust the New Zealander. We don't go for these red-fed ideas here ... <i>(1978,Elver, The,44</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR & HARREX</b> [Caption] The 'Red Fed villains' as they appeared in a rogue's gallery in the Otago Witness on 26 November 1913. <i>(1978,Looking Back,173</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> originally one who supported the socialist ideals and policies of the `Red' Federation of Labour; a RED FEDERAL; (informal) a socialist or leftist; a slightly abusive term for a left-wing `agitator', militant unionist, or other assertive person of the left

>  "It must be the red-feds," someone suggested amidst laughter. <i>(Nov 6, 1928,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  [Caption] A Slap in the Face for the "Red Feds". <i>(Jun 22, 1912,NZ Free Lance,1</i>)

>  It is Town Talk... That a nick-name has been found for Bob Semple's Federation of Labour. It's the "Red Fed."... That the Red-Fed's game is about up in New Zealand. That Waihi strike has just about put their pot on the fire. <i>(Jun 22, 1912,NZ Free Lance,26</i>)

>  It is Town Talk... That Mr. Red Fed Parry will "shake the country from end to end". <i>(Sep 28, 1912,NZ Free Lance,22</i>)

>  The aviator pinned the tribute to the breast of the Right Honourable R. Fedd. "He died for a principle!" <i>(Dec 1, 1920,NZ Free Lance,Xmas Annual 45</i>)

>  Suddenly two women, a red fed. and a loyalist, attacked each other with their umbrellas. <i>(Oct 4, 1912,Grey River Argus,6</i>)

>  No farmer who disapproves of disloyalty and lawlessness, of the foreign agitators who deluded the true working man into law-breaking, could desire to be classed as a Red Fed. <i>(Nov 28, 1914,Grey River Argus,3</i>)

>  Some of the Red Feds are now fed at the State's expense. <i>(Sep 28, 1912,NZ Free Lance,22</i>)

>  <b>BOLLINGER</b> As things turned out, the O.F.O.L. never became much broader than the old 'Red Fed.' <i>(1968,Against the Wind,100</i>)

>  The Waihi affair led to a bit of an "argument" on the wharf yesterday afternoon between a Socialist of the Red Fed brand and a believer in peaceful arbitration. <i>(Nov 14, 1912,Grey River Argus,4</i>)

>  The notorious "Red Feds" gained political representation in the House, and in the longer term were to be a driving force in the emerging Labour Party... <i>Ibid.</i> 33 Most of us were out and out Red Feds. <i>(Jul 28, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  The notorious "Red Feds" gained political representation in the House, and in the longer term were to be a driving force in the emerging Labour Party... We'd always have a piece of red in the lapel of our coats. Most of us were out and out Red Feds. <i>(Jul 28, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  The Red Feds are obviously not included under the general definition. <i>(Feb 15, 1913,NZ Observer,4</i>)

>  McRobie apparently did not count on the `Unity Schemers' and the `Red Feds' burying the hatchet-... <i>(Feb 15, 1913,NZ Truth,399/1/6</i>)

>  RED FED: colloq. male supporter of the 1913 strikers; ... [?quot. or LC's gloss: check] <i>(Nov 29, 1913,Hutt Valley Independent,1</i>)

>  `Red Feds' and force. ... The charge you prefer against the `Red Feds' ... <i>(Jan 14, 1914,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  `If you want a good electric torch I'll get one from the Ossies; they're white, they are. Of course, they call me a bit of a Socialist.' `I heard you were a Red Fed,' interjected my companion. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,90</i>)

>  Have I not sent many Redfeds into Jug. <i>(1918,NZ Black Watch Transport Magazine,3</i>)

>  ANZAC DAY is an excellent thing. It shows these gentry that there is a big body of the public that holds different views from themselves. ... The incident of the flag-burning, too, in Sydney, was very instructive - bitterly instructive to the Red Feds. They must realise that there are many to whom the magic word Unionmakes no appeal. <i>(1921,Auckland University Carnival Book,17</i>)

>  <b>HICKEY</b> [See Red Federation 1925] <i>(1925,Red Fed. Memoirs,48</i>)

>  <b>COAD</b> `They [sc NZ customs officials] are on the look-out for Bolshevists, Red-Feds and I.W.W's,' replied the New Zealander, looking at him suspiciously. <i>(1931,Such Is Life,24</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> ... they were the worst people in Calver Street - the poorest, anyhow, and not well thought of, since John had become a Red Fed and started spending his evenings in Carl Withers' bookshop. <i>(1938,Godwits Fly, The,37,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In contrast to the United Labour Party, the `Red Feds' eschewed political action ... They would fight in the factories ... the strike would be their weapon. Consequently they were bitterly antagonistic to the arbitration system which sought to minimize class antagonism, ... <i>Ibid..</i> 303 [Colonial-N.Z. glossary][?also 320] Red-Fed. Member of the Red federation [check] <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,199</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> The attempt of the `Red Feds' to call a general strike throughout New Zealand failed. <i>(1968,Seddons, The,217</i>)

>  <b>MITCHELL</b> red fed: Non-member of National Party. <i>(1972,Half-Gallon Quarter-Acre Pavlova Paradise, The,183</i>)

>  <b>STEWART</b> Red-feds they call this Labour crowd. The priests. Communists in disguise, they reckon. <i>(1981,Hot & Copper Sky,74</i>)

>  red-fed n. NZ colloq. socialist, left-winger. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,636</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 408][Federation of Labour, founded 1909]]

>  In 1912 the federation pledged support for the disgruntled tramwaymen in Wellington, and the Evening Post made its contribution to history by declaring: `A victory for the Reds in the present quarrel would be a public disaster, possibly on a national scale. The Reds are out to fight ...' The tramwaymen struck and won, and henceforth that first Federation of Labour became known as the Red Federation and its leaders as Red Feds. <i>(May 20, 1988,Dominion,8</i>) [NOTES: (W. P. Reeves)]

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> In contrast to the Federation of Labor [sic], which a hostile press had labelled the `Red Fed,' the Labor party aimed to achieve public ownership ... gradually and by purely political measures. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),60</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> Who cares if he is a red-fed? I've gone a bit bolshy myself after some of the balls ups I've seen out here. <i>Ibid.</i> 138-139 I was like you myself once, a bit of a redfed. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,102</i>)

>  <b>128</b> The cow was mad, Gil thought. Only a madman would want to go to a war unless he had to. These redfeds were always thinking about killing someone; ... <i>Ibid.</i> 152 That crazy young redfed, .... who landed himself in trouble over at the works with his gabbing and got the boot. <i>(1948,Cunninghams, The,1976</i>)

>  I have always prided myself on my British descent and our fair play and am not a Red-fed, but I admire the natives for kicking over the traces and striking on this account. <i>(Mar 9, 1959,Christchurch Star,2</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> `Thirty bob? ... Is that all your dolie old man can afford, thirty bob?' ... to the well-off it seemed that all who worked for wages [in the 1930s] were dolies or red-feds, whatever that was. <i>(1965,Power of Joy,213</i>)

>  ) Look what happened to the Russians when they tried to do without churches - turned into a mob of worthless red-feds. <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,50,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Grenfell `Old Tolly']

>  <b>SARGESON</b> <i>(1969,Joy of the Worm,134</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> <i>(1970,Not Here, Not Now,280</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR & HARREX</b> It took the 'Red Feds' some years to realize the irrelevance of such a doctrine [sc. workers taking over industry] in a country where the biggest factories were the railway workshops and where large-scale urban industries did not exist. <i>(1978,Looking Back,169</i>)

>  <b>FARRELL</b> Nancy-boy Gilchrest who was of the opinion that the country's salvation had lain with Campbell Begg not some ex-Red Fed. <i>(1992,Skinny Louie Book,58</i>)

### Red Federation
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  On the West Coast the miners Federation changed its name to New Zealand Federation of Labour (soon known as the red Federation, or simply the Red Fed) and sent out organisers to win support from other unions. <i>(1993,Wharfie,p28</i>)

>  <b>HICKEY</b> ... I issued a circular, stating that the Federation would stand behind the tramwaymen in any action that they might take. I had no authority whatever for issuing the circular, and in looking back on that time now, can only account for my impetuous action through my anger at the contempt displayed towards organised Labour by the City Council and the press. The leaflet (which happened to be printed on red paper, this giving the idea to the `Evening Post' [in early 1912] that was responsible for the coining of the term `Red Federation,' which was afterwards abbreviated to `Red Fed.') caused a wave of militancy to sweep through the tramway service, and a meeting was called for the p.m. shift the following day. <i>(1925,Red Fed. Memoirs,48</i>)

>  <b>MORRELL & HALL</b> A conference in 1910 with the Dominion Exective of the Trades and Labour Councils failed to achieve unity, and next year the Councils brought their own Federation into being. In the competition for support which followed, the militant Federation, which its opponents nicknamed the `Red Federation', had valuable assets in the eloquence of its leaders and the propaganda of its organ The Maoriland Worker. <i>(1957,History of NZ Life, A,207</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> In 1908 there was a local strike [on the West Coast] and shortly afterwards the miners formed a Federation of Labour to which, within a few years, were affiliated most of the miners and water side workers. The 1912 constitution of the `Red Federation' began with the preamble of the IWW ... <i>(1959,History of New Zealand,198</i>)

### Red Federationist
 
 <i>n.</i> (historical)

>  There is a diversity that doth hedge a "strike" is practically the doctrine of some of the Red Federationists. <i>(Sep 16, 1912,Evening Post,6</i>)

### Red Fedism
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Red Fedism hasn't a hope in rural New Zealand. <i>(Sep 1, 1920,NZ Free Lance,6</i>)

>  [Hdg] Red Fedism. Akin to Prussianism... Professor J. Macmillan Brown... contended that the ideals of the I.W.W. and the Red Fed element resembled, in many cases, the Prussianism against which we were fighting. <i>(Apr 18, 1917,Wanganui Herald,23</i>)

>  That Sir Joseph Ward appears to realise that the strike is going to have its effect upon the general election is evident from a statement which he has made to the Ashburton "Guardian." "Farmers in the North Island," he said, "were giving their whole-hearted support to the Liberals, and although the Prime Minister was endeavouring to make party capital out of 'Red-Fedism,' the farmers had not forgotten that the Government had helped to secure the return of active supporters of the Red-Fed. movement." <i>(Nov 23, 1914,Grey River Argus,5</i>)

>  <b>GLOVER</b> There was one defender of freedom in those dark days [1930] when many ... a student was being hauled across the coals for radicalism, Communism, red-fedism, and all sorts of other nonsense. <i>(1962,Hot Water Sailor,93,1981 ed.</i>)

### red gum
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Some of the timber trees are of large growth, particularly three sorts; the first is of the pine tribe and is known by the name of Pimise (Pinus??) in three varieties on the north. The second is something like the Lucombe Oack, and the third is called the Red Gum or Eucalipticus I have seen it in England. ... The three varieties of pine are very tall and handsome, and one in appearance like the Lucombe oak grows tall but not so straight, the Eucalipticy grows to a great thickeness in the stem and frequently not less than fifteen feet in girt but not more than 15 or 20 feet in height before it spreads its large branches in every direction, ... <i>(1940,Rakiura,356</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 356]

The name Matipo is generally applied by Europeans to <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, but this is apparently the plant to which the Maoris attached it. This tree bears some resemblance to <i>Pittosporum tenuifolium</i>, but can be readily distinguished by the reddish colour of the leaves. [<i>Rapanea Urvillei,</i> 1907] <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,332,1940 p. 353 ed.</i>)

### red perch
 
 <i>n.</i> Alternative name for the butterfly perch; brightly and variably coloured; common around northern NZ. [<i>Caesioperca lepidoptera</i> aka butterfly perch, red snapper, St. Peter's Fish, or bastard long-fin'] (See [butterfly perch](bri-byo.md#butterfly-perch))

>  [species name] alfonsino .. [scientific name] <i>Beryx splendens </i>.. [other name(s)] red perch (name used by Sealord (Nelson)) <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Red Perch, as is the case with many of our common fishes, has received many popular names. .../ It is also known as the `Red Snapper', `St. Peter's Fish' or `Butterfly erch'. It probably received the name of `St. Peter's Fish' on account of the two black marks, one on each side of the body, which suggest the thumb/and finger marks of St. Peter, as I have previously explained in the case of the John Dory. ... One of the popular names in Australia for [Red Perch] is `Butterfly Perch', so called from its specific name <i>Lepidoptera. </i> <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,73</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BUTTERFLY PERCH [<i>Caesioperca lepidoptera</i>] ... Other names are red perch when this colour predominates. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,38</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BUTTERFLY PERCH ...<i> Other names: Caesioperca lepidoptera</i>; red perch; oia (Maori). The name red perch is better reserved for another species, particularly as butterfly perch has been in common usage for many years. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,228</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Butterfly perch .. .. oia .. .. <i>Caesioperca lepidoptera </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Butterfly perch Caesioperca lepidoptera</i> New Zealand and southern Australia. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,84</i>)

Called `red snapper' or `St. Peter's fish' in Queen Charlotte and Pelorus Sounds, where it is occasionally taken in the seine net during July and August. ... Distribution North Iisland coasts and northern coasts of the South Island. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/115</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> RED PERCH (OIA) <i>Caesioperca lepidoptera</i>(Bloch and Schneider) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,233</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE RED PERCH <i>Caesioperca lepidoptera</i>(Bloch & Schneider)... <i>Maori name: OIA. </i> <i>Ibid.</i> 73 The Red Perch, as is the case with many of our common fishes, has received many popular names. It is known in Australia as the `Bastard Long-fin', a name probably associated with its long dorsal fin. It is also known as the `Red Snapper', `St. Peter's Fish' or `Butterfly Perch'. It probably received the name of `St. Peter's Fish' on account of the two black marks, one on each side of the body, which suggest the thumb/and finger marks of St. Peter, as I have previously explained in the case of the John Dory. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,72</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> [=BUTTERFLY PERCH] BUTTERFLY PERCH [<i>Caesioperca lepidoptera</i>] ... Other names are red perch when this colour predominates. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,38</i>)

>  [species name] butterfly perch .. [scientific name] <i>Caesioperca lepidoptera</i>.. [other name(s)] red perch <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### red pine
 
 <i>n.</i> (obsolete) [either totara or matai (black pine) 1844, (1898) Hocken Contributions, 1869 TrNZI, 1889 KFF (Andersen) (c) (rare) <i>P.totara</i>, =TOTARA]

>  <b>HAY</b> The Matai, or `Red Pine' (<i>Podocarpus spicata<M/i>), needs special mention. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/194</i>)

>  <i>red pine n 3 a</i> : RIMU<i> b</i> : a black pine (<i>Podocarpus spicata</i>) of New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1904</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> There is scarcely a more splendid sight to behold than a New Zealand forest. In the one we now entered, we did not perceive any kouri, or yellow pine-trees; but the totara [a's-acute], or red pine, grew in vast abundance. ... The rito, and innumerable other palm-trees, were in great quantity. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/391 Totara [a's-acute] (taxus australis) g. coniferae ... is known as red pine. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/105</i>)

>  <b>MONRO</b> In the woods [near Molyneux Bay, Otago] there are not many red and white pines, but a large proportion of rima. <i>(1844,Notes of a Journey</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner [=totara in contrast to `rima'] July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 249]

>  <b>Polack</b> .. the most superior [canoes] are to be found at Wairoa, (Hawkes' Bay) the tribes at that place employ the totarra or red pine, the kouri or white pine being rarely found beyond the Thames (Horake). <i>(1840,Mangamaire School,1/227</i>)

>  On drier ground [in Marlborough Sounds] he would find the Mai or Red Pine (<i>Podocarpus spicata</i>) ... festooned with parasites. <i>Ibid.</i> 12 In an economic point of view, the chief trees of the Middle Island, are the Red and White Pine, respectively called by the Maoris: the former Mai or Matai (<i>Podicarpus spicata</i>),[fn] the latter the Kahikatea (Podocarpus dacrydioides)<i>[ed. fn] Black Rue of Otago, Hook., f.; Miro, Black Rue or Black Pine in Otago, Hector; Miro, often confounded with Black Pine, Balfour; Matai, Colenso, - Mai or Matai (Taylor). </i> <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/ii/10</i>) [NOTES: (Monro)]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> rimu, used instead of rimu in very early days of the North Island settlements probably before European settlers became accustomed to the Maori word: ?esp. a bushmen's use; occas. used as a descriptive generic (from colour of wood) for other genera and species; (In New Zealand) - <i>Dacrydium cupressinum</i>, Soland; called also <i>Rimu</i>(q.v.). ... [<i>Rimu </i>has replaced <i>Red-pine</i> in the North Island and in Marlborough, Nelson and Westland; in Canterbury and Otago (and to some extent Westland) the timber has up to fairly recently always, and the tree chiefly, been <i>Red-pine,</i> although `<i>Rimu</i>' was by no means unknown, and by 1984 had generally replaced <i>Red-pine</i> except perhaps in the South South Island. ca1952 Dunedin `It's built out of red-pine; you know, timber from a rimu-tree'. ca1952 Hollyford Valley Red-pine (the tree), never <i>rimu </i>; (In Australia) - <i>Frenela endlicheri</i>, Parlat.]

>  Mr. M'Gruer prides himself a good deal on the specimen of the rimu or red pine (<i> Dacrydium cupressinum </i>) which he had transplanted from the bush, and which is a plant very difficult to start. <i>(May 27, 1871,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  The upward movement with regard to red pine began at Southland, due, it is said, to the increased difficulty of procuring that sort of timber. <i>(Jun 22, 1912,Poverty Bay Herald,3</i>)

>  The ceiling and structural timbers are of specially selected figured red pine and oiled, a dado of oiled oregon covers the bottom half of the walls, the top half of the brick wall being painted cream. <i>(Feb 28, 1928,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,3</i>)

>  26 Rimu (red pine) figured slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,16</i>) [NOTES: 1890 PWD Catalogue of Timbers : NZ and South Seas Exhibition Vol / Page 16]

>  <b>McDONALD</b> Q. Are there many harbours there [on the west side of New Zealand]? A. Several ... All covered with wood Q. Of what sort? A. Of white and red pine and a wood called `black oak'. Q. Is it of the species called `kaikatua'? A. It is. Q. Did you see any of the species called `cowrie'? A. I did not. <i>Ibid.</i> 1/560 Q. Whereabouts do the red and white pine trees grow? A. The white pine grows in wet swampy places. The red pine grows on the sides of hills. Q. Is the red pine like the cowrie? A. It is the same grain, but not the same color. <i>(May 1821,Evidence of McDonald before Commissioner Bigge</i>) [NOTES: 1821 May Evidence of McDonald before Commissioner Bigge in 1908 McNab Historical Records 1/559]

>  Walked about it [sc. Peel Forest], fine totaras, white and red pines: ... <i>(Mar 28, 1849,Torlesse Papers,70,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> [Rimu] which sawyers call red pine <i>(1841,NZ Journal,2/29/51</i>)

>  The red pine is splendid timber for furniture, it is like mahogany. <i>(Feb 10, 1842,Letters from New Plymouth,1843 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (1842 Feb 10) 1843 (1968) Letters from New Plymouth 4]

>  Red pine <i>(1847,McLean Papers,4/67</i>)

>  <i>Dacrydium cupresssinum</i> <i>(1848,NZ Journal</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  Went to Gurdon [Peel] Forest ... Walked about it, fine totaras, white and red pines. <i>(Mar 28, 1849,Torlesse Papers,70,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <i>(Jul 10, 1850,Papers & Proc. RS Van Diemen's Land</i>) [NOTES: (And)Collinson Timber Trees of NZ]

>  <b>TORLESSE</b> Kaikatea, (white pine), mahi, black pine, remu, (red pine) <i>(1851,Spring in the Canterbury Settlement, A,6</i>)

>  The timber in [the groves] is chiefly Kaikatea, (white pine), Mahi, (black pine), Remu, (red pine), Pokaka, Kowai, and Totara. ... That in the other forest [ie. not Harewood or Alford] is principally tawai, (black birch), the best timber for boat and ship building, but sometimes unsound: ... <i>(Jun 28, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

>  [Taranaki] Artistic James ... wants to preserve a magnificent red pine ... that a good burn would probably have killed. <i>(Feb 19, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/143</i>) [NOTES: (A.S. Atkinson)]

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> <i>Dacrydium cupresssinum</i> red-pine <i>(1906,Introductory Classbook of Botany</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>Dacrydium cupresssinum</i> red-pine <i>(1908,AJHR,C-14</i>) [NOTES: (And) Cockayne Report on a Botanical Survey of Waipoua Kauri Forest]

>  <i>Dacrydium cupresssinum</i> red-pine <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: 1909 Turner Botanical Examination of the Higher Waimarino District]

>  <i>Dacrydium cupresssinum</i> red-pine <i>(1924,NZ Journal of Science & Technology</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Rimu is the red pine, and for the miro I never heard an English name. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,13</i>)

>  The chief indigenous trees of commercial value are: Kauri (<i>Agathis Australis</i>), Kahikatea or white pine (<i>Podocarpus dacrydioides</i>), rimu or red pine (<i>Dacrydium cupresssinum</i> ), totara (<i>Podocarpus totara</i> and <i>Podocarpus Hallii</i>), Miro (<i>Podocarpus ferrugineus</i>), Kaikawaka or mountain cedar (<i>Libocedrus Bidwilli</i>), yellow pine or silver pine (<i>Dacrydium Colensoi </i>and <i>D. intermedium</i>), red and hard beech (<i>Nothofagus fasca</i> and<i> Nothofagus truncata</i>), silver beech (<i>Nothofagus Menziesii</i>), black beech (<i>Nothofagus Solandri</i>), black maire (<i>Olea Cunninghamii</i>), northern and southern rata (<i>Metrosideros robusta</i> and <i>Metrosideros lucida</i>). <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,578</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSEN</b> The rimu is a rakau rangatira, once called a red pine, a name now seldom used. <i>(1942,Maori Place Names,287</i>)

>  <i>red pine n 3 a</i> : RIMU <i>b</i> : a black pine (<i>Podocarpus spicata</i>) of New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1904</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The European name for rimu, especially in the South Island, is red pine. <i>Ibid..</i> 3/705-12 Pine, red .. .. rimu .. .. <i>Dacrydium cupresssinum</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/85</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>DACRYDIUM CUPRESSINUM, rimu, red pine. </i> <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,208</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Dacrydium cupressinum Lamb.</i> Common name: <i>Red pine</i> Maori name: <i>Rimu </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,31</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Dacrydium cupresssinum</i> RIMU ... Red pine <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,48</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The red pines are the <i>Totara, Towa, Mahi</i>, and <i>Rat</i>a, all of which are close-grained and moderately hard. ... They are all of a red colour, and when oiled and polished are very ornamental. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,42</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> These boxes are cut from red pine ... A stone adze was once shown to us whose carved handles had been destroyed in a succession of fires. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/229</i>)

>  Hope you like the tree. "Dacrydium cupressinum," blathers the tag, before continuing in plain English, "Rimu, Red pine. Excellent specimen tree with pendulous branchlets when young. Prefers rich moist soil. Bird attracting seed. Slow growing." <i>(Jun 25, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  The Committee recommends the disuse of the terms `red-pine', `white-pine', and `black-pine', in favour of rimu, kahikatea and matai, in order to prevent their being confused with timbers having similar names in Europe. <i>(Jul 17, 1896,AJHR,H-24</i>) [NOTES: Interim Report No. 3 (i) of Timber Conference]

>  <i>North Island Red-pine District. </i> <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,118</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>D[acrydium] cupressinum, Soland. ... Rimu; Red-pine</i>. ... The wood is deep-red, strong, hard, and heavy, but often twisted in grain. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,654</i>)

>  The timber on the western side consists of red- and white-pine, matai (or black-pine), totara, kawhaka (or cedar), rata, and occasional silver-pine, besides black- and red-birch (<i>Fagus fusca</i>). <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,19</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEW</b> <i>Dacrydium cupresssinum</i> red-pine <i>(1905,Tree Culture in NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  ... sawing up Rimu, that is Red Pine. <i>(Sep 1, 1857,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,45,1914 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1857 Sep 1 Harper Letters from Canterbury (1914]

>  In the decoration of the [Otago Horticultural Society] show-room, the graceful young Red Pine, and the Ti and Fern Trees, ranked first among our native trees. <i>(Jan 19, 1861,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  Here [at Taeri] was the tall and graceful black pine; the manukau, a shrub like the [Victorian] tea-tree scrub, only more greenly leaved; a few broad leaved grass-trees; the mappo, the kolu, the red pine, and others too numerous for me to remember; but their leaves were ... delightful to one accustomed to the dingy foliage of Victorian forests. <i>(Sep 27, 1861,NZ Goldfields,23,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1861 Sep 27 Tuapeka in NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 23]

>  <b>CARBERRY</b> A large Torch is maid [sic] from the Red Pine (Remu) which will last for about 3 hours. <i>(Feb 24, 1865,Journal</i>)

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>) [NOTES: (list of trees from Taylor 1848) (And)]

>  <b>HOCHSTETTER</b> <i>(1867,New Zealand (various authors/titles)</i>) [NOTES: (list of trees from Taylor 1848) (And)]

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Pine, red. <i>Dacrydium cupresssinum</i> . <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),767</i>)

>  Red Pine, or Rimu ... [In `<i>Botany of Otago</i>', European names precede Maori, as above; in `Botany of North Island' Maori names precede European] <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/31</i>) [NOTES: (Botany Otago)]

>  <b>PYKE</b> <i>(1868,Province of Otago in NZ, The</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>PRENTICE</b> Black, white and red pines, totara .... <i>(1870,Tale of NZ,33</i>) [NOTES: (Otago)]

>  the native name for this tree, is now tolerably well known in Otago. So if professional men and timber merchants would only encourage its use, it would soon supercede the vague conventional term of `red pine'. <i>(1876,TrNZI,163</i>) [NOTES: Blair Rimu]

>  [Native name] Rimu .. [Settlers' name] Red-pine .. [Family] Coniferae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>MUELLER</b> <i>(1888,Select Extra-tropical Plants</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  Rimu (red-pine) .. Dacrydium cupressinum .. A strong, handsome, reddish coloured timber, grain beautifully marked and figured; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,14</i>)

>  ... terrace flats [in Westland] ... covered with Red and Black Pine, Rata and Totara .... <i>(1892,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1892 Letter to Strauchan in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 175]

>  <b>McGill</b> Their wartime job was to drain .. the stunted manuka swamps Bill calls parkee (pakihis). it was once, he said, a great area for red pine, rimu, and the black pine, matai. <i>(1985,G'day Country,156</i>)

>  FOR SALE - Polished Red Pine Table, 5ft 6in. x 2ft 10in. <i>(Aug 2, 1932,Otautau Standard & Wallace County Chronicle,2</i>)

>  Red Pine. Dacrydium cupressinum, Sol. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/iii/49</i>)

>  <i>rimu</i>, a fine tree (Dacrydium cupressinum), red-pine; the young tree, a fountain of weeping foliage considered one of the most beautiful trees in the world. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/146</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... the most superior [canoes] are to be found at Wairoa, (Hawkes' Bay) the tribes at that place employ the <i>totarra</i> or red pine, the <i>kouri</i> or white pine being rarely found warm and cheery ... <i>(1840,Mangamaire School,1/227</i>)

>  Mr M'Gruer prides himself a good deal on the specimen of the rimu or red pine ( <i> Dacrydium cupressinum </i> ) which he had transplanted from the bush and which is a plant very difficult to start. <i>(May 27, 1871,Otago Witness,5</i>)

>  <b>TUCKETT</b> [?rimu] I observed a fine stream of water, and the wood with abundance of timber - the red pine, or, as it is here [Otago, Tuckett had come from ?Nelson] called, the black ... throughout the district. <i>(Apr 19, 1844,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Apr 19 Tuckett Diary in 1898 Hocken Contributions 208]

### red rock cod
 
 <i>n.</i> a scorpionfish <i>Scorpaena cardinalis</i>.

>  SCORPAENA CARDINALIS Richardson. <i>Red Rock-cod; Matuawhapuku</i>. Very common throughout the year in rocky localities around North Auckland Peninsula, but not greatly utilized as a food fish. Distribution: A northern species; occasionally seen on the northern and western coasts of the South Island. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/121</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Scorpaena cruenta</i> Richardson. RED ROCK-COD; MATUAWHAPUKU. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,53,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Scorpaena cruenta</i> Richardson. Red rock cod. (Australia) <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/417</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Matuawhapuku,</i> n. <i>Scorpaena cruenta, red rock cod. = rarai. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,195</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE RED ROCK COD <i>Ruboralga cardinalis (Richardson)</i> ... The Red Rock Cod is a fairly common fish in the North Island but becomes less plentiful in southern waters. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,163</i>)

>  The scorpion fish, variously known as red rock cod, grandfather hapuku, Jock McKenzie or scarpee is not good to eat and its spines are capable of inflicting painful cuts. <i>(Aug 20, 1965,Evening Post,29</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>SCORPION FISH Scorpaena cardinalis</i> (Grandfather hapuku, red rock cod, pahuiakaroa) ... [196] They are basically red in colour ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,195,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> There is another very similar [to red scorpionfish] but more variably mottled species, <i>S[corpaena] cardinalis</i>, the red rock cod, in northern New Zealand; it can reach 40 cm, and is often called grandfather hapuku. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,79</i>)

### red rock lobster
 
 <i>n.</i> Jasus edwardsii

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i> Red rock lobster Jasus edwardsii </i>... Closely related to <i> J. novaehollandiae </i> of southern Australia and <i> J. lalandii </i> of South Africa ... `Crayfish' has been regularly used as a common name, although it is more properly applied to freshwater species. Some combination of `red', `spiny', and `rock lobster' is more appropriate. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,150</i>)

### red rover
 
 <i>n.</i> a children's game

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Others wanted [to play] `Prisoners' Base'` or `Chivvy', `Brewer', `Egg-in-the hat', `Duck-stone', `Red Rover', or `Kingy-seeny'. The boys did not know where this east game came from. They did not know its proper spelling or pronunciation. `Kingy-Seeny' it was to them and they did not care whether that had a meaning or not. This rough and tumble game was the most popular of all. <i>(1905,Bush Boys of NZ,84</i>)

### Red Shed
 
 <i>n.</i> a Warehouse retail building; the Warehouse company as a whole

>  Red Shed raises advertising stakes <i>(Aug 3, 2005,NZ Herald,A3</i>) [NOTES: headline]

>  [heading] <b>DDB wins 'red sheds'</b> <i>(Jul 7, 2005,NZ Herald,C4</i>)

>  "I was disappointed with the New Zealand red sheds, but in terms of Australia they were spot on."... The red sheds, the New Zealand retail stores, posted a 3.2 per cent increase in overall sales... <i>(Feb 6, 2003,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  Cast like some damp, ugly devil into a vast sea of car parking, it was no place for the living - and certainly few enough had gathered inside the centre's enormous red shed, the Warehouse, for the imminent arrival of rugby league legend Stacey Jones. <i>(Jul 6, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

>  The last building proposed for the site is the most contentious. Three words: Big Red Shed. <i>(Apr 24, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

>  The company said yesterday that it had taken the first steps towards obtaining a liquor licence for a Tauranga "red shed". <i>(Nov 4, 2005,NZ Herald,C1</i>)

>  This [increase in sales] would have helped it boost margins, particularly from its "red shed" stores, an analyst said yesterday. <i>(Sep 5, 2002,Dominion Post,C2</i>)

>  The Warehouse has three core businesses - The Warehouse New Zealand or "red shed" stores, The Warehouse Stationery referred to as the "blue sheds", and The Warehouse Australia called the "yellow sheds". <i>(Sep 7, 2002,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  Sales at The Warehouse's Te Rapa "laboratory" store were the strongest of all 85 Red Sheds since its July opening, Mr Morrice told November's annual meeting. <i>(Mar 11, 2006,Dominion Post,C4</i>)

>  Sales from the Warehouse's "red shed" stores in New Zealand rose a modest 3.9 per cent... Sales in the red sheds were "artificially depressed" because it had "massive sales in the same quarter last year to get rid of excess stock... Mr Muir expected red shed sales to bounce back in the next quarter. <i>(Aug 8, 2002,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

### red snapper
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [species name] red snapper .. [scientific name] <i>Centroberyx affinis </i> .. [other name(s)] golden snapper, nannygai, redfish <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  There's also a red snapper, sometimes known as golden snapper, sometimes available in Auckland. Despite the name, it is quite unrelated to the true snapper and is, in fact, more similar to the roughys. The Australian snapper is the same as ours. <i>(Aug 1986,Metro,148</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] snapper, red 1020-1, 1307 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> RED SNAPPER ... (<i>Scorpis Hectori </i>). This is described as a new species by Captain Hutton, and is not unfrequently caught in the sands on the west coast of Otago, ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,106</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> RED SCHNAPPER (<i>Anthias Richardsoni </i>). ... The red schnapper belongs to the same family as the hapuku and kahawai, the <i>Percidae. .</i>.. [84] It is generally called the red schnapper by seamen who are acquainted with the fish of the coast, the colour being a uniform bright red, ... <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,83</i>)

>  The following is a list of the fishes which are chiefly met with in the market: hapuku, kahawai, red-snapper, snapper, tarakihi, moki, trumpeter, frostfish ... gurnard, mullet, sea-mullet, ... flounder or patiki, ... grayling, smelt, kokopu, sardine, sprat, eel (tuna), black-eel, conger-eel [168], and silver-eel. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  CAESIOPERCA LEPIDOPTERA (Forster). <i>Red Perch.</i> Called `red snapper' or `St. Peter's fish' in Queen Charlotte and Pelorus Sounds, where it is occasionally taken in the seine net during July and August. ... Distribution* North island coasts and northern coasts of the South Island. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/115</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Red Perch, as is the case with many of our common fishes, has received many popular names. .../ It is also known as the `Red Snapper', `St. Peter's Fish' or `Butterfly Perch' It probably received the name of `St. Peter's Fish' on account of the two black marks, one on each side of the body, which suggest the thumb/and finger marks of St. Peter, as I have previously explained in the case of the John Dory. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,73</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> RED SNAPPER ... Other names: Trachichthodes affinis; <i>golden snapper; nannygai (Australia); koarea (Maori). </i> <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,211</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> <i>RED SNAPPER [Trachichthodes affinis </i>] ... Also known as golden snapper, and koarea (Maori). <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,36</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> GOLDEN SNAPPER Not a snapper at all but a relative of the Roughy. This big-eyed, Golden (or Red) Snapper is usually taken accidentally when bottom fishing for snapper. <i>Ibid.</i> 227 Red snapper or sometimes reef snapper, is a name given to those fish which have spent a good part of their time in rough-bottom areas; they are a deeper, golden red than the normal open-water snapper. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,114</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>GOLDEN SNAPPER Centroberyx affinis (Trachichthodes affinis) </i>(Nannygai, red snapper, koarea) ... In spite of its name the golden snapper is not related to the true snapper and is in fact more similar to the roughys. ... Schools of golden snapper often move out over open sand flats ... and are then regularly caught in bottom trawls and sold as red fish. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,177,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Red snapper Trachichthodes affinis </i> Alternatively the golden snapper, and redfish or nannygai in southern Australia. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,72</i>)
